# October 2026 — the record

Shipped-slice postmortems: what was decided, what was measured, which of the slice's own predictions were wrong. **New entries are appended directly at the end.** The dated `**Last updated:**` blocks further down are historical provenance — they were rolled out of `docs/project/next-steps.md` verbatim by `tools/docs/archive_next_steps.py`, which is **retired for that path**: `CLAUDE.md` now forbids the handoff from ever carrying a dated block again, so the tool finds nothing and nothing new arrives this way. Shipped backlog entries moved out of `docs/project/deferred-work-backlog.md` also land here.

**Last updated:** 2026-10-26 (**The third human verification pass ran: 7 items, 6 pass and 1 deliberate fail, no engine bug found — and the screenshot caught something nobody asked about.** Seven items went out over the U8 arc (U8a-U8d, PRs #310-#313): five eye-only checks and two decisions. **All five visual items passed**, and the attached screenshot confirms each prediction independently: the layout-only container's three bars with bar width equal to the gap and nothing drawn behind them (U8b's sharp failure, the one with no other symptom, did not occur); the grid's six cells with zero magenta; the flow's 3+2 cells with the magenta square filling the empty third slot; the HUD bar and its inherited-alpha label; and U8a's three stacked panels, pastel because they inherit a 0.55 container, exactly tiling it. **The two decisions, which are the durable half.** (1) **Build `field_bool`** — PASS. 16 reflected bool fields exist, 11 predating U8b, and `visible` is the first whose entire purpose is to be switched while you watch; the Inspector renders every one as a read-only label because `FieldVisitor` has no `field_bool`. (2) **Flip `UiFlow`'s default axis to horizontal** — FAIL on "keep it consistent with `UiStack`", i.e. change it. The supervisor had ruled the other way during review, for cross-container consistency; the human overruled it. **No reasoning was captured for either decision** (both notes empty), so what is recorded here is the verdict and the case as it was put on the page, not the human's own argument — if the consistency question is reopened, it reopens without their reasoning. **THE FINDING NOBODY ASKED FOR, and it came from the screenshot rather than from any item.** U8c's flow block is the first bottom-centre HUD content the demo has ever had, and it **covers the lower half of the lime "GLOW" world label**. The mechanism is not a bug: the `WorldLabel` pass runs at `render_system.hpp:445` and the `UiPanel` pass at `:503`, so panels draw over world labels unconditionally, and the flow block's top edge is `y = 0.79` which is exactly where the text is cut. It matters because the 2026-10-21 pass's recorded decision on that label — keep the colour, "we can look at adjusting it later if it becomes a problem in the future" — named **"a different position"** as one of its three remedies, and that trigger has now fired on occlusion rather than on contrast. **No verdict was invalidated by any of this**, and that was checked rather than assumed: neither decision changes an appearance (the demo authors `axis = 1` explicitly, so flipping the default moves nothing on screen, and `field_bool` adds a widget), and the occlusion is pre-existing to the pass itself — the screenshot that revealed it is the one the human judged item 1 against. A fix that moves the flow block would change where item 3 appears but not the property it asserts (equal cell widths, magenta in the empty slot), so that verdict would carry.)

**Previously, last updated 2026-10-21:** (**The second human verification pass ran: 5 items answered, 3 pass and 2 fail, and both failures were ONE root cause, a latent engine bug that five passes of a 1456-test suite never could have found.** The orbiter's world label, authored "Orbiter", rendered as "ter". `VulkanRenderDevice::draw_world_text` staged vertices in ONE per-frame buffer at offset 0 (a CPU memcpy) while the draw only executed at submit, and `RenderSystem` called it once per `WorldLabel`; with two labels the second memcpy overwrote the first, so "Orbiter" drew 4 glyphs of "GLOW" and its own last 3, "ter". Latent, not new: the demo had one label until #274 added a second. **The fix** moves batching into the engine (one `draw_world_text` per atlas `TextureHandle`, groups in first-seen order), where `FakeRenderDevice` can see it, and makes the backend structurally safe anyway (one buffer per call per frame, so a second call, e.g. a second font, cannot alias). The fake now models the backend's shared buffer, so the test asserts what each draw READS at submit, not what was passed: before the fix `REQUIRE( orbiter_verts == 7 * 6 )` failed with `18 == 42`, after it passes; suite 1456 -> 1459, all passing. **What the gate still cannot see:** the backend change (chunk list, begin_frame reset) has no test, because nothing creates a `VkDevice`; it is verified by reading and by a windowed run being clean, and the pixels are a human's to re-check. **The other results held:** the magenta ribbon and the lime label both glow as predicted (the arithmetic held: ribbon `(0.93, 0.16, 0.91)`, label `(0.80, 0.95, 0.37)`). **Human decision on the lime "GLOW" label:** "The word GLOW is legible, though the contrast is pretty low. I count this passing for now. We can look at adjusting it later if it becomes a problem in the future." Kept as authored; recorded beside the colour in `app/run/demo_scene.hpp` and in the backlog (Rendering), including that `WorldLabel` has no outline or shadow field. **Re-check DONE, at the window, after #275 landed:** the orbiter label reads "Orbiter" again, and GLOW still reads well. That closes the two failed items, and it matters for the DECISION above rather than only for the bug. GLOW was drawn TWICE before the fix (its own draw, plus the first four glyphs of the Orbiter draw), so it was double-blended and brighter than authored -- and the "legible, though the contrast is pretty low" verdict was given on that brighter version. It now draws once and is dimmer, and was re-confirmed on its TRUE appearance. So the decision to keep `{1.0, 2.85, 0.25}` stands on what the label actually looks like, and is not owed another look on those grounds. **Final tally: 5 of 5 items answered, all now passing, one latent engine bug found and fixed.**)

**Previously, last updated 2026-10-21:** (**The HUD-underlay case's cause is found and removed, and two small demo items rode along (PR #274).** `the default scene's HUD underlay image draws beneath the checker` was the only case in the suite that renders a frame, so it alone paid `setup_runtime`'s lazy full-size IBL bake (plus the BRDF LUT) on the first frame; the claim it pins, UI image draw order, reads no environment. Fix: one line, `set_environment_asset({})` through the existing public setter, no production change. **Local (20 cores, debug, `ctest -j20`):** 14.2 s -> 0.17 s (the reviewer measured 14.69 s on the committed binary); the suite's new longest case is `PluginFileWatcher.poll detects a modified file's mtime` at 1.11 s; whole-suite wall 15.1 s -> 2.6 s. No other case pays the bake. **Probes (the oracle):** deleting the image pass's `stable_sort`, flipping its comparator, and moving the underlay to `order = 1` each fail the case both before and after the fix, so it asserts exactly what it did. **Prediction for the CI parallel step (4 vCPU, nproc 4):** the other ~37 s of serial work over four workers, so about 10 s on a fast host and 17-20 s on a slow one, central estimate ~14 s, against 31.77 s measured post-#273; **Achieved (PR #274's own run 39614010471, CI, nproc 4, gcc 13.3.0, image `20261018.339.1`):** `Total Test time (real) = 9.81 s` and an 11 s step, the HUD case 0.23 s, longest case `PluginFileWatcher.poll...` 1.11 s, `check_ctest_run: OK, 1456 of 1456 tests ran and passed`. Predicted ~14 s (10-20 s), achieved 9.8 s: at the fast end of the range, one run on one host, and the 31.77 s post-#273 figure was on a different image (`20261012.334.1`) so the two are not a like-for-like pair; the like-for-like fast-host figure on `20261018.339.1` was ~19.4-20 s. **Task 2:** a magenta ribbon comet (`{2.4, 0.12, 1.9}`, predicted `(0.93, 0.16, 0.91)`) and a lime world label (`{1.0, 2.85, 0.25}`, predicted `(0.80, 0.95, 0.37)`), both unseen; and a general test that no drawing prop sits below the ground's top face. The suite is **1456** tests (one added). The backlog's three rows are struck with what each got wrong. The next human verification pass should judge the ribbon and the label.)

**Previously, last updated 2026-10-21:** (**CI test step now runs in parallel (PR #273, merged as `94f5259`).** `ctest -j"$(nproc)"` in the unit-test step, guarded permanently by `tools/ci/check_ctest_run.py`: the JUnit file must hold exactly N passing testcases, N taken from `ctest -N` in the same job, failing hard when N is empty or zero (negative controls on CI went red, the filtered one with `JUnit has 6 testcases but 1455 tests were discovered`). **The backlog's gate ("two flaky tests") was stale:** the raycast case was fixed in #230, and the threaded-`ScriptSystem` SIGSEGV cannot happen under ctest because `catch_discover_tests` runs each of the 1455 cases in its own process. The real findings were one slow case and a port range: the six ENet test ports moved from 34001-34006 (inside the ephemeral range, where a hard-coded port has already failed a real CI run) to 24101-24106, and the `TIMEOUT 60` comment, which claimed 54x headroom, now states the real figure (about 2.6x on CI). **Achieved against predicted** (CI, nproc 4, gcc 13.3.0, images 20261012.334.1 and 20261018.339.1): predicted ~26 s step, achieved 19.4-32.9 s, median 30.2 s, **bimodal by host not by image** (fast host ~20 s, slow host ~25-33 s); serial was 43 s (fast) to 61 s (slow). The slow-host gap is a long case that runs 1.17x slower under contention (+3.9 s) and the first 412 cases dispatching in 4.9 s, not 2.7 s (+2.3 s). **The next lever is one test, not the workflow:** `the default scene's HUD underlay image draws beneath the checker` (`tests/unit/app/demo_scene_test.cpp:116`) takes 16-27 s of a ~20-32 s parallel step; from about +5 s the step's wall is that single case alone. Removing it would take the step to roughly 10-20 s, a larger remaining win than parallelism itself. Its cause is **unknown** (ptrace is blocked in the sandbox); no profile has been taken. Eleven clean CI runs can refute, not prove, stability (about 27% bound at 95%). Spec and results: `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-21-ci-parallel-ctest-design.md`. **Post-merge (run 39605518805, green):** `check_ctest_run: OK, 1455 of 1455 tests ran and passed`; test step 31.77 s on image `20261012.334.1` against 61 s serial on that image; the ccache was saved under the new SHA. The "cause is unknown" above is superseded: see the entry that follows.)

**Previously, last updated 2026-10-20:** (**The first human verification pass ran, and it found a bug the 1454-test suite could not see and had never been able to see.** Six items went out covering the bloom arc (#258), the UI layout/alpha arc (#260), the two render-sync fixes (#261) and the editor press fix (#263). **Five passed. One failed: the demo's ground decal was invisible — and had been since the day it was added, on 2026-09-07 in `58496d0`.** The ground is a unit cube (±0.5) scaled to `{10,1,10}` at the origin, so its top face is at **y = 0.5**; the decal was authored at **y = 0.02**, i.e. 0.48 units *inside* the ground. The decal pipeline depth-tests with `VK_COMPARE_OP_LESS`, so the ground rejected it every frame. **No test looked at those two numbers together**, and the demo's own comment described a colour ("Shows as (0.95, 0.80, 0.44) now") that **nobody had ever seen** — the second time in this arc a document of ours asserted something false about pixels. Fixed to `y = 0.52`, with a test that derives the ground's top face from the scene's only static `RigidBody` rather than hardcoding an entity index, so it survives a scene reorder. Suite 1455/1455.

**Two decisions came back, and both are now recorded beside the code they govern** rather than only in a chat log. **The glowing sky: KEEP.** The HDR sky (`kloofendal_2k.hdr`, mean radiance 0.72, 5.3% of samples above 1.0) does glow against the authored 1.0 threshold, and the pass says that reads as a bright sky rather than a veil — so do *not* raise the demo's threshold; that was considered and declined, and the note sits in `demo_scene_test.cpp` beside the threshold assertion. **The translucent "HP" label: KEEP.** It now renders at the bar's 0.6 through inherited alpha instead of its own authored 1.0; fading a container should fade what is inside it. An opt-out flag was considered and declined, and that is written into `demo_scene.hpp` beside the label.

**What the pass confirms about the gate, and what it does not.** The three items with no automated backing at all — effects glow, glow stays locked across a Viewport resize, stacked panels tile exactly — were the right three to spend a human on: one of them was the failure. But note what *found* it. The reader could not tell whether the decal glowed, and the honest "I am unsure" is what sent me to the code; the screenshot then showed no warm stripe anywhere on the ground. **An uncertain human answer is a finding, not a non-answer.** The page's own predicted pass-condition for that item was also partly wrong — it reasoned about the decal's authored colour against the bloom threshold and never asked whether the decal was on screen at all.

**The CI caching slice has shipped: Slice A (shallow `FetchContent` clones, #268) and Slice B (ccache, restore on every run, save only from `main`, #271) are both on `main`, and the post-merge checks are done (follow-up PR #272).** The design is `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-20-ci-build-caching-design.md`, the plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-10-20-ci-build-caching.md`. **Three claims in the entry this replaces were wrong, found by measuring.** (1) *"Cache `build/_deps`"*: third-party objects are **198 of 452 objects but 80 s of ~1,009 s of compile CPU (8%)**; Mosaic's own **254** objects are **92%** (the old "256" was a different tree's count). And restoring a build *tree* leaves Ninja to judge it by mtimes against a fresh checkout, which is exactly the fast-and-subtly-wrong shape; it is rejected, not deferred. (2) *"That file's hash is a clean cache key"*: it is not. ccache's own content hash carries correctness, and keying on `CMakeLists.txt` would reset the bucket on every dependency bump. (3) *"Cache the apt layer"*: it is 20-30 s against a stale-package-list risk with a third-party apt repo; left alone. **Where a run's time goes** (five runs, `gh run view --json jobs`): Build 358-477 s of 486-643 s; Configure 66-78 s (this is where the clones are); tests 37-65 s. **What Slice A measured:** `_deps` 998 MB -> 291 MB; fresh configure 43.0 s -> 19.7 s locally (3 alternating runs each); on CI, same image, Configure median **62 s -> 33 s**. The floor was a median, not worst-against-best (the shallow worst, 38 s, beat the baseline best, 56 s, by only 18 s, and that is accepted). **What the ccache measurements say Slice B can and cannot do** (local, 20 cores, so shape not magnitude): fully warm 9 s vs cold 285 s, but a change to `foundation/core/entity_id.hpp` (included by 171 of 254 TUs) still takes 233 s; the median recent commit touches 5 TUs. The win is bimodal by which file changed. A build assembled from a 100%-hit cache was byte-identical (471 files) to an uncached one. **What Slice B measured on CI** (full table and probes in the spec's Results): a cold job is ~9m (mean 547 s); with a restored cache a one-file change is **2m05s median (1m44s-2m23s)**, a 41-TU header ~3m56s, the widest header (171 TUs) ~7m04s. ccache misses equalled the include fan-out exactly in every scenario, and cached builds matched uncached ones byte-for-byte (sha256 of 471 files) including with 171 objects invalidated. Restore costs 2-5 s, save 2 s, cache 266 MB. **What changed in the design because of measuring:** `ImageVersion` was removed from the cache key (a runner-image rollout made three consecutive runs silently cold). A warm job is now ~80% apt + configure + serial `ctest` (~40 s), so the next lever is `ctest -j` (backlog), not more caching. **Verified after merge:** the seeding push was cold and saved a 253 MB cache; the **first PR restored it, 452 / 452 hits, job 2m10s (130 s) against 544 s cold**; re-running the seeding push stays green but leaves a yellow `Cache save failed.` annotation (expected, commented in the workflow); a wide-header change restoring `main`'s cache gives 281 hits / 171 misses and fills the 1G store to 50%. The widest-header scenario's median is **7m50s over 8 runs** (the 7m04s from the first five was optimistic). **Still unmeasured:** successive `main` saves driving the store toward its 1G cap, and the hit rate over weeks; check `gh api repos/Evol-Luci/mosaic/actions/cache/usage` and a representative PR's hit rate occasionally. **Next lever:** `ctest -j` (backlog), gated on two flaky tests. **Do not forget the standing rules recorded in the spec:** first-party `actions/*` only; only `main` saves a cache; a red CI run must be classified (three known flaky tests/links, the `noble`-pinned LunarG step, or a real finding) before it counts as a data point.

**Last updated:** 2026-10-17 (**U8a is in: `UiStack` lays children out, and a container's alpha fades everything inside it. Two of the U8 decomposition's claims were wrong, and both were found by reading the code it predicted against.** The decomposition said layout would be "a pre-pass that overrides its children's `pos`". It cannot be: `resolve_ui_rect`/`resolve_parent_frame` are **lazy and pure**, nothing stores a rect, and `pos` is reflected — so writing computed values back would **serialise over authored data** the first time the scene was saved, and would reintroduce the draw/hit-test disagreement class this UI has already paid for twice (the `to_anchor` triplication, the `UiButton`/`UiPanel` `order` lint). **Layout narrows the FRAME instead**, inside the one function every consumer funnels through. And the hook belongs in **`resolve_parent_frame`, not `resolve_ui_rect`** — there are *two* entry points into the hierarchy, and the HUD **text** pass calls the former directly, so hooking the wrapper would have laid out panels and images and silently left every label behind, with no existing test failing. **That second finding also settled the decision the decomposition explicitly reserved for the human** ("children's authored `pos` becomes ignored under a layout parent... worth deciding how that is communicated"): narrowing the frame means `resolve_in` already treats `pos` as an offset within it, so `pos` is **not** ignored — it offsets the child from its slot, `{0,0}` lands exactly on the slot, and the alternative would require *adding* a special case to suppress arithmetic that already works. **The sharpest correctness point** was sibling order: `ComponentRegistry::for_each` visits "in dense order" over a sparse set, and removal swaps the last element into the hole, so deriving layout order from iteration would let destroying **any** unrelated entity carrying `UiParent` reshuffle a stack — a flicker for draw order, but a *relocation* for layout. Order comes from the authored `order` (the one the renderer already sorts by), with the entity index as a deterministic tiebreak only. **One finding against my own test:** the case proving that stability originally created its "stranger" entity LAST, so swap-and-pop merely popped and moved nothing — it passed with the sort deleted, making its own comment false. Fixed by inserting the stranger in the middle of the stack's children and re-probing. **Gate:** 1445/1445, `run --window` clean, and `mosaic editor` under `MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1` shows **10 hazards — unchanged from the pre-existing baseline**, as expected for a slice that adds no render pass. **[Noted 2026-10-25, during U8c: do NOT read that number as a passing baseline.** The gate as [defined precisely on 2026-10-24](#two-gates-defined-precisely-moved-out-of-the-handoff-2026-10-24) requires **zero** VUIDs, and two independent captures on 2026-10-25 — at `1c87b9b` and on the U8b branch — each showed exactly three `[INFO]` lines and **0** VUID/ERROR/WARN. So either something between 2026-10-17 and 2026-10-24 fixed them or this count measured something else; which one is unresolved, and nobody has spent time on it because the current gate is absolute rather than a delta. The hazard this note exists to prevent is a future reader treating a 10-hazard run as unchanged-and-therefore-fine.]** **What only a human can check — and one of these is a deliberate change to EXISTING content, so read it before filing a regression.** (a) **The "HP" label on the blue HUD bar is now translucent.** It is a child of a `UiPanel` authored at `alpha = 0.6`, so inherited alpha renders it at 0.6 rather than at its own authored 1.0. That is the intended semantics — fading a container fades what is inside it — but it changes shipped, previously-verified content, and the whole-branch review caught that the demo's own comment claimed the stack was kept off the camera to protect exactly this label, while the alpha half changed it anyway through a different door. (b) The three stacked panels top-right (red, green, blue, top to bottom) must **exactly tile** their container at two window sizes — `spacing` and `padding` are authored at 0 and each child's `size` is `{1,1}`, so a seam means the division leaves a gap, an overlap means it double-counts spacing, and a wrong colour order means `stack_children` is not sorting by the authored `order`. **Deferred, each with a reason in the backlog:** `UiGrid`/`UiFlow` (same machinery, different slot arithmetic — better judged against a stack that exists), content-sized slots (would make layout depend on asset loading, since `UiText` has no height until its atlas resolves), a real `visible` bool (**not** the same feature as `alpha = 0`, because alpha 0 still hit-tests), and a slot gizmo. Spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-17-ui-layout-containers-design.md`, plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-10-17-ui-layout-containers.md`.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-17 (**Bloom is in, and the RenderGraph prediction it was supposed to vindicate turned out to be wrong.** The payoff for #255/#256: a `Bloom` component on the camera entity (`intensity` 0, `threshold` 1.0, `knee` 0.5 — defaults that render byte-identically to before), one device hook `build_bloom(BloomParams)` between `end_hdr_scene_pass` and `draw_hdr_resolve`, and in the Vulkan backend one half-resolution `R16G16B16A16_SFLOAT` image with N mips (N clamped to 6), one render pass, three pipelines and `N+1` descriptor sets driven by a single loop. **The backlog twice predicted bloom would trigger the RenderGraph** on the grounds that a mip chain's pass count varies with resolution. It does not: the chain is a *linear* chain recomputed on resize — the same lifecycle the HDR target already has — so in code it is `for (i = 0; i < levels_; ++i)`. The graph's trigger is restated in the Rendering section of the backlog as passes whose *existence or order* depends on per-frame scene content, or a post chain whose order is data rather than code. **Two decisions the spec left open.** `bloom_level_count` lives in its own module file (`modules/render_vulkan/vulkan_bloom_levels.{hpp,cpp}`, the `vulkan_swapchain_support` precedent) because nothing in the suite creates a `VkDevice` — without that, the "N can be 1" trap has no possible test. And the downsample and upsample **share one shader**: the taps are offset by a *source* texel, which is exactly what makes one a box downsample and the other a tent upsample, so they differ only in blend state. **Both real findings came from the one step that runs real Vulkan, not from 1404 tests.** `create_bloom_chain` clears the chain through `begin_single_time_commands`, so placed beside `create_hdr_target` — the obvious template for a swapchain-sized resource, and above the command pool — it **segfaulted**; the constructor already carried that exact lesson for the IBL stand-ins. And the plan's validation step said `mosaic edit`, which is not a subcommand: it exits 106 with zero VUIDs, which *looks* like a pass, and would have reported the editor path verified without ever running it. Both are now pinned by contract tests. **Gate:** 1404/1404, and `mosaic run --window` and `mosaic editor` each run the full 20s with zero validation errors, with the chain's eleven passes actually executing. **What only a human can check — three items for the next verification pass.** (1) *The fountain, trail and puffer glow; the ground does not.* Their brightest channels are authored at 2.85, 1.75 and 2.85, all above the demo's threshold of 1.0; the ground is lit but never authored above 1, so it must stay crisp. A glow on the ground means the threshold is not arriving as 1.0. **Run `mosaic run --window`, or press Play first — the editor opens stopped, so no ticks means no particles.** (2) *The HUD bar is unaffected*, because it draws after `draw_hdr_resolve` onto the resolved image; a soft HUD means the UI draws moved inside the HDR pass. (3) *Drag the editor's Viewport panel edge while the demo runs* — the glow must stay locked to the objects making it, at the same apparent size relative to them. The chain is sized from the **swapchain**, not the panel, and this is the class of bug #256 shipped; `ctest` plus a validation-layer grep is structurally blind to anything differing between `vp_extent_` and `extent_`. **The named risk, decided in advance:** the demo lights itself with a real outdoor HDR sky whose radiance genuinely exceeds 1.0, so the sky and horizon *will* glow. That is physically correct and may still veil the image — if it does, the fix is the demo's authored threshold in `app/run/demo_scene.hpp`, **not the pipeline**. Spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-17-render-bloom-design.md`, plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-10-17-render-bloom.md`.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-17 (**The HDR slice's human verification pass ran: 6 of 7 genuinely pass, and the one real failure was the one the spec predicted.** Results and screenshots came back through the test page's own datastore. **T1 was recorded as a fail and is not one — the test was wrong, twice.** It asked whether the editor viewport shows the whole scene after the crop fix, and set the pass condition as “the scene scales, the same framing, just smaller or larger”. That demanded behaviour the engine should never have: `editor.cpp` calls `set_aspect(vp.aspect())`, so a narrower panel gives a narrower HORIZONTAL field of view with the vertical one fixed, and content correctly leaves the frame at the sides. The same item also told the reader to look for particles in the editor, **which opens stopped** — no ticks, so no particles are emitted until Play. The fix is confirmed working by measurement instead: the HUD bar is authored at normalised width `0.25` and ends cleanly at ~24% of the panel in the narrow-panel screenshot, where the crop bug would have started it at 16% and run it off the right edge clipped. **T3 is a real fail, and the measurements are the useful part.** ACES desaturates: the puffer's cyan dropped from 0.70 saturation to 0.46, and the decal's warm tint from 0.40 to 0.16 — nearly grey, and genuinely unfindable against the white ground in the pass's own screenshots. The fountain's orange was not merely duller: the curve RAISES green (0.5 → 0.62) while lowering red (1.0 → 0.80), so it read yellow. **Retuned by authoring above 1.0** — `{1.0, 0.5, 0.1}` became `{2.85, 0.30, 0.05}`, which maps to `(0.95, 0.44, 0.04)` — the first use of the headroom this slice bought. **What was deliberately not done:** the reader's own words were “staying the same colour and just fading out”, and that is NOT the desaturation and NOT a regression. `color_at` lerps every frame; the end colour simply arrives on a particle that has shrunk 5x (`end_size` 0.03 against `size` 0.15) and gone transparent on an `EaseOut` alpha. That is the effect's motion design rather than its palette, the pass only authorised the palette, and it is now a backlog item with the numbers attached. **The lesson worth keeping** is about the pass, not the code: a verification item whose pass condition is wrong converts a working feature into a reported bug, and costs the reader's attention twice — once to run it and once to argue with it. Both of T1's faults were avoidable by tracing the instruction to real code, which is what the skill already requires and what I did not do for that one item. **Next:** bloom, and with it the RenderGraph question.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-16 (**The HDR resolve is in: the scene is tone mapped once, as one image, and bloom is unblocked.** The Vulkan half of the slice whose engine-side boundary landed on 2026-10-13. The scene now renders into a swapchain-sized `R16G16B16A16_SFLOAT` target and `hdr_resolve.frag` applies ACES and exposure to the whole frame on the way to the destination. **Why the old arrangement was wrong, not merely suboptimal:** tone mapping ran per OBJECT — only `cube.frag` and `skybox.frag` called the curve, so decals, particles, ribbons and world text never did; they alpha-blended against a buffer already through it. And a “bright” particle could not be bright, because 1.0 was the ceiling of the attachment it was written into, which is exactly the headroom a bloom pass needs to find something in. **The boundary:** everything world-space is inside — opaque meshes, skybox, decals, particles, ribbons, the vignette, world text — and the UI draws onto the resolved result. A `UiPanel`'s colour is **authored rather than captured**; `{0.1, 0.6, 0.9}` is a value someone typed and expects to see, and through ACES what appears on screen would become a function of how bright the scene behind it is, so the HUD would dim whenever the world got bright. The vignette is inside because attenuating light is what a real lens does before exposure. **The resolve is a DRAW, not a pass**, and that is still the load-bearing choice: a fullscreen triangle into whichever colour target is recording, so the window and the editor stay on one code path. **Two things execution found that the plan did not, in opposite directions.** Every pipeline was being built TWICE, once against the swapchain pass and once against the editor's viewport pass — but the scene now renders into ONE pass whatever its destination, so its seven pairs collapsed to seven singles and the backend got *smaller*: fourteen scene pipelines to seven, with only the resolve and the three UI draws still needing a pair. Against that, the editor wraps the whole of `RenderSystem::render` between `begin_viewport_pass` and `end_viewport_pass`, so opening the HDR pass inside it would nest one render pass in another — a validation error on the first editor frame. **Fixed by making the viewport pass lazy** (`begin_viewport_pass` requests, `ensure_viewport_pass` opens), mirroring `ensure_swapchain_pass`, which has worked that way since the editor path landed. **The appearance shift is accepted, not worked around.** Those four effects are tone mapped now; their current look was an accident of bypassing the curve, not a design. If they read wrong, the fix is to retune their authored colours — a compensation step that pre-inverted the curve would be physically meaningless and would fight bloom later, which is the whole problem being solved. **The contract tests inverted with the code.** They used to hold the two copies of `tonemap_aces` byte-identical; they now assert that exactly one shader in the tree maps anything, and that the three UI draws stay OUT of the HDR pass. Rewriting them earned their keep immediately: the first pass at `cube.frag` deleted its UBO's trailing `vec4 exposure;` along with the comment above it, and the vert/frag block-match test caught it. The Y-flip trap the plan flagged was real and is now closed by construction — the resolve has its own vertex shader that emits no UV at all, so the fragment shader cannot drift back to an interpolated one; `screen_effect.vert` still has that bug, invisible because a vignette is radially symmetric. **Gate:** 1358/1358, deps-ok, names-ok, and zero validation errors across `run --window --ticks 60` and `editor --ticks 40` on a real device. **What is NOT verified is the image.** Ordering is tested; that the scene is not darker or washed out, that the four newly-mapped effects read acceptably, and that the vignette still works needs a human at the window, and that is why the shift was accepted as a decision rather than asserted as a fact. It goes on the next verification page together with U6's drag feel and the standalone hover change. **Next:** bloom — and with it the RenderGraph question, which finally has a real trigger, since a mip chain's pass count varies with resolution.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-13 (**The HDR boundary is in; the Vulkan half is deliberately a separate slice.** Bloom being wanted soon fired the trigger the 🟡 offscreen-HDR item had been waiting on. **The problem it solves is that tone mapping is per-object:** only `cube.frag` and `skybox.frag` call `tonemap_aces`, so decals, particles, ribbons and world text are never tone mapped at all — they blend against a buffer already through the curve. That is incoherent in a way bloom makes fatal, because a "bright" particle *cannot* be bright: it is written into a buffer whose ceiling is 1.0, leaving no headroom for a bloom pass to find. **The boundary, decided with a human:** everything world-space renders into the HDR target and is tone mapped as one image; the UI draws onto the resolved result. UI colours are **authored rather than captured** — `{0.1, 0.6, 0.9}` is a value someone typed and expects to see — so tone mapping them would make the HUD dim whenever the scene got bright. The vignette sits *inside*, because attenuating light is what a real lens does before exposure. **`draw_hdr_resolve` is a DRAW, not a pass, and that is the load-bearing choice.** It renders a fullscreen triangle into whatever colour target is currently bound: the swapchain in `mosaic run --window`, the offscreen viewport target in the editor. Neither caller needs to know which, so both stay on one code path — which is what 37d worked to achieve and a resolve-as-a-pass would have undone. **What landed: Tasks 1–2 only.** The three default-no-op hooks, and `RenderSystem` bracketing world-space drawing between them. Because the hooks are no-ops, **nothing renders differently yet** — verified on the real device, all three entry points exit 0 with zero validation errors. `FakeRenderDevice` gained a single ordered call log, since the property under test is *relative* order and separate per-call vectors record counts rather than sequence. Seven cases, including the three empty-ish frames that would otherwise become special cases: no UI (a UI-driven resolve would leave a black screen), no world content (an unbalanced begin is a validation error, not a visual one), and no camera at all — the state every freshly created `World` is in, where `render()` has no early return. Probes 5/5 red. **Why the split:** the backend half has **no automated gate**. Nothing in the suite creates a `VkDevice`, so its only checks are a validation-layer grep and a human at the window; keeping it in its own diff is what makes that reviewable rather than buried. **A trap found before writing any of it**, and recorded for every future fullscreen pass: both render passes use a **negative-height (Y-flipped) viewport**, `ui.vert` compensates explicitly and `screen_effect.vert` does not — undetectable there because a vignette is radially symmetric. The plan said to reuse `screen_effect.vert` for the resolve, which would have shipped an upside-down scene visible only by eye. The resolve will instead take its UV from `gl_FragCoord.xy`, which is framebuffer space and immune to the flip — correct by construction rather than by reasoning no test can check. Bloom's blur chain will meet the same trap. **The RenderGraph stays deferred**, by the backlog's own refined criterion: the trigger is a *dynamic* pass need, "not just one more fixed pass", and this is one more fixed pass. **Bloom's mip chain is named as the probable real trigger**, since its pass count varies with resolution. **Gate:** 1357/1357, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. **Next:** Tasks 3–5 of the same plan — the `R16G16B16A16_SFLOAT` target, the resolve pipeline, and moving `tonemap_aces` out of the object shaders. Then bloom.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**U6 is complete — pointer capture and drag shipped, and half the routing rule turned out to already exist under another name.** `UiSystem::pressed_` persisted across frames and gated the click on a same-button release; that *was* a capture rule without the name, so the slice opened by renaming it `captured_`. What was genuinely missing: hover ignored the press entirely (`ui_button.hpp` documented that as deliberate), and there was no drag event at all. **Capture means the captured button owns the pointer:** no other button hovers while a press is held, and the captured one is hovered only while the cursor is inside its own rect — so it un-highlights when you drag off and re-highlights when you drag back, which is the affordance that tells a user whether releasing here will fire it. That changes shipped behaviour, so the comment asserting the opposite is corrected rather than left to contradict the code. **Four new actions**, on the button's existing channel: `drag_start` fires on movement past `kDragThreshold` rather than on press — otherwise every click in the engine would also emit `drag_start` and `drag_end`, and no handler could tell a click from a one-pixel drag — then `drag` per moving frame, `drag_end` on release, `drag_cancel` when the pointer leaves. Only the drag actions carry `x`/`y`; `click` and the hover actions keep their exact `{entity, action}` shape, which three shipped handlers already read. **The two endings are distinguishable without new plumbing, and that is the design's one real insight.** The editor's provider deliberately drops `down` when the cursor leaves the viewport panel, so a drag cannot keep running while the user clicks in the Inspector — which means `down` false with `inside` true is a genuine release, and `down` false with `inside` false is the pointer leaving. Aborting a drag and completing one are different outcomes, and conflating them is where drag code goes wrong. **Probes found two real gaps and one equivalent mutant, and telling them apart mattered.** Dropping `last_at_ = at` from the drag branch survived, because the no-movement test stopped one frame after `drag_start` and so only ever compared against that position; a test that moves twice then holds still now covers it. A stale `dragging_` would make the next plain click emit a spurious `drag_end`, which the after-cancel test could not see because it counts `drag_start`s. But removing `dragging_ = false` from the *release* stayed green and is **not** a gap: the press edge already resets it, and between release and the next press `captured_` is nil so the drag branch cannot run. That line stays as defence in depth with its comment corrected to say it is not load-bearing — the same call `ui_hover_style.hpp` made for its `is_valid` guard, and the same reasoning. **One test also passed before its implementation existed**, which is a finding about the test rather than a free win: it gave the neighbouring button a negative `order`, so the neighbour was never topmost and would not have hovered with or without capture. Replaced with a disjoint neighbour at a higher order, which is the only arrangement where the press lands on A while B is genuinely topmost later. **Gate:** 1343/1343, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. **What tests cannot judge, and is queued for the next human pass:** the threshold's *feel*. `0.01` is normalized and therefore anisotropic — about 19px horizontally against 10px vertically at 1920x1080 — and the value is a guess until someone drags something. Both are recorded in the backlog rather than left implicit. **Next:** the UI pillar's remaining groups are U8–U11; the five standing decisions from the previous entries are unchanged.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**A human verification pass caught the only defect this whole arc produced, and no test could have.** Seven manual checks over #245–#249, all seven passing — but the notes on two of them reported **two warnings where one was described**, and that turned out to be the real finding. The per-entity order lint and the pairwise conflict check were each firing correctly on the same action: one about an entity whose own two orders disagree, one about the pair that entity is half of. **Both were individually right, which is exactly why 1326 tests could not see it** — the duplication existed only at the seam where two correct things meet, and nothing is positioned to assert on a seam. **Fixed in #251:** `without_entities_in_conflict` drops a per-entity finding whose entity is already named in a pairwise conflict, so one authoring mistake earns one line, and the line kept is the more informative one. Matching BOTH ends of a pair is load-bearing there — `a` and `b` are assigned by entity value rather than by which one an author edited, so checking only `a` would still double-report about half the time, and which half would depend on entity ids. The precedence lives in its own function rather than inside either check, so each stays a pure independent answer to its own question and a reader asking "why didn't I get the other warning" finds it in one place. **The checklist page was also wrong, and that is the more transferable lesson.** It quoted ONE of the two lines the step emits as the expected output, so a correct result read as a surprise and had to be chased down with screenshots. The sting: the code comments written in that same session say plainly that both diagnostics run. **Familiarity is the cause** — you stop seeing output you have grown used to — which makes it a rule to write down rather than a slip to be more careful about. **The practice is now a registry skill rather than a habit of this session:** `human-verification-pass`, which went 1.0.0 → 1.2.0 in one evening, and every version came from using it rather than from thinking harder about it. 1.0.0 from building two pages; 1.1.0 from the expected-output mistake above plus per-item screenshot attachment, asked for mid-pass because evidence routed through chat arrives unanchored from the step that produced it; 1.2.0 from a cadence correction. **The cadence decision, which is the part that makes this affordable:** run a pass over an **arc**, not per change. A pass costs a human a context switch and a block of attention; a change costs them one line, and a reader asked too often stops reading carefully. Keep a running list of unverifiable claims between passes — that list is what a pass is assembled from, and without it the items that get lost are precisely the ones nothing else was checking. An arc that accumulates none needs no pass, which is a good sign rather than a missed step. **Where that leaves the project:** `main` at 92f029f, 1326/1326, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. The 🟢 actionable tier is empty. One 🟡 item (the offscreen HDR target, whose additive-blending trigger has not fired) and eight 🔴 items, none started. **Next:** the five decisions the previous entry lists still need a human, and none has become more answerable on its own. If the goal is to keep moving, **pointer capture/drag** is the strongest pick — U6's remaining third, implementable headlessly, and substantial enough to be worth the next verification pass.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**Session close — the `order` thread is finished end to end, the actionable backlog tier is EMPTY, and everything left needs a human decision rather than more code.** Five further slices landed after the pairwise check, all unattended and all machine-verified: **#245** proves a conflicting pair *actually* misroutes the click (one world, a real `PointerState` through press and release, then the same world rendered through `FakeRenderDevice` — the entity that answered is provably the one drawn underneath; probed by inverting `topmost_hit` and by reversing the panel sort, so it holds both mechanisms); **#246** collapsed three copies of the anchor clamper into one `to_anchor` in `ui_rect.hpp`, bounded against `Anchor::BottomRight` rather than a literal `8` — which was the actual hazard, since three independent bounds that must agree is how hit-testing and rendering come to disagree about where a button is; **#247** corrected two stale comments and re-derived the quick-wins tier; **#248** replaced the past-the-edge clamp's bounds assertions with exact values; **#249** shipped the off-texture sub-rect warning. **THE PATTERN WORTH CARRYING FORWARD: five of the eight slices found their own backlog entry to be wrong.** #244's recorded premise was two-thirds invalid (cross-pass `order` comparisons are meaningless). #241's row claimed "a copy of a shipped loop" and "documented" — neither true. #247 found `UiButton::order` carrying a comment that said validating the disagreement "needs an ownership relation ... which does not exist", sitting on the very field two shipped validations check. #248's row predicted the probe would be the hard part and was exactly right, including that the obvious mutation is **UB** rather than wrong behaviour. #249 was filed at "the `resolve_image_quad` seam", the one place it cannot go — a pure header-only function the render pass calls 60 times a second, with no ledger within reach. **None of those entries was careless; each was written before the code existed to contradict it.** Every one is corrected in place rather than left to mislead, and that correction is now the most valuable half of each slice. **A probe habit that paid for itself four times:** derive probes from the code, never from the assertion list. Two gaps in #244 (dropping either overlap axis survived because the one non-overlapping case separated its buttons on *both*; dropping the pair normalization survived because sequential entities already iterate in ascending order — so the test now destroys the first button to make swap-and-pop reverse them, which is the production hazard `ui_hit.hpp` documents), one in #241 (`std::sort` is incidentally stable below **17** elements on this toolchain, measured, so both stability tests use 20), and a pre-existing one in the panel pass that the same probe exposed. **A process hazard now fixed in the harness:** restoring a mutated source with `mv` preserves its mtime, so ninja keeps the MUTANT binary and a later full `ctest` reports bogus failures — in the probed area, which is exactly where they look real. Always `touch` after restoring. **Gate at close:** 1323/1323, dependency-rule and test-name lints clean, `mosaic run`, `run --window` and `editor` all exit 0 and silent on the shipped demo. **WHAT NEEDS A HUMAN, and why each is blocked on judgement rather than effort:** (1) **the `Ambiguous` conflict's noise level** — reporting a tied-order pair is defensible, since destroying an unrelated button can flip the click with nothing looking different, but nothing in this repository has two buttons so it cannot be judged here; flagged rather than tuned. (2) **`imgui_test_engine`** — the recorded `ImGuiFieldVisitor` gap was filed as "needs a headless ImGui context", which was too optimistic and is now corrected: a null-backend context is easy, but exercising a `DragInt`'s write-back needs a simulated multi-frame mouse drag against ImGui's internal active-item state. That is a new third-party dependency, so it is a decision, not a task. (3) **pointer capture/drag**, U6's remaining third — implementable headlessly, but its payoff only verifies under someone's hands. (4) **enum combo boxes** for `anchor`/`fit_mode`/easing — needs enum names on the reflection surface, and the widget half lands in gap (2). (5) **the offscreen HDR target** — carries a real design decision (where UI draws relative to the resolve) and a visual result no test can check. **Next:** pick one of those five with a human in the loop. There is no further unattended work in this area that would not be busywork.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**UI — the pairwise order conflict check shipped, the correctness check the #240 lint was only standing in for.** Two overlapping `UiButton`s can be ranked one way by `UiButton::order`, which decides the click, and the other way by `UiPanel::order`, which decides what the user sees on top — so the click lands on a button nobody can see, with no symptom but the wrong thing happening. `find_order_conflicts` reports those pairs. Its prerequisite landed with #241: before that only the panel pass sorted, so "what the user sees on top" was not well defined. **The rects are shared, not re-derived, and that was the first design decision.** `UiSystem::candidates` flattened every button into a `HitCandidate`; the check needs the *same* rects, so the flatten moved to `engine/ui/ui_button_rects.hpp` and both use it. A diagnostic that resolved buttons its own way would police rectangles nobody clicks on, and would drift the first time either side changed. Deliberately **not** put in `ui_hit.hpp`, whose comment makes a point of needing no `World` and no component storage so `topmost_hit` stays trivially testable. **Two finding kinds, and the second one is a judgement call worth reviewing.** *Inverted* is the definite bug: both axes ordered, opposite rankings. *Ambiguous* is one axis tied and the other not — reported because the outcome then falls to iteration order over two different sparse sets, so **destroying an unrelated button can flip which one receives the click** with nothing in the scene having changed visually, exactly as `ui_hit.hpp`'s rule 2 warns. Nothing in this repository has two buttons, so I cannot tell yet whether *Ambiguous* proves useful or noisy in a real scene; it is flagged rather than quietly tuned. **Both axes tied is deliberately silent**, because the author expressed no preference either way and reporting it would fire on every pair of overlapping default-constructed buttons. **Only the four authored integers are compared** — the check never asks which entity comes first in a `for_each`, because that is precisely the fragile property it exists to warn about. **Overlap is inclusive**, matching `topmost_hit`'s `>=`/`<=` containment, so two buttons touching along an edge count: a pointer exactly on that line is inside both. **Probes: 9 of 9 red, and two were real gaps that each taught something.** Dropping *either* axis from `rects_overlap` survived, because the one non-overlapping case separated its buttons on **both** axes and stayed non-overlapping with half the test gone; the distinguishing inputs are a pair overlapping in x only and its mirror in y only, and both now exist. Dropping the pair normalization also survived, because entities created in sequence iterate in ascending order and the swap never fires — so I **measured** that destroying the *first* button makes the sparse set's swap-and-pop yield the later entity first, and built the test on that. That is the production scenario `ui_hit.hpp` documents, now pinned. **The ledger was unified rather than duplicated.** A pairwise finding has two entities and four numbers and does not fit `OrderWarningModel`'s key, so the transition-only semantics moved into a string-keyed `WarningLedger` that both diagnostics build their own key and signature for; `emit` returns indices so a caller maps results straight back to its own findings. Behaviour-preserving — all six existing cases pass unchanged, including the drag-burst reviewed at the window and kept. **A stale comment got corrected on the way through:** `order_warnings.hpp` argued "why every frame at all" from `order` *not* being editable in the Inspector, which #242 made false three commits after it was written. **Confirmed end to end, because the call site has no unit test:** a temporary second overlapping button in the demo produced both warnings — the per-entity lint on the one entity, the pairwise check on the pair — exactly once across 40 ticks, with the lower entity correctly normalized first. Reverted; the shipped demo has one button and stays silent. **Gate:** 1321/1321, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0. **Nothing here needs eyes**, which is why this slice was chosen for unattended work. **What it still cannot prove:** that a reported conflict *causes* the wrong button to receive the event. That rests on `topmost_hit`'s tests plus the panel sort, covered separately but never together; a test driving a real pointer into a conflicting pair would close it and is filed. **Next:** combo boxes for the enum-as-int fields (needs enum metadata on the reflection surface); pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third; the three duplicated anchor clampers, now recorded.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**Manual verification of #240–#242 passed, 7 of 7, and it closed the one design question the three slices left open.** A human worked a seven-item checklist at the window. **The two items no test could ever cover are now confirmed by eye:** the demo's orange underlay sprite renders *behind* the checker, which is #241's image-pass sort working in a real frame rather than merely in a submission-order assertion; and hovering the HUD bar brightens **both the bar and its "HP" label**, which is the `UiParent` style walk from #239 still intact — the label carries no style of its own, so its brightening cannot come from anywhere else. **The open question is now answered: the order lint's drag-burst is accepted as it stands.** Dragging `UiButton::order` from 0 to 7 in the Inspector emits roughly seven warnings, one per intermediate value, because `OrderWarningModel` treats the two numbers as part of a finding's identity. That was unreachable until `field_int` shipped, so the two slices' interaction had never been seen by anyone; reviewed at the window, the verdict was to keep it. **Recorded in `order_warnings.hpp` itself, not only here**, because the next reader to watch seven lines appear during one drag will reasonably assume a de-duplication bug — the comment now says it is not one, that debouncing was considered and declined, and that the burst should not be "fixed" without revisiting the decision. Also confirmed: `field_int` edits reach the live component (dragging `anchor` moves the bar, 47 clamps to TopLeft without crashing, the scene goes dirty); the text pass sorts when two labels are made to overlap by hand; a negative `UiText::order` still does **not** cross the pass boundary, so the limitation written into both component headers is accurate; and headless `mosaic run` emits no `ui:` warnings, so the editor-time diagnostic has not leaked. **Nothing needed fixing.** The checklist is at https://claude.ai/artifact/SomnwAWoXWikiMKFbPbdqZ. **Still unverified anywhere:** `ImGuiFieldVisitor`'s widget calls remain untested by machine — T3 and T5 exercised them by hand, which is not a regression guard, so the recorded gap stands. **Next:** the real pairwise overlapping-button order check (its prerequisite landed with #241); combo boxes for the enum-as-int fields; pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-11 (**Studio — `int` fields are editable in the Inspector, and a probe corrected a claim I had just written into the code.** This closes the gap the order lint opened two slices ago: a user read "UiButton order 7 but UiPanel order 0" in the Console and then could not edit either number, because `ImGuiFieldVisitor` had no `field_int` and every `int` rendered as a read-only label. Three small pieces: `FieldVisitor::field_int` defaulting to `field_readonly` (the same optional-hook pattern `field_asset`, `field_string`, `field_entity` and `field_handle_asset` all follow), one `std::is_same_v<U, int>` branch in `FieldVisitorAdapter`, one `DragInt` override. **Thirteen other `FieldVisitor` implementors needed no changes** — ten in tests, the `MarkdownFieldVisitor` behind `docs/reference/components.md`, three collectors in `mcp_tools.cpp` — because the default hook keeps them compiling, and the default renders `std::to_string`, byte-identical to the generic arithmetic branch it replaced. **Predicted and then verified: the generated component reference did not move** (`git status docs/reference/` clean after the pre-commit hook regenerated it), which mattered because that hook runs on every commit. **The payoff is wider than the lint that prompted it.** Surveying what `int` reaches made `ParticleEmitter`'s `burst`, `burst_trigger`, `max_particles` and three easing curves, `TrailRenderer::max_points`, `AudioSource::attenuation_model` and `NavAgent::goal_x`/`goal_y` editable too. **`int` only, deliberately** — the reflected `std::uint32_t` fields are ids, dimensions and versions (`Image::width`, `NetReplicated::id`, `AssetMetadata::version`), which nobody should drag. **Safety was checked rather than assumed, and all four enum-as-int paths defend themselves:** `to_anchor` clamps outside 0–8, `resolve_image_quad` falls back to Stretch, `apply_easing` and `to_ma_attenuation_model` each have a `default:` arm. The two allocation-shaped fields are safe too — `max_particles` is only ever compared (`<`), so a negative emits nothing, and `max_points` is guarded `> 0 ? : 1`. A `DragInt` also grants no new *capability*: `SetComponentCommand` already let a script write any of these. **The probe that mattered failed to compile, and that was the finding.** I had written a comment claiming the int branch sits below the bool branch because putting it above "would turn every reflected bool into a draggable 0/1". The mutant that does exactly that **does not build**: `field_int` takes `int&` and a `bool&` cannot bind to it, so the compiler forbids the mistake outright. The comment is corrected to say that, and the test's `ro:flag` assertion is relabelled as a cheap guard rather than the thing holding the line — it was never what made the ordering safe. Remaining probes 3 of 3 red (default hook made inert, the adapter handed a copy instead of the live field, the branch removed). **One gap recorded rather than papered over:** `ImGuiFieldVisitor` has **no test file at all** — pre-existing, not introduced here. ImGui needs a live context, so `field_float`, `field_string`, `field_asset` and now `field_int` are each one widget call no test executes. The adapter dispatch is well covered; what is not is "does the widget write back and set `changed_`". Filed, with the note that closing it needs headless-ImGui test infrastructure. **Gate:** 1296/1296, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. **Still unconfirmed by eye:** dragging an `order` in the running Inspector — the widget call itself is the untested seam above. **Next:** combo boxes for the enum-as-int fields (needs enum metadata on the reflection surface, probably its own spec); the real pairwise overlapping-button order check; pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-11 (**UI — the HUD image and text passes now sort by `order`, and the backlog row describing the work was wrong in both of its claims.** The row called it "a copy of a shipped loop onto a sibling path" and said `order` was "reflected, documented, and ignored". **Reflected and ignored, yes. Documented, no** — `UiImage::order` and `UiText::order` carried **no comment at all**, unlike `UiPanel::order`; `docs/reference/components.md` is generated from reflection, so it listed the fields with no semantics. That turned out to be good news: with no promised contract to honour, the semantics were ours to define. **And it was not a copy.** The panel pass collects every quad and batches them into **one** `draw_ui` call after sorting; the image and text passes each draw **per entity**, carrying their own texture or font-atlas handle, so neither can be batched. Both needed a collect → `stable_sort` → emit **reshape**, which changes the order of the calls and not their number. All three passes now read alike. **The semantics chosen, and written into both headers:** higher `order` draws on top **within its own pass**; equal orders keep `for_each` order via the stable sort. **The pass boundary is absolute and the order cannot cross it** — the three passes run in a fixed sequence, so a `UiText` is above every `UiPanel` whatever either `order` says, and a negative `UiText::order` will **not** push a label behind a panel. That limitation is documented where a reader meets the field, not only in the backlog, and a unified cross-pass z-order is filed as its own architectural item. **The stability is load-bearing, not incidental:** `ui_hit.hpp`'s rule 2 documents the hit tie-break as agreeing with "the entity later in iteration draws on top", so an unstable sort here would quietly break a rule another file depends on. **Probes: 6 of 6 red, and the two interesting ones were about that stability.** Swapping `stable_sort` for `std::sort` survived at first — libstdc++ falls back to insertion sort below **17** elements, which is stable in practice, so three equal-order items cannot tell the two apart. Measured the boundary at exactly 17 and raised both stability tests to **20**; the probe went red. Then the same probe aimed at the **panel pass alone** came back green: **a pre-existing gap**, in the very pass the new comments cite as the reference rule. Closed it with a twenty-panel stability test, which is a cheap fix in code this slice was already touching. **The demo gained an overlapping sprite**, following U7's precedent of making a feature checkable by eye: a warm-tinted second `UiImage` at `order = -1` under the existing checker. Before the sort, `for_each` order would have put the later-created underlay on top — so the demo shows the fix rather than merely containing it. **A sandbox cannot screenshot the window, so the demo's claim is pinned by a test instead**: `tests/unit/app/demo_scene_test.cpp` renders the real default scene through a recording device and asserts the underlay is submitted first, identified by **tint** rather than index so a swap cannot pass. Confirmed load-bearing — removing the sort fails it. The new entity carries no `Transform`, like the existing `hud_label`, so the default-scene count assertion (which counts `Transform`s) is untouched. **Gate:** 1293/1293, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. **Still unconfirmed by eye:** the demo's overlap needs a human at the window; every claim a test can make is made. **Next:** `field_int` in the Inspector, which would make the previous slice's order warning actionable in-editor; the real pairwise overlapping-button check; pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-11 (**UI — the `order`-disagreement diagnostic shipped, and building it proved two-thirds of its own recorded premise wrong.** The backlog asked for a warning on "an entity carrying both a `UiButton` and a `UiPanel`/`UiImage`/`UiText` whose `order` values differ", unblocked by the hover slice's ownership rule. That check was built, tested, and **fired a false positive on our own demo scene** — and the false positive was the valuable part. **The three HUD passes in `render_system.hpp` are fixed and sequential** (panels, then images over panels, then text over both) **and only the panel pass `stable_sort`s.** So a `UiText` is above a `UiPanel` unconditionally, whatever either `order` says: `UiImage::order` and `UiText::order` are reflected, documented, and **inert**. Comparing a button's `order` against either is comparing numbers that participate in no ordering at all. The demo made this unarguable: its `camera` entity carries `UiPanel{0}`, `UiImage{0}` and `UiText{1}`, so **no single button order could have silenced the warning** — the three visuals do not agree with each other either. **Scoped to button-vs-`UiPanel`**, which is the only comparison with a meaning, and the demo went silent. **The deeper correction, recorded rather than papered over: this is a heuristic lint, not a correctness check.** A single button cannot misroute a click whatever its `order` says. The real failure is **pairwise and relative** — two overlapping buttons where A outranks B in `UiButton::order` but B outranks A in `UiPanel::order`, so the click goes to A and the user sees B. The shipped lint catches the authoring slip that bug grows from (edit the panel's order, forget the button's), which is cheap; the real check needs rect-overlap testing between every pair of buttons and is filed as its own item. **A second premise also cracked:** the hover slice cited the template's entity 8 carrying button+image+panel+text as proof that co-location means ownership, but the demo piles the unrelated "MOSAIC" title and checker image onto `camera` purely for convenience. **Co-location is not always ownership** — the rule is a convention the repo follows, not an invariant the data enforces. Nothing breaks today, because the lint reads only `UiPanel`, which there genuinely is the button's visual. **The lint's advice is currently unactionable inside the editor**, and that is worth knowing before someone treats the warning as finished: `ImGuiFieldVisitor` has no `field_int`, so every `order` renders as a read-only label — a user reads "UiButton order 7 but UiPanel order 0" and then has to edit the scene file by hand. Filed as a quick win. **The de-dup is every-frame on purpose**, and the reason I gave for it while designing was wrong: `order` is not Inspector-editable, so "catches what a user types" is unreachable. It stands because `SetComponentCommand` goes through reflection, so a script, a plugin, the Script Console, an MCP `set_component` or the Blender live-link can all write an order mid-session. **Probes: 9 of 9 red, and the one gap found was real** — a comparison written on `std::abs` survived the first suite, because the only negative case (-1 against 0) has unequal magnitudes. The distinguishing input is **-2 against 2**: equal magnitude, opposite z, the worst disagreement to miss. A test for it exists and the probe is red. **A process hazard worth recording:** the probe harness restored each mutated source with `mv`, which preserves mtime, so ninja saw the file as up-to-date and **left the mutant binary in place** — a full `ctest` then reported four bogus failures. The harness now `touch`es after restoring. **Gate:** 1286/1286, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0, and the editor is silent on the demo. **Functionally CONFIRMED end-to-end on 2026-10-11:** authoring `order = 7` on the demo's HUD button produced exactly one `[WARN] ui: entity ... has UiButton order 7 but UiPanel order 0` across 40 ticks — the call site and the de-dup together, which no unit test covers since the warn lives in `app/run/editor.cpp`. **Next:** the paired pass-sorting fix (image *and* text, now known to be one slice rather than the recorded image-only quick win); `field_int` to make this lint actionable; pointer capture/drag is still the remaining third of U6.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**UI — engine-rendered hover feedback shipped, and the blocker it waited on turned out to be a non-problem.** Two backlog items were recorded as blocked on a missing definition of "this visual belongs to this button". **The relation already existed as ENTITY IDENTITY and had simply never been written down:** the shipped template's entity 8 carries `UiButton` + `UiImage` + `UiPanel` + `UiText` together, the demo adds `UiButton` to the `camera` entity that already holds the HUD `UiPanel`, and there is no counter-example anywhere in the repository. So the slice was "state the rule, then use it" rather than "design an ownership model" — worth remembering as a pattern, because the backlog's framing of a blocker was wrong in a way that made the work look bigger than it was. **The rule:** an element is styled by the nearest `UiHoverStyle` at or above it in the `UiParent` chain, and only if that chain reaches a `UiHovered`. **The walk goes UP** because `UiParent` points upward and nothing stores children — resolving a hovered button's descendants would have meant scanning every entity. Two things fell out free: the demo's nested "HP" label brightens with its parent bar, and a style authored on the bar is **inherited** by that label, so hover is configured once per button rather than once per visual. **`UiHovered` is deliberately unregistered, and that absence is load-bearing.** `ComponentReflection::save` iterates every *registered* type, so registering it would persist a phantom hover into any scene saved while the cursor rested on a button — and loading that scene would start up already highlighted, with no pointer near it and no exit event coming. Asserted two ways: a test that the type is not registered, and a test that serializes a world **while hovering** and finds no `UiHovered` in the output. The cost is that it does not appear in the Inspector, which works off reflection — a claim made while choosing the approach said it would, and the spec records that as withdrawn rather than quietly dropping it. **Feedback is a render-time override, never a mutation.** Nothing writes to `UiPanel::color` / `UiImage::tint` / `UiText::color`, so authored values survive. The demo previously assigned `panel->alpha` from a script; that worked and was the wrong thing to ship as the example, since a scene saved mid-hover would have persisted the tint. The script is deleted and the demo authors a style. **Multipliers rather than absolute colours**, so a default-constructed style is an exact no-op; the cost is that hover cannot be a *specific* colour, recorded as a backlog item. **The clamp is two-sided on purpose:** `tint` is authored data, so a scene can hold a negative, and a clamp written as `min(v, 1.0f)` passes that through to the shader as a negative channel. **Probes: 16 of 17 red, and the two that were not each taught something different.** One was a genuine coverage gap — mutating the cap-exhausted return left the suite green, because the only deep-chain case put the style ABOVE the cap where the collected value is null either way; the distinguishing input is a chain with a style INSIDE the cap and no tag anywhere, which that mutant would style while nothing is hovered. A test for it now exists and the probe is red. The other, dropping the `is_valid` guard in the walk, produced no red and **is not a gap**: `get_component` already returns nullptr for a nil or stale id because the sparse set compares the full `EntityID` including its generation. The guard stays as defence in depth, but the comment claiming it was what made nil and stale safe is corrected — that comment was the actual defect the probe found. One of the spec's eight named probes could not be built at all ("walk down instead of up" has no implementation, which is why the design walks up); probe 7, farthest-ancestor-wins, is the real equivalent. **Gate:** 1269/1269, tier rules and the test-name checker clean, and all three entry points exit 0 — including headless `mosaic run`, which installs no pointer provider, so nothing there can ever be hovered. **Visually CONFIRMED at the window on 2026-10-11:** hovering the HUD bar brightens **both the bar and its "HP" label**. That is the ownership rule confirmed at runtime, and it is the one claim no test could make — the label carries **no style of its own**, so its brightening can only come from inheriting the bar's style up the `UiParent` chain, through the render pass's own walk. The unit tests pin the walk and the arithmetic; only a human at the window could confirm the two meet correctly in a real frame. In `mosaic editor` it does nothing until **Play**, because the loop is stopped in authoring mode. **Next:** the `order`-disagreement diagnostic is now concrete and unblocked; pointer capture/drag is the remaining third of U6; 9-slice stays deliberately deferred as a longer-term goal.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**Fix — click-to-select was dead on a freshly opened editor, a regression from the editor-starts-stopped slice that its own acceptance pass could not have caught.** `PhysicsSystem` creates backend bodies **lazily inside `update()`**, and `app/run/editor.cpp` calls `loop.stop()` while stopped, so `RuntimeLoop::advance` returns without ticking and `update()` never runs. Once the editor STARTED stopped, a fresh launch had **no physics bodies at all** — and `pick_entity` raycasts the physics world (`studio/pick.hpp`), so every viewport click cleared the selection instead of making one. Inspector edits to a `RigidBody`'s Transform were not reaching the backend either, by the same path. **Why the earlier 23-item manual pass missed it:** the editor started *playing* then, so the first tick created the bodies, and pressing Stop afterwards left them in place — the tester's click-to-select check ran against a world that had already ticked. **Why no test caught it:** every case in `pick_test.cpp` ticks first, and the setup comment says exactly what that buys (`// derive WorldTransform + register the body`), so the suite had no un-ticked case at all. **The fix:** `PhysicsSystem::sync_to_world` reconciles the backend with the ECS — reap, create, push Transform edits — **without advancing time**, and the editor calls it every frame beside the `transform_system.update(world, 0.0f)` it already called for the same reason. Deliberately **not** `update(world, 0.0f)`: that still reaches `backend_->step(0, substeps)` and then harvests contact events, so a stopped editor would emit contact-begin events for anything already touching, which the demo logs. Verified: a stopped editor run emits zero contact lines. The call is unconditional rather than gated on `!is_playing()`, because while playing `world.tick` runs the same reconcile as its first phase, so it is idempotent — and a gate there is one more thing to get wrong at the play/stop boundary. **The general lesson, worth more than the fix:** anything initialised lazily inside a system's `update()` silently does not exist in a stopped editor. Physics was the one that bit; any future system with lazy setup will behave the same way, and the editor is now a first-class caller that needs an explicit authoring-time entry point rather than a tick. **Gate:** 1213/1213 with two new cases — one picking on a frame that never ticked, one asserting the sync emits no contacts — plus both CI checkers clean and `mosaic editor` exiting 0.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**UI — U6 interaction depth shipped, two of its three features: z-order hit resolution and hover enter/exit. The motivating bug was a WRONG answer, not an undefined tie-break.** `UiSystem`'s press path took the **first** button `for_each` yielded, while the panel pass `stable_sort`s ascending and draws in that order — so among equal `order` values the entity drawn **last** is on top. First-hit and last-drawn are opposite ends of the same list, which means a click on two overlapping equal-order buttons went to the button **the user could not see**. Worth recording that an earlier scoping read of `ui_system.hpp` concluded the opposite — that the *last* match won — and was corrected in the spec rather than quietly dropped, because it changes how strong the motivation is. **What shipped:** all the tie-break arithmetic in one pure function (`engine/ui/ui_hit.hpp`'s `topmost_hit`), testable with no world and no pointer provider; `UiButton` gains its own `order`, declared **last** so the demo's positional brace init kept compiling; and hover emits on the button's **existing** `event` channel with `action:"hover_enter"` / `"hover_exit"`, needing no new component field and no second channel because the click path already carried `{entity, action}`. **Why the maths is a pure function, third slice running:** the failure mode is picking the *wrong* button, which produces plausible behaviour — a click does something, just not the thing under the cursor — so neither review nor eyeballing catches it. **Revisit that seam if** hit resolution ever needs the hierarchy (U9 clipping regions, or ownership-aware ordering); today it is flat. **Capture/drag was split out at the start**, not dropped: z-order and hover are coupled ("which button is hovered" has no answer without a tie-break, so hover would have flickered between overlaps), while capture is already half-present and is the riskiest third — its failure modes are the subtlest, which is the bug class that shipped viewport pointer input **dead** while thirteen tests stayed green. **Two accepted costs, both recorded as live backlog items rather than footnotes:** a button's `order` and its visual's are independent and can **disagree** with nothing warning about it (validating needs a button→visual ownership relation, and `UiParent` is a *transform* hierarchy); and on events-only, the engine renders **nothing** — hover is visible only because the demo got a script, so "the UI feels dead" is half-fixed by the engine and half by a demonstration. **Writing the plan's Review Focus list found two edge cases that fell out of neither the spec nor the natural tests.** An absent pointer provider fell back to `PointerState{}` — which is `{0, 0, false}`, a *real* position inside any button anchored `TopLeft` at `{0,0}`, and the test harness's own button is exactly that — so hover would have emitted a phantom enter for a pointer that does not exist; harmless for clicks, since no press ever arrives. And a hovered entity that loses its `UiButton` has no channel name left to emit `hover_exit` on, because the name lived on the component: it emits nothing but still clears `hovered_`, or the next genuine hover would be mistaken for "no change" and swallowed. **The spec's one open risk is now measured and answered.** Hover does **not** stick when the pointer leaves the window — the GLFW provider clamps the cursor to `[0,1]` (`app/main.cpp:301-314`), so it tracks to the window **edge** instead. Still wrong for a button flush to that edge, which stays hovered while the pointer is over another application; the demo's bar starts at x = 0.02 so it is unaffected, which is also why eyes would not catch it. Backlogged as a **provider** fix (a `GLFW_HOVERED`-backed "inside the window" flag, the same `PointerState` widening U9 needs) rather than a timeout inside `UiSystem`, which would make hover depend on frame timing. **Probes: 12 of 12 red.** One of the spec's eight named probes did not apply — `std::sort` versus `stable_sort` — because `topmost_hit` deliberately does not sort: it is a single O(n) pass where the equal-order rule is one documented character (`>=`), so probe 1 (`>=` to `>`) is the direct equivalent. **Two things the plan got wrong, both caught by running it:** eight assertions across `ui_system_test.cpp` and `windowed_systems_test.cpp` counted *every* event on a button's channel as a proxy for "a click happened", and hover now shares that channel **under the same name** — so the count cannot even be filtered by name; they now assert the click specifically, which is sharper.
