And the demo's own `hud_click` logger checked only `e.name`, so it would have logged "HUD button clicked" on **every pointer crossing** — a name-only test is no longer sufficient anywhere. **Gate:** 1234/1234, tier rules and the test-name checker clean, and both `mosaic run --window` and `mosaic editor` exit 0. **One Critical and six Important findings came back from the whole-branch review, all fixed in one pass.** The sharpest: the `if (!pointer_)` guard closed the phantom-hover hole for an EMPTY provider and I never extended the reasoning to providers that return a ZEROED one — and every real provider spelled "no pointer" as exactly the `{0,0,false}` the guard was written to reject. Headless `mosaic run` never creates a GLFW window, so it reported `{0,0}` every tick and would hover any button whose rect contains the origin **forever** — which a default `UiButton` (`TopLeft`, pos `{0,0}`, size `{0.1,0.1}`) does; the demo escaped only because its bar starts at x = 0.02. In the editor, `pointer_state` gated only `down` on `hovered`, so moving the cursor onto the Inspector kept reporting a position clamped to the viewport edge and latched an edge-anchored button lit with no `hover_exit` ever firing. Fixed by giving `PointerState` an explicit `inside` flag honoured in `topmost_hit`, gating the editor provider on the `hovered` signal **that already existed** for `down`, and giving headless no provider at all rather than one that returns zeroes. My own backlog note had deferred part of this claiming the fix needed a U9-style widening — that was wrong for the editor path, where the signal was already sampled three lines away. **Also corrected: this file and the spec both recorded a FALSE reason for hover being inert in a stopped editor.** It is not the play-mode gate — `pointer_state` never gates position — it is that `editor.cpp` calls `loop.stop()`, so `UiSystem::update` never runs. That matters because the recorded reason is what a maintainer acts on: anything that makes the editor tick while stopped (an authoring-time system, or a fix for `editor --ticks`) turns hover on in authoring mode, where in this demo pointer movement writes `panel->alpha` into a scene the user may save. **Outstanding, needs a human:** with `mosaic run --window`, moving the pointer over the HUD bar should brighten it and moving away should dim it; in `mosaic editor` it does nothing until **Play**, because the loop is stopped. **Next:** capture/drag is the remaining third of U6; 9-slice is unblocked but deliberately deferred as a longer-term goal; the two accepted costs above likely want solving together, since both need a UI ownership definition. The standing open question is unchanged — whether UI is a near-term goal at all.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**UI — U7 image presentation shipped, two of its three features: `UiImage` sub-rects and Stretch/Fit/Fill aspect modes. 9-slice was split out at the START of the slice, not dropped from it.** The three features U7 grouped are not independent — 9-slice needs nine sub-rects of one texture, so it is built *on top of* this UV plumbing, and bundling would have put the plumbing, an aspect enum and a **nine-fold draw-count change** in one diff where a reviewer would have to find the performance dimension amongst two unrelated features. **Three of the four scoping findings changed the design rather than confirming it.** The sub-rect mechanism was not new design at all: `UiTextPush` has carried a `uv[4]` and `ui_text.vert` the exact `mix()` line since the text slice, so `UiImagePush` 32 → 48 and `ui_image.vert` are a near-copy of a shipped pattern. `TextureCache` already loaded the `Image` to call `create_texture` and then **threw the dimensions away**, so it now keeps `{handle, width, height}` and nothing new is loaded. And `RenderSystem`'s existing `if (tex == 0) return` turned "what if the dimensions are unknown" from a fallback to invent into an **invariant to pin**: a non-zero handle implies non-zero dimensions, recorded only alongside a successful upload, so no caller needs an unknown-dimensions branch and no frame can show the whole atlas while waiting for a load. **The arithmetic is CPU-side behind a pure function** (`engine/ui/ui_fit.hpp`'s `resolve_image_quad`), and that was a close call — roughly 75/25, recorded as such in the spec. The shader could call `textureSize` and get the dimensions for free, and aspect correction is rasterization-adjacent. Three things decided it: the failure mode (using one of the two aspects) produces a **plausible** image rather than a broken one, so neither review nor eyeballing catches it and only a unit test can; `layout_text` already takes `aspect_` and corrects on the CPU, so this follows precedent rather than preference; and `textureSize` saves nothing, because a **pixel** sub-rect needs the dimensions on the CPU regardless. **The condition for revisiting, stated now so it is checkable later:** if the correction ever needs **per-pixel** information — tiling within a sub-rect, or a shader-side 9-slice — the shader is right and this seam becomes a dead end; today the correction is per-quad. Sub-rects are authored in **texture pixels**, not normalized UVs, because that is how atlas art is authored and how any tool reports it, and hand-divided UVs break silently when an atlas is repacked. **BOTH aspects are load-bearing and every aspect test uses a non-square screen deliberately** — a normalized rect is not square, so the correction reconciles the texture's `w/h` against `(rect.w * screen_aspect) / rect.h`, and a case on a square screen would pass with the screen-aspect term dropped. **The probes earned their keep: 11 of 12 red, and the one that was not found a real gap.** Recording `width`/`height` even when `handle == 0` left the whole suite green, because the only miss the tests exercised was an *unregistered* id — which never loads an `Image`, so the guarded code never runs. The case that exercises it is an `Image` that loads and then fails to upload; that test now exists and the probe is red. Two other things worth knowing: the plan's own worked examples were executed against a reference implementation rather than trusted, which caught a test case that would have gone **red against correct code** (a 50x100 texture in a `{0.2, 0.2}` rect at screen aspect 0.5 has rect_aspect *equal* to the image aspect, so nothing letterboxes); and `app/run/demo_scene.hpp`'s **positional** `UiImage` init silently re-bound `tint` onto the first new field — gcc caught it only because `Vec3` does not convert to `Vec2`, so two same-typed neighbours would have compiled and rendered wrong. That init is now designated. **Gate:** 1203/1203 green, tier rules clean, `ui_image.vert` compiles to SPIR-V, and both `mosaic editor` and `mosaic run --window` exit 0 with zero validation output — which matters here rather than being ceremonial, since a push-constant range that disagreed with the shader's 48-byte block is exactly what Vulkan validation reports. **Visually CONFIRMED at the window on 2026-10-10**, which is the only instrument that could confirm it: the demo's top-right checker renders **square**, letterboxed inside its rect, rather than stretched into rectangles, and it **stays square as the window is resized**. That single observation is the whole slice, because it can only hold if BOTH the texture aspect and the screen aspect are live at runtime — a build using one of them would look correct at exactly one window size and wrong at every other. The unit tests pin the arithmetic and the contract tests pin the GLSL, but neither can execute the shader, so this closed the last gap rather than being a formality. **Next:** 9-slice is now unblocked and carries three decisions of its own; U6 interaction depth still waits on the `UiButton` `order` question; the editor-starts-stopped change still carries the `--ticks` trap. And the standing open question is unchanged — whether UI is a near-term goal at all.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**Manual acceptance passed, and it produced a design decision — the editor should start STOPPED.** A human worked a 23-item checklist at the window and every item passed. That mattered rather than being a formality: the viewport-pointer branch review had proved the feature was *shipped dead* while all thirteen of its tests were green, so a person at the window was the only instrument that could confirm the fix. **What it confirmed, specifically, because none of it is reachable by an automated test here:** the HUD button logs `hud_click` on a play-mode click; the sphere, both boxes and the capsule each take a visible impulse while the static ground does not; clicks over the Inspector or Hierarchy never reach the scene; pressing Stop restores click-to-select and silences the button; a press held through a mode change clicks nothing until a fresh press; a slider drag released over the viewport shoves nothing; and coordinates stay true near the panel edges after a resize. Exposure was confirmed in the same pass — 1.0 unchanged, 2.0 brightening geometry **and** sky together, the unlit overlays correctly *not* following, and a negative value rendering **black rather than white**, which is the ACES floor fix holding. **The decision that came out of it.** Because `PlayState` defaults to `playing_ = true`, the editor begins simulating the moment it opens — so with viewport clicks routed to the game while playing, the default click now drives the game and selection requires pressing Stop first. The product is editor-facing, so the default should be authoring. **Deferred rather than done, with the trap written down:** it is one token (`playing_ = false`) plus one inverted test, but `mosaic editor --ticks N` **would never terminate**, because `FramePump` exits on `loop_.tick_count() >= ticks` while `RuntimeLoop::advance` returns early when stopped, so the counter never moves. CI is unaffected (its smoke test runs `mosaic run`), but `--ticks` is exactly how the editor is verified in a sandbox that cannot screenshot — which is how every editor change this month was checked. So it needs a decision, not just an edit: `--ticks` counting frames in the editor path, or implying play, or a separate frame limit. The backlog entry also names the consequences that are **correct and must not be "fixed" on sight** — the scene sitting still until Play, the Blender live-link staying quiet, the Step button becoming enabled — because "the editor looks broken, nothing moves" is the obvious wrong conclusion. **Handoff state: nothing is outstanding.** Eight PRs merged across 2026-10-09, main green at 1183/1183, both `--window` entry points clean, and the repository and build verified intact after a power loss. Both 🟢 tiers and the sole 🟡 are struck, so the next pick comes from 🔴 — in order of how little it needs from a human: **U7 image presentation** is independent and needs no decision, **U6 interaction depth** is first in the UI decomposition's own ordering but carries the `UiButton` `order` question, and **U10** is blocked on an editor keyboard-input provider. Whether UI is a near-term goal at all is still unanswered, and the decomposition deliberately does not answer it.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-09 (**Session close — six slices merged, the actionable tiers are empty, and one recorded item was withdrawn rather than built.** Shipped: multi-scatter energy compensation (#225), exposure control (#226), a 60s suite-wide ctest `TIMEOUT` (#227), viewport pointer input (#228), the UI pillar decomposition (#229), a fix for the flaky threaded-raycast test (#230), and a test-name lint (#231). **Both 🟢 quick wins and the sole 🟡 item are now struck**, so the next pick comes from the 🔴 tier — where the PBR row is complete and the UI row is decomposed into U6–U11. **What a worker should pick up, in order of how little it needs from a human:** U7 image presentation (aspect-preserving fit/fill, 9-slice, atlas sub-rects) is independent, cheap and needs no decision; U6 interaction depth is first in the decomposition's own ordering but carries an open question — whether `UiButton` gets its own `order` or inherits its `UiPanel` ancestor's, where the recommendation is inherit-with-override; U10 is blocked on an editor keyboard-input provider, which is itself a recorded near-one-line item. **Whether UI is a near-term goal at all is still unanswered and is not the decomposition's to answer.** **Two outstanding things that need a human, not a worker** — both since resolved; see the 2026-10-10 entry above. Visual acceptance of viewport pointer input was genuinely load-bearing rather than a formality: the branch review proved that feature was *shipped dead* while all thirteen of its tests were green, so a person at the window was the only instrument that could confirm it. And the exposure slice's visual change was unconfirmed at the time of writing. **The most transferable lesson of the session is about tests rather than about rendering, and it recurred in three different shapes.** A guard can be green and guard nothing: because its probe set was derived from the assertion list rather than from the code (multi-scatter, which left one statement unpinned); because a textual pin asserts that a line *exists* and never that it is *correct* (the pointer slice, whose pin asserted the presence of the very `WantCaptureMouse` call that made the feature unreachable); and because a test can assert a *race outcome* (the physics raycast case, which failed on a 2-core runner despite every claim it makes being met). Each was found by a different instrument — mutation probes derived from code, a fresh-context review, and `taskset -c 0` reproducing 9/15 failures on demand — and none of the three would have been found by reading. **A fourth shape, worth equal weight: the right answer is sometimes not to do the recorded work.** The backlog said nine test names were unrunnable because of commas; there are 89, `CatchAddTests.cmake` escapes them, and the defect was in the probe scripts' own unescaped invocation. Renaming 89 well-written names would have been churn against a non-problem, so the item is withdrawn with the evidence and only the two constraints that genuinely break ctest discovery got a lint.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-07 (**Studio — viewport pointer input shipped, play-mode only: the editor can finally click its own scene, and the three things that made this the backlog's only 🟡 were not the three things that turned out to be hard** — `mosaic editor` could select entities and edit components live, but a click in its viewport reached nothing: `setup_runtime`'s `pointer` parameter defaults to `{}` and `editor.cpp` never passed one, so `UiSystem` saw `PointerState{}` forever, and `ClickNudgeSystem` was never registered there at all — `register_windowed_systems` had exactly one caller, `main.cpp`. Manipulating a scene and exercising its scripts were two different programs. **The behaviour question the backlog flagged as blocking was answered first, as it should have been: play mode only.** A viewport click drives the game's pointer while playing and means "select this entity" while not. **Scoping then found the coordinate transform already existed** — `StudioApp::draw_viewport` has converted the mouse to panel-relative coordinates and raycast-picked on `IsItemClicked` since 37d, for edit-mode selection. That is what made play-mode-only cheap: the existing pick *is* the edit-mode behaviour and stays. But it had to be **gated on `!is_playing()`**, or one play-mode click both drives the pointer and moves the selection, so the Inspector jumps to whatever the user just poked. **That gate is a behaviour change to shipped code, not an addition:** clicking the viewport no longer selects while playing. Selection stays reachable from the Hierarchy panel, so nothing becomes unreachable. **The two findings scoping did not anticipate are the interesting ones.** `ClickNudgeSystem` fires on a press **edge** (`p.down && !was_down_`), so gating `down` on play mode *synthesises a click*: hold the left button in edit mode, press Play, and `down` flips false→true with no physical press, landing wherever the cursor happens to sit — and the same thing happens when a drag on an inspector slider is released over the viewport. `ViewportPanel` now latches "this press began while ineligible" and stays suppressed until the raw button goes up. And **the `aspect` provider must be the viewport panel's, not the window's**: the scene renders into a panel-sized offscreen target, so a window-derived aspect makes the nudge ray diverge from the image that was clicked — worse the further from centre, and wrong whenever the viewport is not the whole window, which is always. **The seam is `ViewportPanel`**, which was already headless, already unit-tested and already record-then-query for sizing; it now records the raw ImGui facts and computes the `PointerState`, so every suppression rule is tested with no window and no GPU. The one-frame latency is **inherited, not invented** — `on_frame` ticks the world before drawing the UI, the same ordering that already gives sizing its documented frame of lag. **A test-design subtlety worth carrying forward.** Testing each suppression condition by starting a press with that condition already unmet *cannot work*: the latch blocks such a press anyway, so dropping the condition from the gate leaves the test green. The conditions are tested as **press-then-change** instead — press legitimately, then remove one condition — which isolates the gate and happens to match the real scenarios (press in the viewport and drag off it; press and then hit Stop). The probes confirmed it: dropping any one of the three gate conditions turns that case red. **Eleven probes derived from the code, ten red.** The eleventh is recorded as a known gap rather than papered over: registering `ClickNudgeSystem` before `UiSystem` would break the UI-claim arbitration, and **no headless assertion in this slice can see it**, because registration order is a runtime property of `World`. Also honest about a limit the probes exposed: the end-to-end case that drives a real `UiButton` through a real `UiSystem` builds its *own* world, so it proves the mechanism works but **cannot** prove `editor.cpp` uses it — only the textual pin catches the provider being dropped. Neither alone is sufficient. **Two of my own test-authoring errors, from opposite directions, both caught by running them.** Searching raw source for `glfwGetCursorPos` matched the *comment explaining why it is not used* — exactly the trap `shader_source.hpp`'s `code_only` exists for, whose comment already said "a test that forbids documenting the decision it enforces is the wrong test" — and then, after stripping comments, a search for `/*windowed=*/true` could not match because stripping had removed it. Both pins are now comment-stripped and whitespace-normalised. **And the branch review found that the gate used the wrong ImGui signal, so the feature shipped DEAD — the single most valuable finding of the slice.** `down` required `hovered && !imgui_capturing`, with the second fed from `ImGui::GetIO().WantCaptureMouse`. That flag reads like "ImGui wants the mouse for a widget"; `imgui.cpp:5184` defines it as true whenever the cursor is over **any** ImGui window or any button is held. The Viewport panel *is* an ImGui window, and `IsItemHovered()` is false unless `g.HoveredWindow == window` (`imgui.cpp:4575`) — so the two conditions were **mutually exclusive** and `down` was unreachable. The latch had the identical defect, so fixing one would not have helped. **And because click-to-select is now gated on `!is_playing()`, the net effect was a regression rather than a no-op:** a play-mode viewport click did *nothing*, where before the slice it selected an entity. **Three things make this worth remembering.** No test of the shape chosen could ever have caught it — all thirteen feed the sample by hand, so the policy was right and thoroughly proven while its *input* was wrong. The textual pin made it worse by **asserting `WantCaptureMouse` was present**, cementing the bug; it is now inverted to *forbid* it, verified by reintroducing the call and watching the pin go red, and the general lesson is that a textual pin asserts a line exists and never that it is correct. And the fix **removes** the field rather than feeding it something else, because `IsItemHovered()` already encodes every case it was written for: another item active such as a slider drag elsewhere (`imgui.cpp:4581`, which excludes `MoveId`/`TabId` so a drag *inside* the viewport still counts), the viewport window behind another, and an open modal. Two further review findings fixed in the same pass: the end-to-end case asserted `press_consumed()` after one tick with `down` true, so the **release** edge that actually emits the event went untested — and its full-panel button made *every* position pass, so a provider returning (0,0) unconditionally would have been green; it now presses and releases, asserts the `ScriptEvent`, uses a centred sub-rect and has a negative-position case. And an undrawn Viewport panel (closed, collapsed, or an inactive dock tab) left the sample and the latch frozen, so draws resuming mid-press reused the previous press's verdict; all three paths now feed an empty sample, which makes the header's "sees every frame" claim true. Gate **1181/1181**; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation lines. **Visually confirmed 2026-10-10** by a human working a 23-item checklist at the window, and it is worth saying what that confirmed because no automated test could: the HUD button logs `hud_click` on a play-mode click; the sphere, both boxes and the capsule each take a visible impulse while the static ground does not; clicks over the Inspector or Hierarchy never reach the scene; pressing Stop restores click-to-select and silences the button; a press held through a mode change clicks nothing until a fresh press; and a slider drag released over the viewport does not shove anything. Exposure was confirmed in the same pass — 1.0 unchanged, 2.0 brightening geometry *and* sky together, the unlit overlays correctly not following, and a negative value rendering **black rather than white**, which is the ACES floor fix holding. **The acceptance pass also produced a design change, which is the useful kind of outcome:** because the editor starts in play mode, the default viewport click now drives the game rather than selecting, and the user's judgement is that an editor-facing product should start **stopped**. Recorded in the backlog with its one real trap — `mosaic editor --ticks N` would never terminate, since `FramePump` counts world ticks and a stopped loop never ticks. Deferred and recorded: keyboard input to the scene from the editor (the same shape of gap, deliberately not bundled, since it needs none of this slice's decisions), extra mouse buttons in `PointerState`, and an editor fly-camera — whose real cost is that it breaks the current property that the user's click ray and the game's camera ray are identical by construction.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-07 (**Rendering — exposure control shipped, and the backlog's own one-line description of it was wrong in the one way that mattered** — tone mapping had shipped with a hard-coded curve and no way to set the exposure feeding it. `Camera` gains `float exposure = 1.0f`, reflected, so it serialises with the scene, is per-camera, and arrives in the Studio inspector as a live `DragFloat` with no extra code; 1.0 renders byte-identically to before, which is the whole migration story. **The knob's location was the real work, exactly as the 2026-10-04 deferral predicted** — it went on `Camera` because exposure is a camera property physically and in every DCC tool. **But the backlog also said it was "one `float` through the existing lighting UBO (which already carries `camera_position`)", implying a free padding slot, and there is none:** IBL slice 4c took `camera_position.w` for its enable flag and left a comment warning that a future field claiming that slot would *silently disable image-based lighting*. This slice is that future field. It grew the UBO by a `vec4` instead, 192 → 208, and **appended it at the end rather than grouping it with `camera_position` where it belongs logically** — because `static_assert(sizeof(FrameLightingUbo) == 208)` checks the struct's **total size and never its field offsets**, so a mid-struct insertion updated on only one side of the C++/GLSL boundary would rebind every later member with the assertion still green, surfacing as a wrong shadow matrix rather than a failed build. A new contract case pins the GLSL block's member **order**, with its own honest limit recorded: it pins the GLSL side only, since the C++ struct is file-local to a `.cpp` in the backend and no test can include it. **The skybox needed a second route.** `skybox.frag` does not bind the per-frame UBO at all — its set 0 is the environment cubemap — so exposure reaches it through `SkyboxParams` and a push constant (72 → 76), and a test asserts the two routes carry *the same value* rather than each merely being present; drift there reads as "the sky doesn't match the scene", an authoring complaint rather than a bug report. Exposure applies **exactly where tone mapping applies**, which is not a coincidence to tidy up later: a shader with no curve has nothing to scale. The four unlit scene shaders stay untouched, recorded with the genuinely open question of whether `world_text` is UI or scene. **Eleven mutation probes, derived from the CODE and not from the assertion list** — the lesson the multi-scatter review produced, applied on the first slice after learning it, and it paid immediately: **three probes are caught only by tests that did not exist when the probe list was written.** Dropping `sky.exposure`, and replacing either GPU-payload copy with a literal, all passed the entire suite until two new cases were added for them. **Two defects in this slice's own work, both found by the probes rather than by reading.** Two new test names contained **commas**, which Catch2 treats as test-spec separators — so running either by name matched nothing and exited non-zero, producing two false-red columns across all eleven probes, the same trap that had already cost a full probe round on the previous slice. Renamed — though **this diagnosis was itself half wrong, corrected 2026-10-09**: the same sweep reported "nine pre-existing comma'd names" and the backlog recorded them as unrunnable. There are **89**, and they are **not broken** — `CatchAddTests.cmake` escapes commas when generating each `add_test`, so every one runs correctly under `ctest`. The defect was always in the probe scripts' *unescaped* per-name invocation, not in the test names; the entry is withdrawn and the surviving real constraints (a leading `--`, an unbalanced `[`) now have a lint instead. And the mid-block UBO probe inserted a *duplicate* member instead of moving one, so glslc rejected it and the probe tested nothing; corrected, it is now red on both the member-order pin and the vert/frag match test. A pre-existing guard also did real work: the case pinning that `cube.vert` and `cube.frag` declare matching `FrameLighting` blocks caught the first version of this change, which added the member to the fragment shader alone. Gate **1165/1165**; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with **zero Vulkan validation errors**, which is meaningful evidence here rather than a formality — a std140 mismatch between the grown buffer and the shader's declared block is exactly the kind of thing validation reports. Deferred and recorded: exposure on the four unlit shaders, EV-stop authoring units with a UI slider, and auto-exposure, which depends on the still-deferred offscreen HDR target.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-06 (**Rendering — multi-scatter energy compensation shipped: the PBR arc's one recorded correctness gap is closed, and the test the slice existed to satisfy could not validate the constant the slice introduced** — single-scatter GGX drops the light that would have bounced between microfacets, and IBL slice 4c's furnace test had measured the loss precisely: a white surface in a uniform environment returned **0.786** at roughness 0.508 and **0.3085** at roughness 1. The Fdez-Agüera analytic fit puts it back: **1.000000 at every one of the 4096 LUT texels.** `energy_compensate` in `app/asset/ibl_bake.cpp` carries the math so CI can execute it with no GPU, mirrored in `cube.frag`, and the whole slice cost little to scope **because 4c had chosen to pin the measured shortfall with its reason rather than widen a tolerance until it hid** — the number to beat was already written down, which is what that decision was for. **The BRDF LUT is untouched**; the correction is applied on top at reconstruction time, because it depends on the per-material `F0` while the table is indexed by `(NdotV, roughness)` alone — so baking it in is impossible, not merely undesirable. That also means the single-scatter baseline case was **kept rather than rewritten**, and the before and after both stand in the suite. **The headline finding is about the test, and it was predicted while scoping rather than found in review.** At `F0 = 1` the compensated total cancels to `Ess + (1 − Ess)` *algebraically*, so the furnace returns exactly 1.0 for **any** value of `Favg` — `/21`, `/5` and a bare `1.0` are indistinguishable to it. Dropping to a dielectric does not help: `kD` is *defined* as the energy residual, so the three terms sum to 1 for any `Favg` whatsoever at albedo 1. Energy conservation is tautological here and cannot be the test. The cover is to ask for `FmsEms` **in isolation** — `diffuse_color = 0` makes `kD` vanish, so the returned diffuse weight *is* `FmsEms`, needing no extra field on the struct — pinned against independently computed references where a bare `Favg = 1` is **15× to 36×** out. That is the sixth instance in this arc of a guard that guards nothing; the only new thing is that it was anticipated. **Twelve mutation probes, twelve reds — and the branch review then found a hole all twelve were structurally incapable of seeing.** The GLSL contract pinned five of the six new statements in `cube.frag` and omitted the one that *computes* `FmsEms`: the multiple-scattering series sum, which is the entire formula the slice adds. `vec3 FmsEms = vec3(0.0);` keeps the identifier declared, leaves both pinned expressions textually intact, and turns `Favg` and `Ems` into unused locals that glslc accepts without a diagnostic — **reproduced, with all 1158 tests green and the correction fully disabled in the shipped renderer.** Since `energy_compensate` has no production caller, that contract file is the only thing standing behind the shader. **The reason the probes could not find it is the lesson:** the five GLSL probes were derived *from the assertion list*, so they could only ever test statements something already asserted. A probe set built that way measures how well the existing assertions resist mutation, never whether the assertions cover the code — so it comes back 5/5 red and tells you nothing about the gap. Probes need to be derived from the *code*, not from the tests. Also tightened: the `Favg` margin was 5e-5, which admitted a `/20` divisor (`FmsEms` drifts only 1.9e-5 to 4.4e-5) — a case whose stated purpose is pinning that constant was resolving it to ±1 in the divisor. Now 5e-6, with the derivation in the test; the floor is the **2.6e-6** gap between the independent reference integral and the real bake at the extreme corner texel, which is a limit of the reference rather than of the code, and is why a first attempt at 2e-6 came back red on correct code. **Two of the plan's own predictions were also wrong.** The plan claimed that moving `FmsEms` from the irradiance group onto the prefiltered radiance would produce **no red anywhere in C++**, and built the shader contract test's rationale on it. Three C++ cases catch it: the plan's reasoning — a uniform environment makes the irradiance and prefiltered maps identical — holds for an assertion on the **sum** of the two weights and fails for one on the **parts**, and those cases assert the parts. The contract test still earns its place by pinning the GLSL independently of a twin CI cannot execute, but it is not the lone guard it was advertised as. Likewise `FssEss → Ess` was predicted to fail the `Favg` case and does not, since at `f0 = 0.04` with `diffuse_color = 0` the diffuse weight is pure `FmsEms`. **Two smaller traps are worth knowing.** `(1.0 - metallic)` had to stay, moved onto `diffuseColor`: it is tempting to let the energy residual handle metals alone, since `F0 = albedo` makes `kD` zero for free — but only where `albedo` is **1**, so a dark conductor (`albedo = 0.5`) would grow a diffuse lobe. And **the handoff this slice started from quoted the pre-4c buggy curve** — 0.896 at roughness 0.5 and 0.656 at 0.76 are what the wrong `k = alpha^2/2` remap produced, not the corrected integral — while `ibl_bake.hpp`'s own header comment was still documenting that bug *as the design*, against an implementation whose comment explains at length why it was wrong. Both corrected here. **Also learned the hard way:** a comma in a Catch2 `TEST_CASE` name makes the filter split on it, match nothing, and exit non-zero — indistinguishable from a failing test, and it cost a full round of seven mutation probes. Two case names lost their commas. Gate **1158/1158** (1151 before this slice), 23.4s, no exclusions; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation errors and "image-based lighting active (5 prefiltered levels)" confirmed. **And the slice's own stated visual expectation was wrong, found by computing the magnitudes rather than by looking.** Every doc here, inherited from the backlog's original wording, said "a rough *dielectric* comes out darker than it should" and named the demo sphere's rough bands as where to see it — while the demo sphere is a **metal**, so the sentence contradicted itself. **The energy loss scales with the specular weight, which scales with `F0`:** a dielectric has `F0 = 0.04`, so specular is ~4% of its response and the diffuse lobe, which never lost energy, carries the other 96%. Measured against the demo scene, the ground, both boxes and the capsule change by **0.998× to 1.022×** — invisible — while the metal sphere's red channel gains **1.47× at roughness 0.75 and 1.95× at roughness 1**. The furnace test's dramatic 0.31 is measured at `F0 = 1`, which is a metal. **So the visible result is the one prior slices kept apologising for:** the metal sphere's rough bands go from near-black to a visible dark red glow, because `FmsEms` rides the irradiance and reaches conductors, which had received *zero* ambient before (`kD` is exactly 0 at `metallic = 1`). The glow is strongly red rather than grey, since multi-bounce energy is tinted by `F0` repeatedly — red gains 0.257 against green's 0.011. Smooth surfaces of any kind are unchanged, `Ems = 0` at roughness 0 making the correction identically zero, so a reviewer looking at the smooth end will correctly see nothing. **Visually confirmed 2026-10-06** by a human running `mosaic editor` against an eight-item checklist: the rough bands glowing red rather than near-black, the mirror band unchanged, the gradient free of new seams, the dielectrics unchanged, the skybox unchanged, and no NaN/black pixels at silhouette edges (the one place `cube.frag` divides without a guard). `demo_scene.hpp`'s "EXPECTED, NOT A BUG: this sphere reads near-BLACK" block was corrected too — it had been stale since 4b/4c (it still described the flat `0.1` ambient and told the reader IBL was what would eventually help) and this slice makes it wrong in the other direction as well. Deferred and recorded: the same compensation for the **direct** lighting path, which the furnace test structurally cannot measure because `brdf()` is shader code — it depends on either a C++ mirror of `brdf()` or GPU-capable CI.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-06 (**Rendering — IBL slice 4c shipped: the PBR arc is complete, and the furnace test it unlocked immediately found a Critical bug in its own BRDF table** — the flat `0.1` ambient is replaced by a diffuse irradiance lookup and a specular prefiltered-environment lookup combined through the split-sum approximation. Both environment products are convolved **on the CPU at import**, the same decision 4b made for equirect-to-cube and for the same reason: there is no GPU in CI, so shader-side integrals could not be checked at all. **That decision is what made the furnace test possible, and the furnace test is the point of the slice.** PBR slice 2's spec recorded as its honest limit that *no test there could show the BRDF is correct, only that it is wired* — and named the furnace test as what would. It now exists: a white surface in a uniform environment must return exactly the light it receives, and it runs in CI because the integrals are C++. **It found the known limitation, quantified:** single-scatter GGX returns **0.896** at roughness 0.5, **0.656** at 0.76 and **0.31** at roughness 1 — losing about two thirds at the top end. Those numbers are pinned with their reason rather than hidden behind a widened tolerance, so when multi-scatter compensation lands the improvement is checkable against a recorded baseline; the backlog entry points at them. **And the branch review found that the BRDF table itself was wrong.** The Smith geometry remap was `k = alpha^2/2` where it must be `alpha/2` — the reference writes `float a = roughness; k = (a*a)/2`, whose `a` is *roughness*, while this code's was already alpha, so it squared twice. **The more instructive half is that the symptom had already been seen and mis-explained.** A 4.1x energy overshoot at grazing angles was diagnosed as sampling divergence (g_vis divides by NdotV) and renormalised away. It converges — 4.16x at 262,144 samples, verified independently before acting. The renormalisation was masking a real integration error, and in masking it clamped **21% of the table flat**: a hard white rim on every silhouette, identical at every roughness, where true values span 0.60-0.89. With the correct remap the grid maximum is exactly 1.0, reached only at roughness 0 where it is analytically right, so the renormalisation was deleted rather than kept. **Worse, the test added to catch exactly this was pinning the wrong value with its labels inverted** — fixing the code would have produced a red test blaming the fix. **The recurring lesson got its sharpest instance yet.** Across this slice six separate guards turned out to guard nothing, each found by mutation rather than by reading: the prefilter tests passed with NO BLUR AT ALL; the wrong geometry remap passed because every assertion sat at roughness 0 and 1, where the two remaps coincide; pinning the split-sum reconstruction did not pin the LUT lookup, so a constant passed while discarding the entire energy correction; the production level count had nothing tying it to the shader's `roughness * 4.0`; and `R = reflect(-v, n)` was unpinned, so `R = n` — the sphere reflecting whatever is directly overhead — left every test green. Also fixed: the LUT was sampled with `texture()`, choosing an implicit LOD from screen-space derivatives of TABLE coordinates, which put a visible seam at every roughness-band boundary on the demo sphere; a cooked-blob overflow guard that bounded `px*bpp` but let the callers multiply by six before it; and an irradiance normalisation using the nominal step measure rather than the actual one, a 1.85% error that had consumed 93% of the furnace tolerance *which was itself the stated justification for choosing that step*. **Performance mattered more than expected:** the demo bakes at startup because it has no import step, and five test files call `setup_runtime` with ctest giving each case its own process — an eager bake added minutes to the suite. A lazy loader fixed it; the suite is 22.7s. A bright CC0 environment (kloofendal, Poly Haven) was added alongside the space one, because space has 0.2% mean radiance and cannot show that any of this works. Gate **1151/1151**; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation errors and "image-based lighting active (5 prefiltered levels)" confirmed. **The PBR arc is now complete:** tone mapping, metallic/roughness with a GGX Cook-Torrance BRDF, texture maps with real image import, mipmaps, cubemaps with an HDR path and a skybox, and image-based lighting. Deferred and recorded: multi-scatter energy compensation — now with a measured number to beat — specular occlusion, parallax-corrected probes, environment rotation, and a runtime quality knob.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-05 (**Rendering — IBL slices 4a and 4b shipped: mipmaps, cubemaps, a real HDR path and a skybox — and the slice's own orientation tests turned out to constrain nothing** — the 🔴 IBL item was **decomposed** on 2026-10-05, for the same reason full PBR was: scoping measured **four missing capabilities stacked underneath it**, not one feature. There were no cubemaps, no compute shaders, **no mipmaps** — and prefiltered specular *is* a mip chain, so that was a hard prerequisite — and HDR decoding was switched off. So: **4a** mipmaps, **4b** cubemaps plus the HDR path, **4c** the IBL math. **4a** gives every texture a full chain via a `vkCmdBlitImage` ladder, gated on the format advertising the three features the blit actually requires — the first version checked only one, and a driver advertising the filter but not `BLIT_DST` for the sRGB variant would have made every blit an invalid call. It also fixes the distance aliasing PBR slice 3 introduced. **4b** adds `ImageFormat{Rgba8, Rgba16F}` to `Image` (byte storage, only the stride changes), loads Radiance HDR through stb into half-floats, converts equirectangular to six cube faces **on the CPU at import** — chosen specifically so the direction math is testable, since CI has no GPU — and draws the result as a tone-mapped skybox. **The skybox invalidates part of slice 1's reasoning:** that slice tone-mapped `cube.frag` alone, explicitly because every other colour-producing shader is unlit and cannot exceed 1.0. A skybox sampling HDR radiance can, so `tonemap_aces` is duplicated with a contract test holding the copies identical. **Three findings are worth carrying forward.** The branch review's Critical: `draw_skybox` had neither the render-pass nor the skipped-frame guard that **all nine** sibling recording methods have — resize the window, `begin_frame` returns before `vkBeginCommandBuffer`, and four commands get recorded into a non-recording command buffer. Undefined behaviour, divergent by driver, and invisible to every validation run because none of them resized. **An imported `.hdr` produced an asset nothing could load:** `load_project_assets` had no `cubemap` branch, so it looked for a `.data` file import never wrote, and `uncook_cubemap` had no caller outside tests — the import half shipped without the load half. **And none of the orientation tests constrained orientation:** every one evaluated at the face *centre*, where both in-face coordinates are zero, so only the major axis was exercised — rotating a face 180° or mirroring it left the whole suite green. That was the slice's stated top risk and the entire reason for converting on the CPU. Corner assertions now pin both in-face signs for all six faces. **A pre-existing test caught a regression no new test would have:** rewriting `uncook_image`'s size check for the format tag dropped its overflow guard, so `w = h = 0x80000000` wraps the product to zero and a malformed blob is accepted. Gate **1132/1132**, no exclusions; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation errors; sky confirmed visually. **Expectation to set for 4c:** the committed environment is *space* — 0.2% mean radiance, 187 of 100 million samples above 1.0 — so it will produce almost no ambient light and the metal sphere will stay near-black. That is correct physics for a chrome ball in a void, not a failed slice; a bright environment is the right choice for verifying 4c. Deferred and recorded: anisotropic filtering (needs a device feature at logical-device creation), sRGB mip generation through blits that some drivers filter in the wrong space, mip chains on the environment cube, cubemap compression, runtime environment switching, `.exr`, captured reflection probes, a GLSL include mechanism, and a runtime-selectable face size.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-04 (**Rendering — PBR slice 3 shipped: per-texel metallic/roughness and real image import — and three of the slice's own guard tests turned out to guard nothing** — `Material` gains `metallic_roughness_texture`, `cube.frag` samples it at set 0 binding 1 with glTF 2.0 packing (G = roughness, B = metallic) as **factor x texel**, which is what keeps every existing scene rendering identically: an untextured material binds a 1x1 white map and multiplies by 1. **Scoping corrected a claim this project had made about itself.** The engine was said to have no image file loading at all; it was wrong. `asset_io` already dispatched by extension, `read_ppm` already decoded binary PPM, `cook_image`/`uncook_image` already implemented the `MOSAIMG1` cooked form, and `AssetRegistry` already resolved an `AssetID` to an `Image`. The only gap was that the one source format was PPM, which nothing exports — so the slice was far smaller than first framed, and the spec records the correction rather than letting it propagate to slices 4+. `stb_image.h` drops in beside the `stb_truetype.h` that was already vendored. **The colour-space trap is the thing to remember.** `create_texture` hardcoded `R8G8B8A8_SRGB`; a metallic/roughness map is *data*, so uploaded as sRGB an authored 0.5 arrives as ~0.21 — no crash, no validation error, just a different material. It now takes a `TextureColorSpace`, defaulted to `Srgb` so every pre-existing call site stays correct. **The plan was wrong about descriptors, and following it would have broken four pipelines.** It said to add binding 1 to `texture_set_layout_`; that layout was shared by **five** pipeline layouts, and the other four allocate sets writing only binding 0, so they would no longer have matched the layout they came from. The lit pass got its own two-binding `material_set_layout_` instead. **Then the branch review found six Important problems, four of which were green-and-wrong.** `read_ppm` was **unreachable** — stb decodes P5/P6 too, so the test asserting "the fallback works" ran entirely through stb, and since stb accepts any maxval while copying bytes unscaled, a legal `P6 2 1 15` that used to fail loudly imported as a near-black texture. `read_image` could throw `bad_alloc` from a decompression bomb, out of a function documented never to throw. The **headline** colour-space contract test guarded neither half of what it claimed: asserting `TextureColorSpace{} == Srgb` tests only which enumerator is written first, and four independent token searches were all satisfied by **swapping the ternary arms** — which ships every roughness map as sRGB and every base colour as UNORM, the exact failure the file exists to prevent. And the demo **could not show what the acceptance note told a human to look for**: the 0.174 scalar multiplied the authored range into 0.014..0.150 against cube.frag's 0.045 floor, so two bands collapsed and the sphere showed five, all near-mirror. **Writing the fixes reproduced the lesson a third time:** the first decompression-bomb test passed whether or not the ceiling existed, because a header-only PNG fails at decode first — so the size policy was extracted into a pure predicate and tested directly. Five mutation probes, five reds. Gate **1105/1105**, no exclusions; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation errors. Deferred and recorded: normal mapping with the tangent-frame decision it forces, occlusion/emissive maps, bindless textures with their trigger, mipmaps (which this slice makes *visibly* wanted — a varying roughness map aliases at distance), texture compression, UV transforms, and the demo's cwd-relative asset paths.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-04 (**Rendering — PBR slice 2 shipped: metallic/roughness and a GGX Cook-Torrance BRDF — and the spec confidently named the wrong scene file** — `Material` loses `shininess`/`specular_strength` and gains `metallic`/`roughness`; `cube.frag` gets GGX/Trowbridge-Reitz **D**, Smith–Schlick **G** with the direct-lighting remap `k = α/2`, and Schlick **F** with `F0 = mix(vec3(0.04), base_color, metallic)`. `kD = (1 − F)(1 − metallic)` is the one token that separates metal from shiny plastic, and a test pins it. `base_color` gains a second role — albedo for dielectrics, specular colour for metals — which is why the metallic/roughness workflow calls it *base* colour. **The π is the cost, and it was paid rather than hidden.** Energy-conserving diffuse is `albedo/π`, which darkens every lit surface ~3.14×; the alternative was to skip the division and document that intensity absorbs it, which would have left diffuse π× too strong *relative to specular* — and that ratio is the entire thing energy conservation buys. So every light intensity was multiplied by π instead. **The spec, the plan, and the backlog all named `scene.json` as 'the one committed scene'. It is gitignored** — a local export — so the plan's `git add scene.json` would have failed, and the real committed scene, `templates/default/scenes/main.json`, holds **two** lights to re-author rather than one. Following the plan literally would have left the only scene users actually load rendering ~3× too dark **with a fully green suite**, because no test reads that file's light values. **The branch review then found the reference docs had been lying for two slices.** `.githooks/pre-commit` picked the *first* `mosaic` binary it found, so a **Sep 30** `build/app/mosaic` outranked a fresh `build/debug/app/mosaic` and regenerated `docs/reference/components.md` from pre-rename reflection data — passing the staleness check while committing a reference that documented deleted fields. Regenerating with the current binary also recovered the `apply_impulse` and `raycast` docstring updates from the two *previous* slices, which the same stale binary had been silently suppressing. The hook now takes the newest candidate and warns when it predates staged source. **Three guard tests were pinning the wrong thing, and the mutation probes proved it.** A case titled 'guards the specular denominator against grazing angles' asserted only that `max(dot(n, v), 1e-4)` *appears* — rewriting the divide as `max(4.0 * dot(n, v) * dot(n, L), 1e-7)` leaves the clamped locals defined and used by G, keeps every string present, and reinstates the divide-by-~0 at every silhouette edge with the test green. Likewise nothing tied the clamped roughness to the value actually squared. And the tone-map write counter, already hardened against `outColor.rgb = …`, was still evaded by `outColor[0] = …` — the same defect one character along. Four probes, four reds, baseline green. The two contract files now share one helper header, because they had each carried their own copy and **drifted**: one learned to strip comments before searching and the other did not. **Two comments had inverted into instructions to treat correct output as a bug.** The slice-2 spec devoted a whole section to the Blinn-Phong rule 'specular takes the LIGHT's color, never the albedo' so nobody would reinstate it as a fix — and it survived verbatim above the red sphere, the one object now `metallic = 1`, where `F0 = base_color` means its highlight **must** read red. **And the mechanical conversion quietly cost the scene a surface:** `sqrt(2/(shininess+2))` mapped the deliberate matte box and the defaults box onto the same `0.243`, leaving two of six demo materials byte-identical and the matte baseline gone, with every assertion still passing. The matte box is now `0.9` — the conversion exists to land a surface in the right perceptual *neighbourhood*, not to preserve appearance, and that one's neighbourhood is 'matte' — and the test asserts no two surfaces match. Gate **1089/1089**, no exclusions; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation errors. **The honest limit, restated:** no test here can show the BRDF is *correct*, only that it is wired, guarded, and round-trips. The real check is a furnace test, which needs IBL — slice 4. Until then correctness rests on the formulae matching their published forms and on a human looking at the window. Deferred and recorded: slices 3 (texture maps) and 4 (IBL); the light components' defaults still meaning the pre-π thing, so a user's fresh 'unit' light is now ~3.14× dimmer than the same light was last week; and `metallic` outside `0..1` flipping `kD`'s sign twice to hand a 'more than metal' surface its diffuse lobe back. **Visually confirmed 2026-10-04, including one result worth knowing before it is filed as a bug:** the metal sphere reads near-**black**, not dark red. Its highlights are correctly *red* -- which is the proof `F0 = base_color` works -- but metals have no diffuse lobe, and the ambient is now `kD`-weighted with `kD = (1 - F)(1 - metallic)` exactly zero at `metallic == 1`, so it is the one surface receiving no ambient at all. A mirror-ish ball in a void is a black ball with highlights. IBL (slice 4) is what gives it something to reflect; the flat `0.1` ambient cannot help a metal by construction.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-04 (**Rendering — the 🔴 PBR item is decomposed, and slice 1 (tone mapping) shipped** — the backlog said full PBR "likely [needs] its own sub-decomposition, not a single slice", so it got one: tone mapping, then metallic/roughness with a GGX Cook-Torrance BRDF, then texture maps, then IBL. **The ordering claim is the interesting part and it is argued, not assumed.** Tone mapping goes *first* because an energy-conserving BRDF produces values above 1.0 routinely and the 8-bit sRGB target clips them — so shipping the BRDF first would have made the renderer look **worse** than it did before and left it that way until tone mapping landed. It also pays for itself immediately, since specular already clipped. Slice 2 absorbs two stranded backlog items rather than leaving them: the "perceptual `0..1` gloss remap" *dissolves* (roughness **is** that parameter, which is exactly why the remap was deferred to "belong with" it), and Fresnel/energy conservation was already recorded as folded into PBR. Migration for slice 2 is settled as a clean break, cost stated. **Four things were measured while scoping, each of which changed the design.** The colour pipeline is *already linear* — `B8G8R8A8_SRGB` swapchain, `R8G8B8A8_SRGB` textures, so the hardware converts at both ends; a manual `pow(c, 1/2.2)` would **double-correct**, and a test now forbids one. `cube.frag` is the only one of **nine** colour-producing fragment shaders whose output can exceed 1.0 — the other scene shaders are unlit, and since ACES maps 1.0 to about 0.8, mapping them would *darken* authored colour. **Nothing blends additively** (every pipeline is `SRC_ALPHA`, with `VK_BLEND_FACTOR_ONE` only on alpha), which is what makes in-shader tone mapping defensible instead of requiring an offscreen HDR target — the real objection, `tonemap(a)+tonemap(b) != tonemap(a+b)`, is about additive compositing. And `scene.json` plus four test files reference the fields slice 2 removes, which is why migration needed a decision rather than an assumption. **The sharpest test is not the obvious one.** "Is `tonemap_aces` applied?" is insufficient: a second `outColor` write below the tone map bypasses it while that check stays green, because the function is still defined and still called. So the contract asserts `outColor` is written **exactly once** and from the mapped value. Mutation A proved the asymmetry — restoring the unmapped write failed *only* the write-count case — and Mutation B (a stray second write) failed `2 == 1` while compiling cleanly, which is the whole case for a textual test where there is no GPU in CI. **One test defect, found by the test failing on correct code.** The gamma check greps for `2.2`, and it tripped on `cube.frag`'s own comment explaining that a `pow(c, 1.0/2.2)` would double-correct — it caught *prose*, not code. Fixed the test, not the comment, by stripping `//` comments before searching: a test that forbids documenting the decision it enforces is the wrong test, and rewording would have left the trap armed for the next person who explains the same thing. **Visual verification was a human step and is recorded as one.** The four contract tests pin wiring and cannot see a pixel; the user confirmed highlights roll off with no wash-out. Gate **1087/1087**; both `--window` entry points clean. Deferred and recorded: the offscreen HDR target plus resolve pass, with a *specific* trigger rather than a vague one — the moment any additive pipeline is added, the reasoning above is invalidated; plus bloom (which depends on it) and exposure control.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-04 (**Scripting — a wrong-shaped argument now degrades instead of killing the tick, and two carried backlog claims turned out to be false** — two small slices, both finishing surfaces this arc had been circling. **The binding signature was the bug.** The spec's "a malformed argument returns `False`" only ever covered malformations *within* a sequence; a non-sequence never reached the guard at all, because `py::sequence` in the signature made pybind11's **dispatcher** reject the call before the body ran, so the `TypeError` aborted the script's whole `on_update`. A dict is the likeliest wrong argument and an understandable one: component values on this very surface ARE dicts (`tr['position']['x']`), so reaching for `{'x':..,'y':..,'z':..}` is a reasonable wrong guess. `to_vec3` now takes a `py::handle` and checks sequence-ness itself; both bindings take `py::object`. **Taken wider than the backlog scoped it, on purpose** — the entry named `apply_impulse` only, but #214 had just made `raycast` degrade for within-sequence malformations, so fixing one would have left a dict returning `False` from one binding and raising from the other: a *new* inconsistency rather than a closed one. One imprecision is documented rather than changed: `apply_impulse` under `--script-thread` returns `True` meaning "accepted for replay", not "a body was pushed", because the worker can only judge entities in its own job snapshot — a deliberate 2026-09-30 call that was simply missing from the docstring. And `str` passes `PySequence_Check`, so a string falls through to the element casts and fails a layer deeper, now pinned so both layers stay honest. **The other slice's value was negative findings.** Five `ScriptWorker` tidy-ups had been carried since 2026-09-30; checked against the code, **two were wrong**. "`host_` is write-only" is false (it is read at `script_worker.cpp:19`), and "`execute()` clears `pending_impulses_` after moving it, a no-op" is false — a moved-from vector is valid but *unspecified*, so the clear guarantees empty rather than trusting an implementation detail. Of the two that were right, `has_pending()` had zero callers and is gone, and `dispatch()`'s unconditional `result_ready_` clear is now documented with **measurements instead of a warning**: the drop is reachable only by a race, measured at **9 passes / 11 failures over 20 runs** (so a test would be flaky, not load-bearing), and **removing the clear leaves the whole suite green**, so nothing pins it today. The clear stays, because without it a `collect()` after two dispatches returns the *stale* result while the newer job is still pending — worse than losing the older one. **Also recorded as a negative result:** `PYBIND11_SIMPLE_GIL_MANAGEMENT` does **not** fix the worker's `PyThreadState` churn. I recommended it as a one-line fix on the premise that `PyGILState_Ensure`/`Release` is cached per thread; tried it with the macro verified present in the test TU by an `#error` probe, and the test stayed red. The caching applies only to *nested* `Ensure` calls — the outermost `Release` still tears the state down under both pybind11 implementations. Nesting is what works, so the neutral `ScriptEngine` batch hook is required after all. That investigation also closed #215's open `gilstate_counter` question: the main thread's thread dict survives an acquire/release pair, so its counter is >=1 and the quiet-churn branch does not occur. **The throughline across three of these: a recorded finding is a hypothesis until someone re-checks it against the code.** The `[script]` "flake" was a real bug, #212's end-touch mechanism was wrong in four places, my `atexit` ordering assumption was backwards, and this backlog entry carried two false claims for five days. Gate **1083/1083**; both `--window` entry points clean.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-03 (**Scripting — embedded CPython finally has a GIL discipline, and the nine-day "flake" was written in our own comment** — `mosaic_unit_tests "[script]"` segfaulted 3/3 on main. #208 recorded the mode as unexplained and "has not reproduced since"; it reproduced on the first attempt. The cause was sitting in `ensure_interpreter`'s own docstring: *"After init, the calling (main) thread holds the GIL ... no explicit GIL handoff."* So whoever won the `std::call_once` race kept the GIL for the life of the process. Run the threaded script test **alone** and the `ScriptWorker` thread won it, held the GIL, and passed. Run it after *any* other engine had been created on the main thread and the worker called `PyDict_New()` with no GIL and died. **And `ctest` structurally could not see it:** `catch_discover_tests` gives every `TEST_CASE` its own process, so the worker was always first — the gate was green and blind at the same time, which is the part worth remembering. The invariant is now real: no thread owns the GIL implicitly, every entry into CPython acquires. **Four things were not obvious going in, and each cost a failing-gate iteration.** (1) `globals_` had to become `std::optional<py::dict>` in both engines — not a style choice but an ordering one: a `py::dict` *member* is constructed before the constructor body and destroyed after the destructor body, so there is no scope in which to hold the GIL for `PyDict_New`, the exact call that crashed. (2) `restricted_builtins`' function-local static `ProxyClasses` had to start leaking deliberately, because `__cxa_atexit` destroys it with no GIL and `dec_ref` aborts (a *debug*-only symptom -- in Release the same path is a silent unguarded `Py_XDECREF`, so the leak is **more** necessary there, which review caught) — and my first attempt at this, an `atexit` handler that re-acquires, was **backwards**: function-local statics register at first use, so they tear down *before* a handler registered earlier. (3) `trace_fn_` had to be emptied inside the guarded destructor, since a `py::` member outlives the body; missing it aborted only once a debugger had been attached. (4) Every direct pybind11 user outside the engines — the docs generator and four test files that build their own `py::dict` — had to acquire too. **Two process notes.** I reported "blast radius exceeds the approved scope" off a count of 101 failing tests without diagnosing; 67 of them were **one missing guard** on `bind_world`, which my method enumeration had skipped because its parameters wrap onto a continuation line and my regex only matched single-line signatures. Counting is not diagnosing. And the fix genuinely could not be confined to `script_python` as first scoped — releasing the GIL changes the contract for every direct CPython user, and some construct `py::dict` themselves, so no work inside the module could cover them; the scope was re-decided explicitly rather than drifted into. Evidence: `[script]` in one process is **5/5 clean** where it was RC=139 every time; the **whole binary now runs in a single process** — 1081 cases, 31577 assertions — which the crash made impossible; and removing the release makes the new tests **deadlock (RC=124)** rather than crash, because the worker blocks on a GIL the main thread never yields. Gate **1081/1081**; both `--window` entry points exit 0 clean. Deferred and recorded: nothing *enforces* the new rule — a new entry point, `py::` member, static `py::object`, or direct pybind11 caller will compile and then fail at runtime, and the real answer is to stop exposing raw `py::` types across the module boundary at all.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-03 (**Scripting — `raycast` degrades instead of raising; and the `[script]` SIGSEGV turns out to be one command away** — `raycast`'s binding read `origin[0..2]`/`direction[0..2]` with no length or type check, so a script passing a two-element list, or a string where a number belongs, raised out of pybind11 and — uncaught — aborted the whole tick over one bad call. `apply_impulse` has routed its reads through a `to_vec3` helper since #207; `raycast` simply never got the same treatment, and the gap sat recorded since 2026-09-29. It now uses the same helper, so malformed input returns **exactly the miss dict the call already returns when no host callback is set** — no second failure shape invented. The host is deliberately not called at all, because zero-filling the missing components and casting anyway would hand the simulation a ray the script never asked for; the tests pin that with a call counter. On the behaviour change the backlog was wary of: what goes away is an **undocumented** exception. `ScriptHost` documents raycast as an always-miss when unset, `apply_impulse` documents returning `False` for malformed arguments, and `raycast`'s own `py::doc` promised only "hit data as a dictionary" — it never promised to raise. The doc string now states the rule. Each of the three new sections asserts both that the call reports a miss **and that the script keeps running afterwards**, which is the real property: a raising binding fails the run regardless of what the dict would have said. All three fail under the mutation that restores unguarded indexing. **The more valuable find was incidental.** Running `[script]` after the change segfaulted, and rather than assume the known flake I bisected it: reverting the engine change left it failing, reverting the new tests too left it failing, and **pristine `main` segfaults 3/3** with `./build/debug/tests/mosaic_unit_tests "[script]"`. #208 recorded this mode as unexplained and "has not reproduced since"; it reproduces deterministically, one command, no local changes. The failing case — `script_system_test.cpp:195`, "threaded ScriptSystem applies script writes one tick later" — **passes in isolation**, so it is the in-process ordering that kills it, which matches #208's interpreter-lifetime guess. And the reason CI has stayed green throughout: `catch_discover_tests` registers every `TEST_CASE` as its own ctest test, so each runs in a fresh process and the triggering ordering never happens. The gate reporting **1079/1079** is true and simultaneously blind to this. Recorded as its own slice rather than folded in — the cause is embedded-CPython lifetime across threads, the same machinery #208 spent nine days in.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-03 (**Tests — a tripped watchdog now fails instead of crashing, and the recorded hazard was understated** — `physics_contact_reentry_test.cpp`'s watchdog detaches a thread so a deadlocked tick can be reported as a named assertion rather than a 25-minute ctest timeout. But it captured the `World` by reference, so when the watchdog tripped, `REQUIRE` threw, the case unwound, and the thread was left holding dead stack. The dangerous mode is not the deadlock — a parked thread never touches the world again — it is the case the 3000 ms margin actually guards against: **a slow runner, no deadlock, thread still actively ticking a destroyed World.** Reproduced deliberately with a 50 ms watchdog against 4,000,000 ticks: **RC=134 (SIGABRT) and RC=139 (SIGSEGV)**, printing the assertion and *then* dying on a signal — so CI would have reported a crash in a physics test, with nothing pointing at a timeout. **The backlog entry I wrote for this understated it.** It proposed heap-allocating the `World` and leaking it, which would not have been enough: the subscriber lambdas also captured `handler_calls`, `hit_the_box`, `pushed` and `box` **by reference**, and those lambdas live inside the `World`'s own `EventBus` — so a surviving thread would write to dead stack through them regardless. The state had to move to the heap too. The fix is a `Probe` struct plus `std::shared_ptr<World>`, both captured by value in the tick thread. **Two trip kinds, two leak durations**, worth distinguishing because the docs first glossed them as one: a spurious slow-runner trip is self-healing (the thread finishes its ticks late and drops its references), while a genuine deadlock trip leaks for the rest of the test *process*, since Catch2 keeps running later cases after a failed `REQUIRE`. Both are bounded by process exit, and `backend_mutex_` is per-instance, so a leaked parked thread cannot block the next case. **One ordering detail is what makes it correct rather than merely different:** the EventBus holds a lambda that owns the `Probe`, so a `Probe` owning the `World` back would close a reference cycle and leak both on the *passing* path. `Probe` therefore holds nothing, and the handlers reach the `World` and `PhysicsSystem` through raw non-owning pointers — safe because a handler only runs inside a tick, and a tick only happens on the thread keeping the `World` alive. After: the same 50 ms/4M configuration gives **RC=1 five times out of five** with the clean named failure, and the real configuration is green 5/5. Gate: **1078/1078**, no `-E` exclusions.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-02 (**Physics — destroying an entity now destroys its body, and the bug was never a leak** — `PhysicsBackend` had no counterpart to `create_body` and `PhysicsSystem`'s three maps were insert-only, so a destroyed entity's Box3D body kept stepping forever. The framing that matters: **it kept colliding.** Destroy a crate and it still holds up whatever was stacked on it, with no mesh to explain why. Unbounded map growth was the lesser half. `update()` tolerated the situation only by accident — steps 2 and 4 `continue` on a null `get_component`, step 5a drops unmapped contacts — which is precisely why it survived the whole physics pillar unnoticed. **One predicate, three holes.** The reap is step 0 of `update()`, inside the existing lock, and asks whether each entry's `RigidBody` still exists rather than whether the entity is valid. That covers `destroy_entity` committing (`on_destroy` erases every component), **`World::clear()`** — which bypasses the deferred destroy queue *and runs no systems*, so nothing else could ever notice a scene reload — and removing `RigidBody` from a living entity, which `is_valid` would have missed entirely. `EntityID` is generational, so a recycled slot cannot alias a stale key. **The backend was the bulk of the work, not the interesting part.** `bodies_` is an insert-only vector with `handle == index + 1`, so erasing a slot would shift and invalidate every later handle; a destroyed slot is tombstoned with `b3_nullBodyId` instead. That forced all four handle-taking methods through one `live_body()` guard — three of them previously checked only the range, so they would have happily indexed a freed body. `b3Body_IsValid`'s source was read rather than assumed: it returns false for `index1 < 1`, so it is safe on the null id. **The mutation did exactly the job it exists for.** Drop the `destroy_body` call but keep every map erasure, and the call-counting tests fail *merely because the call is absent* — while the stacked-boxes test fails `1.99934 < 1.49934`, the upper box still propped at y≈2.0 by a ghost instead of fallen to y≈1.0. That asymmetry is the entire argument for writing a positional test rather than a call-counting one. A second probe killed the guard itself: removing `live_body`'s liveness check made the inert-methods case fail *and* then crashed the process with SIGILL on Box3D's own double-free assert. **And review caught me getting a causal story wrong — again, and worth recording.** I first reported that the Review Focus 4 guard — no `ContactEvent` may name a reaped entity — *cannot be made to fail*, having probed it by removing `handle_to_entity_.erase` from the reap and watched it still pass. The stated reason was that Box3D emits no contact event for a body it destroys. **That was wrong.** `b3DestroyBody` does push a `b3ContactEndTouchEvent` (box3d `src/contact.c`), and the buffer swaps at the end of `b3World_Step`, so the reap's event surfaces in the same tick. The test passed because of a *second*, pre-existing guard in our own backend — `step()` drops end events whose shapes are no longer valid — which shields the guard I was probing. So there are two guards, not one; the test is falsifiable (removing the shape filter makes it crash on `b3Shape_GetBody` over a freed shape); and the more interesting fact is that **this slice made that filter load-bearing for the first time**, after it had sat unreachable since the physics pillar shipped, with a speculative comment. Someone tidying `step()` would have deleted it as dead code with every test still green. It now carries a comment saying exactly why it cannot go. The pattern to notice: a probe that comes back green has only tested the guard you aimed at, not the behaviour you care about. Gate: **1078/1078**, no `-E` exclusions; both `--window` entry points exit 0 clean. Deferred and recorded: `bodies_` still never shrinks (tombstones accumulate — a smaller leak, not its absence, and a free list wants a generational `BodyHandle` first), changing a body's shape or type in place, and the untestable Review Focus 4 item above.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-01 (**Physics — contact events now fire outside the backend lock, closing the follow-up #209 left behind** — #209 serialized `PhysicsSystem` behind one mutex, and its own review found the cost: step 5 of `update()` emits `ContactEvent` on the **synchronous** bus, and `EventBus::emit` dispatches sync handlers on the emitting stack, so every contact handler in the program ran *inside* the lock. Two consequences. Latent: a handler calling `raycast`/`has_body`/`apply_impulse` self-deadlocks on a plain `std::mutex` — nothing does today, and the first gameplay handler that reacts to a collision by querying physics would, presenting as a hang with no diagnostic. Already live: every lock a sync handler takes was ordered *under* `backend_mutex_`, the global logger mutex included, since the demo's contact handler logs once per contact. `update()` now collects mapped contacts into a local vector inside a **braced block** holding the lock and emits after it closes. The brace is the point, not the line count: a `unique_lock` + `unlock()` is a smaller diff but leaves the locked region ending at an unmarked statement, so a later edit adding a call-out above it silently reintroduces the bug — the lexical scope is self-enforcing, which is the same reasoning #209 used when it chose to hold the lock across all of `update()` rather than in four narrow sections. **A correction worth recording:** #209's backlog entry and this log's summary of it both claimed the fix would change when subscribers observe a contact relative to the pose write-back. It does not — the write-back is step 4 and emission is already step 5, the last statement in the function — so poses are written before any handler runs, before and after. That made the slice smaller and safer than advertised, and removed the behaviour decision the entry claimed it needed. **Two pieces of test discipline carried the slice.** First, collect-then-emit is precisely the refactor that drops or duplicates events, and *nothing in the suite would have caught either*: the determinism case compares two runs to each other, so an emit-twice bug duplicates identically in both and passes. So three characterization tests landed FIRST, against unmodified code, and were proved load-bearing by duplicating the emit — the new exactly-once case fails `2 == 1` while the determinism case still passes. Second, the pre-fix failure mode is a *hang*, and this repo sets no ctest `TIMEOUT`, so a deadlocking test would stall CI for 25 minutes. The spec's first sketch (arm a watchdog, then tick) does not work: if the tick deadlocks on the main thread nothing ever reads the flag. So the tick runs on a **detached** thread and the main thread times out at 3000 ms, making the failure a named Catch2 assertion in ~3s. **One defect of my own, found by the tests rather than by review:** the impulse case pushed `c.a` unconditionally, but `apply_impulse` is a no-op for a static body and Box3D does not guarantee which side of a contact pair is which — the ground is created first, so it was pushing the ground and asserting against a push that never happened. The handler now picks whichever side is dynamic, which also makes the case contact-order independent. Gate: **1068/1068**, no `-E` exclusions; #209's overlap detector still green, which is what proves this did not loosen the mutual exclusion it was built to guarantee; both `--window` and `--window --script-thread` exit 0 with five real contacts each. Deferred and recorded: no ctest `TIMEOUT` exists anywhere in the suite, nothing enforces the "never call user code under an internal lock" rule this now follows, and — found while scoping — **destroying an entity never destroys its physics body**, which `update()` tolerates only by accident.)

# Shipped backlog entries

## `bool` fields are read-only in the Inspector (no `field_bool`)

- ~~**`bool` fields are read-only in the Inspector (no `field_bool`)**~~ — **SHIPPED
  2026-10-26** (branch `feat/field-bool`), the `field_int` fix of 2026-10-11 one type along.
  `FieldVisitor` gains `field_bool(std::string_view, bool&)`, the adapter's `bool` branch
  calls it instead of formatting into `field_readonly`, and `ImGuiFieldVisitor` renders an
  `ImGui::Checkbox` that writes into the live component and sets `changed_`.

  **Default implementation, not pure virtual.** There are five production `FieldVisitor`
  implementations — `ImGuiFieldVisitor`, `MarkdownFieldVisitor`
  (`app/docs/component_reference.hpp`, which generates `docs/reference/components.md`), and
  `AssetRefCollector` / `ReferencedAssetGatherer` / `AssetReferenceCollector` in
  `app/mcp/mcp_tools.cpp` — plus a dozen in the test suite. A pure virtual would force every
  one of them to answer a question only the Inspector has a use for, and would change the
  generated component reference. The default forwards to
  `field_readonly(name, value ? "true" : "false")`, byte-identical to what the adapter
  emitted inline before, so **only `ImGuiFieldVisitor` opts in and nothing else moves.**
  Verified rather than asserted: `mosaic docs` was re-run and `docs/reference/components.md`
  came back with an identical md5 (`53c3798d…`). The three MCP visitors ignore
  non-reference fields entirely, so their JSON is unchanged by construction.

  **The ordering constraint changed, and the old comment was misleading about which one
  mattered.** The `int` branch's comment said `bool`-before-`int` is compiler-enforced — true,
  and still true: `field_int` takes `int&` and a `bool&` cannot bind to it, so a broad
  `is_integral_v` branch fails to build rather than misbehaving. But with `bool` now routed to
