  its own method, the ordering that actually needs guarding is **`bool` above the generic
  `is_arithmetic_v` branch**: `bool` *is* arithmetic, so deleting or demoting the branch
  compiles cleanly and silently renders `"1"`/`"0"` and drops editability. Both comments were
  rewritten to say that, and the mutation probe confirms it — deleting the `bool` branch builds
  with exit 0 and fails six tests, including the pre-existing
  `component reference renders a bool field via field_readonly`.

  **Scope correction to the entry's own measurement.** The entry claimed 16 reflected `bool`
  fields; an enumeration over every `reflect()` in the tree finds **17**. The missing one is
  `InstalledPlugin::enabled` (`app/project/project.hpp`), reflected for TOML round-tripping
  rather than registered as an ECS component, so it never reaches the Inspector — the 16-field
  count is right *for the Inspector* and wrong for "reflected `bool` fields". Also
  `AnimationClip::step` is really `Track::step`, a nested reflectable inside `AnimationClip`.
  The other 15 names were correct. Scene save/load is unaffected: serialization has its own
  `encode()` visitor and never goes through `FieldVisitor`.

  **What is covered and what is not.** The dispatch is covered by three new cases in
  `tests/unit/reflection/field_visitor_test.cpp` (routes to `field_bool`, the write reaches the
  live object, and the no-override fallback still renders `true`), 1515 → 1518, all passing.
  The **widget is not covered**, for the reason the `ImGuiFieldVisitor` backlog entry gives:
  exercising a checkbox's write-back needs a simulated multi-frame mouse interaction against
  ImGui's internal active-item state, i.e. `imgui_test_engine`, which is not vendored. No
  hand-rolled harness was built. The renderer gate (`timeout 12 env
  MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1 ... editor`) is byte-identical before and after — exit 124, three
  `[INFO]` lines, zero VUID/ERROR/WARN — but note that it proves **no regression, not the
  feature**: the editor opens with nothing selected, so `draw_inspector` returns at
  `"(nothing selected)"` and the new branch never executes under the gate.

  **CI is red on this PR for exactly one reason, and it is not the code.**
  `tools/docs/check_next_steps_claims.py` fails because `docs/project/next-steps.md` lines 19
  and 20 claim `1515 tests` / `all passing (1515/1515)` and the suite is now **1518**. This
  slice was instructed not to touch `next-steps.md` (another agent owned it the same round),
  so the claim was deliberately left stale rather than fixed here. Whoever updates that file
  must set the final number — and if the concurrent slice also adds tests, 1518 is not it.
  Nothing else in the CI run failed; build and the full suite passed.

  Original entry: found 2026-10-25 by the supervisor while reviewing U8b, pre-existing rather
  than introduced by it; decided 2026-10-26 in the third human verification pass, with no
  reasoning captured alongside the verdict.

## U8e — `UiFlow`'s default `axis`, and the "GLOW" label's occlusion

- ~~**`UiFlow`'s default `axis` should be HORIZONTAL, not vertical**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-26.**
  `UiFlow::axis` now defaults to `1`. **The decision was the human's, overruling the
  supervisor's review ruling** (which had kept vertical for consistency with `UiStack`,
  `to_anchor` and `layout_slot`, all of which treat 0 as vertical); no reasoning was captured
  with the verdict, so what stands recorded is the case as it was put: a flow's common case is
  a toolbar, an inventory row, a row of buttons.

  **The shape of the change was the open question, and the answer is "change only the
  default".** The declined alternative was to give `UiFlow`'s `axis` the opposite convention so
  its default could stay `0`. That is a trap rather than a tidier spelling: `axis` is ONE
  reflected int, shown as "axis" in the Inspector and written as "axis" in every scene file,
  and the two components are declared in the same header and share `detail::cell_slot`. The
  inconsistency the human was fixing is a DEFAULT differing between two components, which a
  reader meets once per component; the alternative would be a MEANING differing, which they
  meet on every scene file forever. `flow_slot`'s `axis == 1` test and its "anything that is
  not 1 is vertical" comment are therefore untouched and still describe the arithmetic. The
  reason for the odd default is written beside the field.

  **It moved nothing on screen, as predicted** — the demo authors `axis = 1` explicitly, and
  the `mosaic editor` capture is byte-identical to the base one.

  **The premise that the demo was the only `UiFlow` author held, and so did the warning that a
  test would break.** Grepping the tree found exactly one authoring site (`demo_scene.hpp`) and
  one default-value assertion (`UiGrid and UiFlow defaults are a single slot filling the
  frame`, `REQUIRE(f.axis == 0)`), which was flipped deliberately. **That assertion was the
  only coverage the default had, and it is a stored value rather than a behaviour** — so the
  slice adds `a default-constructed UiFlow flows along a ROW and wraps DOWNWARD`, which reads
  `axis` off a default-constructed component and feeds it to `flow_slot`. Mutation: restore
  `int axis = 0;`. Red at `REQUIRE( a.w == Approx(1.0f / 3.0f) )` with `0.5f == Approx(
  0.3333333433 )` — a default-constructed flow of 4 children at 3 per line gives half-width
  columns instead of third-width cells.

- ~~**U8c's flow block covered the lower half of the lime "GLOW" world label**~~ — **FIXED
  2026-10-26 by moving the FLOW BLOCK, not the label.** Found in the 2026-10-26 verification
  pass's screenshot rather than by any item on it. The mechanism was re-verified rather than
  taken on trust: the `WorldLabel` pass is still `render_system.hpp:445` and the `UiPanel` pass
  still `:503` (and `UiImage` `:553`, `UiText` `:600` — every UI pass is after the labels), so
  panels cover world labels unconditionally. **Not an engine bug**, and no z-ordering was added
  across that pass boundary; that remains its own architectural item.

  **Which thing to move was the choice, and the label was the wrong one.** Its 2026-10-21
  decision does name "a different position" as one of three remedies, so moving it was
  permitted — but the verdict that decision rests on ("legible, though the contrast is pretty
  low") was given against the background the label currently sits on, and moving it in WORLD
  space changes that background and reopens a question that was explicitly closed. The flow
  block is HUD chrome at an authored normalized rect, so moving it is checkable arithmetic and
  changes nothing about the label. The note beside the label is updated to say the remedy was
  spent on the HUD.

  **Where it went, and the arithmetic.** `Anchor::Bottom` / `pos {0, 0.03}` resolved to
  x [0.38, 0.62] y [0.79, 0.97]; it is now `Anchor::Top` / `pos {0, 0.20}`, i.e.
  x [0.38, 0.62] y [0.20, 0.38]. Checked against every other top-level HUD rect, all resolved
  from authored constants: the HUD bar [0.02, 0.27] x [0.02, 0.08], the top-right stack
  [0.76, 0.98] x [0.02, 0.47], the checker sprite [0.85, 0.97] x [0.03, 0.15], its underlay
  [0.79, 0.91] x [0.09, 0.21], the bottom-left layout-only container [0.03, 0.33] x
  [0.90, 0.97] and the bottom-right grid [0.73, 0.97] x [0.79, 0.97]. All six are disjoint.
  **Not flush with the top edge, for a reason worth keeping:** `pos.y = 0.03` would have put
  the block at y [0.03, 0.21], overlapping the "MOSAIC" `UiText` band (y [0.10, 0.16]) on Y and
  clearing it only on X — and a text element's width is total glyph advance divided by the
  window's aspect, so it is not an authored constant and grows as the window narrows. That
  clearance would have failed at some window size. `pos.y = 0.20` clears the text on Y alone,
  which makes every clearance in the demo's HUD a pure normalized-rect disjointness.

  **What the whole-HUD property is NOT.** Pairwise disjointness over all HUD elements is false
  and was nearly asserted: the two checker sprites overlap each other on purpose (that is the
  only way the image pass's `order` sort is checkable by eye) and both sit over the top-right
  stack's panel. The new test is therefore scoped to the flow block against each of the other
  six, and asserts `compared == 6` so a scene reorganisation cannot make it vacuous.

  **U8c's own verdict carries, as the pass predicted.** `the demo's flow keeps its partial
  final line at full-line size` asserts cell widths and offsets relative to the container, not
  the container's screen position, so it passes unchanged in the new place.

  **What the test can and cannot prove, stated because the gap matters.** `the demo's flow
  block is clear of the GLOW label's screen band and of every other HUD element` proves the
  flow covers neither of two probe rows at three x positions across the band where the label
  was seen cut. The probe x comes from authored constants — `build_world_text` centres the
  glyph run on the label's anchor, and both the label's and the camera's `Transform` are at
  world x = 0 with identity rotations, so the run's centre projects to screen x = 0.5. The
  probe y (0.80, 0.90) is inside the band the OLD authoring occupied, which is where the
  screenshot showed the cut. **It does not compute the label's screen rect**, because that is a
  projection of a world Transform through the camera and the font atlas and is not an authored
  constant — so it does not prove the whole word is unobstructed. Mutation: restore
  `Anchor::Bottom` / `pos {0, 0.03}`. Red at `REQUIRE_FALSE( contains(flow, px, py) )`,
  `!true`, with `probe (0.46, 0.8) on the GLOW label's screen band`.

  **NOBODY HAS SEEN EITHER RESULT.** This sandbox cannot screenshot the GUI, and the slice's
  gate (ctest plus zero validation errors) is structurally blind to both the uncovered label
  and the block's new position. Three eye items are in the backlog for the next verification
  pass: that the whole word "GLOW" is visible, that it still READS with its lower half showing
  (new evidence for an otherwise closed decision), and that the block at upper-centre covers
  nothing worth seeing — the prediction is that it is over sky in the demo's framing, and that
  is a prediction.

  **Gate:** suite 1515 -> 1517, all passing (4.4 s local, no flakes seen); `timeout 12 env
  MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1 mosaic editor` exits 124 with exactly three `[INFO]` lines and
  zero VUID/ERROR/WARN, **byte-identical to the base capture at `4cc6481`**; `mosaic run
  --window` under `timeout 12` exits 124 with zero VUID/ERROR/WARN.

## U8d — the `parent -> children` index: measured, and deliberately NOT built

- ~~**`stack_children` scans the whole world once per laid-out element, per frame** (U8d)~~ —
  **RESOLVED 2026-10-25 WITH A MEASUREMENT AND NO CACHE**, which is the outcome the
  decomposition pre-authorised ("U8d may legitimately ship a measurement and no cache... what
  U8d must NOT do is add the cache and report the estimate as if it were measured"). What
  shipped is `engine/ui/ui_layout_stats.hpp` (six deterministic counters on the layout path)
  and `tests/unit/ui/ui_layout_cost_test.cpp` (7 cases, 969 assertions), plus a recorded
  numeric trigger. The scan is unchanged.

  **The numbers that decided it.** Counts are exact and asserted; timings are
  min-of-21-process-runs on an idle 20-core machine, gcc 16.2.1, stated per build type and
  never averaged across them.

  | workload | visits | scans | sorts | allocations | debug cost | release cost |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | the shipped demo scene, all four resolve sites, one frame | 306 | 17 | 17 | 79 | below noise | below noise |
  | 16-child grid, one pass | 256 | 16 | 16 | 96 | below noise | below noise |
  | 64-child grid, one pass | 4,096 | 64 | 64 | 512 | ~1.0 ms | **not resolvable** |
  | 256-child grid, one pass | 65,536 | 256 | 256 | 2,560 | ~17.2 ms | **~0.7 ms** |

  **The decisive sentence is the release column.** The whole O(N^2) layout pass for a
  256-element inventory grid — the exact workload the backlog named as the trigger — costs
  **~0.7 ms per frame optimised**, about 4% of a 60 Hz frame. At 64 children the effect is
  **below this machine's noise floor and was not resolvable at all**: the weaker sentence is
  the true one, so "not shown to be slow" is what the 64-child row says, not "shown to be
  fast". And the thing that actually exists — the demo scene, the largest authored UI in the
  repository — pays 306 component visits a frame, three orders of magnitude under the
  synthetic grid. Trading `slot_for_child`'s by-construction correctness for 0.7 ms on a scene
  nobody has authored is the trade the decomposition told this slice not to make.

  **The debug/release gap is the reason this ruling needed a release build.** Debug says
  17.2 ms — a whole 60 Hz frame — and release says 0.7 ms, a **25x** difference. A slice that
  had measured only in the default preset would have reported a frame-dominating cost and
  built the cache on it. Stated plainly: every count above is build-independent (verified
  identical in both presets), and every *timing* is build-specific.

  **Three premises were checked. One held, one was overstated by ~4x, one was understated by
  3-5x — and the two errors partly cancelled, which is why the estimate's bottom line looked
  closer to right than its model was.**

  1. **"`resolve_parent_frame` runs 4-5 times a frame" — STALE, overstated ~4x.** There are
     exactly four production resolve sites (`render_system.hpp`'s panel, image and text
     passes; `ui_button_rects.hpp`'s flatten), but each iterates ITS OWN component, so an
     element is resolved **once per such component it carries** — not four times. A
     panel-only inventory slot is resolved once; a panel-plus-button slot twice (three times
     in the editor, which calls `find_order_conflicts` per frame and so flattens the buttons a
     second time). 4-5 is the worst case for an element carrying all four components, and the
     review quoted it as the per-element rate. Asserted by `an element is resolved once per
     visual component it carries, not four times`.
  2. **"Each `resolve_parent_frame` allocates 2 vectors" — UNDERSTATED 3-5x.** The measured
     figure is **6** allocations per scan at N=16, 8 at N=64 and 10 at N=256, because `keyed`
     is filled with `push_back` and reallocates geometrically; only `out` is reserved. The
     test derives the expected growth by replaying the same `push_back` sequence rather than
     hard-coding libstdc++'s doubling, so it does not fail on another standard library for a
     reason that is not a regression.
  3. **"Non-stack scenes pay nothing — the lookup short-circuits before the scan" — HOLDS for
     the scan, and the constant tripled unnoticed.** 64 parented elements with no container
     produce **0 scans, 0 visits, 0 sorts, 0 allocations**, so the O(E x P) term is genuinely
     not paid. But U8c took `slot_for_child` from one container probe to **three**, and every
     parented element in every scene now pays three O(1) sparse-set lookups per resolve where
     U8a paid one. They do not scan and do not allocate, so the claim's spirit survives; the
     constant is now proportional to the number of container types, which is what a fourth
     container would grow and nobody would notice. Counted from here on.

  **And the premise about where the cost lives is scale-dependent, which the briefing's
  phrasing did not allow for.** At 256 children `stack_children` dominates absolutely —
  65,536 visits against 768 container probes. At the demo scene's scale it does **not**: 306
  visits against 54 probes plus the per-element `inherited_alpha` and `active_hover_style`
  walks up the parent chain, which are the same order of magnitude and are not counted at all.
  So "is `stack_children` the hot part?" has two different true answers depending on N, and
  only the large-N one was ever asserted.

  **THE FINDING WORTH THE MOST, and it came from running the cache as a mutation rather than
  from reasoning.** The gate named draw/hit agreement as the property a cache endangers. It
  is necessary and **it is not sufficient**: the naive memo (a function-local
  `parent -> children` cache in `slot_for_child`, populated once, never invalidated) left the
  agreement assertions **GREEN**. Both paths read the *same* cache, so a stale enumeration
  makes them agree on a wrong answer instead of disagreeing. A slice that asserted only
  agreement would have shipped that cache believing the gate covered it. The case therefore
  also checks every rect against a slot computed **without** the layout path — the expected
  order re-derived from the authored keys, the expected rect from `grid_slot` directly — and
  that duplication is the point: a trip-wire for a stale enumeration must not ask the
  enumeration what the answer is. With the truth anchor added, the memo mutation fires at
  `drawn.x == Approx(want.x)` with `0.8125f == Approx(0.5625)` after a destroy.

  **The recorded trigger, in the counter's own units so it is checkable rather than a feeling.**
  Build the index when a real authored scene's measured per-frame `visits` exceeds **~200,000**
  (≈ 2 ms per frame at the measured rate, ≈ 12% of a 60 Hz budget), or when `scans` per frame
  exceeds ~500. Today's shipped scene is at 306 visits and 17 scans, so the trigger is ~650x
  away. `ui_layout_cost_test.cpp` is how to measure a candidate scene: add it as a case and
  read the counters.

  **Four mutations were run and each fires on its named assertion.** `keyed.reserve(16)` in
  `stack_children` — `s.allocations == 16 * (growth_steps(16) + 1)`, `16 == 96` (and
  `192 == 512`, `1280 == 2560` at the other two sizes). The container short-circuit moved
  *after* the scan — `s.scans == 0`, `64 == 0`. `resolve_ui_rect` swapped for `resolve_rect`
  in `ui_button_rects.hpp`, so the hit path bypasses the hierarchy — `c.rect.x ==
  Approx(drawn.x)`, `0.25f == Approx(0.0625)`. And the uninvalidated memo above.

  **The instrument's own cost was measured, not asserted.** On the same 256-child workload the
  instrumented and uninstrumented builds differ by **+0.01 ms of 0.77 ms** in release and
  **+0.11 ms of 17.16 ms** in debug — both inside the run-to-run spread, so the counters are
  not shown to cost anything measurable (and not shown to be free; the noise floor cannot
  resolve a 1% effect). `visits` is the only counter inside a loop. They are unconditional and
  non-thread-safe by choice; gating them is in the backlog with its own trigger.

  **Gate:** suite 1508 -> 1515, all passing (4.0-4.5 s local); `timeout 12 env
  MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1 mosaic editor` exits 124 with the same three `[INFO]` lines and
  zero VUIDs, **byte-identical to the base capture**. **U8 is complete** — U8a, U8b, U8c and
  U8d all resolved; content-sized slots and the editor layout gizmo remain deferred behind
  their named blockers.

## U8c — `UiGrid` and `UiFlow`

- ~~**`UiGrid` and `UiFlow`** (U8c)~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-25.** Two more layout containers,
  `UiGrid{cols, rows, spacing, padding}` and `UiFlow{axis, per_line, spacing, padding}`, both
  reflected and registered. **Genuinely additive, as the decomposition predicted:**
  `stack_children`, `child_layout_order` and the `resolve_parent_frame` hook are untouched;
  the only change outside `ui_stack.hpp` is two more component lookups in `slot_for_child`,
  which the decomposition already named as the one place the container type is dispatched.
  That premise was re-checked against the code and **held** — nothing else in the engine
  switches on `UiStack`, and the only other references are the reflection registration and
  tests.

  **The two decisions that were the implementer's, and both are recorded beside the
  arithmetic in `ui_stack.hpp`.**

  1. **Flow's wrap rule: an authored `per_line`.** A flow fills `per_line` slots along its
     main axis and starts a new line on the cross axis, so the line count is
     `ceil(count / per_line)` and moves as children come and go. Nothing measures a child —
     wrapping on a child's own size needs content measurement, and content-sized slots are
     deferred behind "may layout block on asset loading?".
  2. **A partial final line sizes AS IF THE LINE WERE FULL.** Four children at three per line
     means the lone child on the second line is a third of the main axis and flush with the
     cell above it, not the whole line. The alternative (divide the final line among the
     children actually present) was defensible and was declined for consistency with the
     human's grid call: it would make a cell's size change whenever a child is created or
     destroyed, which is exactly the defect that got the cols-only `UiGrid` declined.
     Refusing that reason for the grid and accepting it for the flow would make the pair
     incoherent.

  **The line between the two containers, and it is a tested property rather than a claim.**
  A grid divides BOTH axes by an authored number, so `grid_slot` takes no child count at all
  — that absence *is* the settled decision. A flow divides the main axis by an authored
  number and the cross axis by a derived one. `a flow's line count follows the child count but
  a grid's does not` pins it. **Stated plainly rather than hidden:** the two share one
  `detail::cell_slot` core and `flow_slot` *is* `grid_slot` with one divisor derived, so at a
  child count that exactly fills an authored grid they produce identical rects. Sharing the
  core is deliberate — it is what stops the two from drifting apart the way the three
  `to_anchor` copies once did — and the distinction worth keeping is in what the scene
  authors, not in the pixels.

  **One finding against the briefing, and it was MY OWN TEST that was wrong.** The decisive
  test for the settled grid decision compared a 6-child grid with a 5-child one and asserted
  the cells had not moved. The declined alternative gives `ceil(5 / 3) == 2 == `the authored
  rows, so **the mutation implementing the declined alternative passed that test.** 3 children
  is the count that distinguishes them (`ceil(3 / 3) == 1`, which doubles every cell's
  height). Re-probed, the assertion fires at `half_full.h == Approx(full.h)` with
  `1.0f == Approx(0.5)`. The generalisable error: the test was built from the *shape* of the
  decision rather than from the arithmetic the declined alternative would actually compute,
  so its inputs happened to land where the two agree.

  **Two more premises checked rather than trusted.** `child_layout_order`'s fixed
  `UiPanel -> UiImage -> UiText` precedence is still right for grid and flow children, and
  `UiButton`'s absence from it **does** matter more now — a grid of clickable inventory slots
  is the obvious shape for this feature, and a child that is a bare hit region with no visual
  falls through to `return 0` and is ordered only by the entity-index tiebreak. Deliberately
  not fixed: `UiButton::order` is a hit-resolution z-order, a different concept from the draw
  order layout sorts by, and importing it would turn the authoring slip `ui_order_check.hpp`
  exists for from a mis-routed click into a relocated element. Characterised by a test and
  deferred with the decision it needs. And the handoff's **"four entity-count tests count
  Transforms" line was false for one of the four** — `demo_scene_test.cpp:74` counts
  `ScriptComponent`s — which `next-steps.md` now says. None of the four moved: the eleven new
  demo entities carry neither a Transform nor a script.

  **The demo authoring, and the instrument that made it checkable without a gizmo.** A 3x2
  grid bottom-right, exactly full, with `spacing` and `padding` authored 0 and every child
  `size = {1,1}`, so the six children must exactly tile the container — U8a's own standard.
  **The container is deliberately VISIBLE and MAGENTA**, which is the whole trick: nothing
  else in the demo is magenta, so any magenta visible is a seam. Plus a 5-item flow at three
  per line bottom-centre, where the empty sixth slot shows the backdrop and the two cells on
  the partial line visibly align with the two above them — the second decision above, made
  checkable by eye. **This retires the decomposition's own prediction** that authoring a grid
  by numbers would hurt and would promote the deferred slot gizmo: it did not hurt, and the
  reason is that what makes a grid checkable is a contrasting backdrop behind children that
  should tile it, not a reading of `padding` and `spacing`. The gizmo's trigger is now
  narrower — a container whose children do NOT tile it, where the trick says nothing.

  **U8d's instrument was built here and deliberately left uncached.** `a grid of two hundred
  and fifty six children lays out from a test alone` drives the whole layout path at
  inventory-grid size from a fixture, with no demo scene, renderer or window: it is O(N^2) by
  construction (~65k component visits and 256 heap allocations), so U8d's before/after needs
  no GUI. Adding a cache here would have destroyed that measurement.

  **Gate:** suite 1478 → 1508, all passing (4.5 s local); `timeout 12 env
  MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1 mosaic editor` exits 124 with the same three `[INFO]` lines and
  zero VUIDs, **byte-identical to the base capture**, and `run --window` exits 124 with two
  quiet `[INFO]` lines. Six mutations were run and each fires on its named assertion: cell
  width shrunk 5% (`c.w == Approx(1.0f / 3.0f)`, `0.31667f`), the partial line divided among
  present children (`lone.w == Approx(1.0f / 3.0f)`, `1.0f`), grid overflow wrapped with
  `index % slots` (`past.w == Approx(0.0f)`, `0.33333f`), grid rows derived from the child
  count (`half_full.h == Approx(full.h)`, `1.0f`), the flow line count pinned to 1
  (`one_of_four.h == Approx(0.5f)`, `1.0f`), and the container precedence reversed
  (`frame.w == Approx(stacked.w)`, `0.33333f`).

  **`ui_stack.hpp` now holds all three containers and all the slot arithmetic**, and says so
  at the top: the file name is historical, and the single file is deliberate because the three
  divisions share a floored-input discipline and a never-negative-extent rule that must not
  diverge.

## U8b — a real `visible` bool on the UI components

- ~~**A real `visible` bool on the UI components** (U8b)~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-25.** A
  `bool visible = true` on `UiPanel`, `UiImage`, `UiText`, `WorldLabel` and `UiButton`,
  reflected and last on every struct. The four draw passes read their own component's;
  `button_hit_candidates` reads `UiButton`'s. That makes a **layout-only container**
  authorable at last — the trap U8a shipped with and every later U8 slice would have paid.

  **The authored recipe is `alpha = 1, visible = false`**, and both halves are load-bearing:
  `visible` hides this one component's quad, and alpha 1 is the identity of the
  inherited-alpha product, so the children render at their own authored alpha. `ui_stack.hpp`
  now documents that instead of the two undiscoverable workarounds it used to offer (a fully
  transparent `color`, or an alpha low enough to look hidden but above zero) and instead of
  the alpha-propagation opt-out it named as an alternative, which was **declined** 2026-10-20.

  **The backlog entry's stated mechanism was wrong, and the decomposition spec had already
  caught it:** "`alpha = 0` ... still HIT-TESTS, so an invisible panel still eats clicks" is
  not what the code does. A `UiPanel` is never hit-tested at all — `button_hit_candidates`
  iterates `for_each<UiButton>` and reads no alpha anywhere — so the real defect was the
  **reverse** pairing: fading a button's *visual* left the button fully clickable. The
  decision survived the correction unchanged, and the implementation got *cheaper*: the draw
  and hit paths read **different components**, so there is no single predicate and none of the
  draw/hit coupling U6 spent a slice untangling.

  **The sharp trap, and what the slice turned on.** `visible` affects drawing only. It must
  NOT make `resolve_parent_frame` treat the panel as **absent**: that function returns the
  whole screen `{0,0,1,1}` for a parent with no `UiPanel`, so an invisible container treated
  as absent resolves every child against the *screen* instead of its slot — the exact opposite
  of the feature, and the only failure here with no other symptom (the children are still
  drawn, just in the wrong places). **Proved by mutation, not by argument:** adding
  `|| !pp->visible` to that one condition turns three tests red, the sharpest being
  `an invisible container still narrows its children's frames` at `b.x == Approx(a.x)`,
  `0.0f == Approx(0.112)` — the child snapping to the screen's left edge.

  **The no-inference ruling was pinned the same way.** Hiding a `UiPanel` must not disable a
  `UiButton` on the same entity, because `demo_scene.hpp` piles the unrelated "MOSAIC" title
  and the checker `UiImage` onto the `camera` entity — co-location is a convention this
  repository keeps, not an invariant the data enforces. Adding the "helpful" cross-component
  check to `button_hit_candidates` turns exactly one test red,
  `an invisible UiPanel leaves its entity's UiButton hit-testable`, at `cands.size() == 1`
  with `0 == 1`.

  **A prediction of the briefing that was too narrow.** The positional-brace-init landmine was
  located as "exactly one site, the camera HUD bar at `demo_scene.hpp:722`". That is correct
  *for `UiPanel`* and narrower than the hazard: the same file held two positional `UiText`
  inits and two positional `WorldLabel` inits, both components this slice also gave a field.
  All five were converted to designated initialisers in the first commit.

  **One free consequence, asserted so it stays free:** `find_order_conflicts` draws its rects
  from `button_hit_candidates`, so an invisible button raises no order finding — it can
  misroute nothing. The per-entity lint `find_order_disagreements` reads `for_each<UiButton>`
  directly and still fires; deferred with the one-sentence decision it needs.

  **Gate:** suite 1462 → 1478, all passing; `timeout 12 env MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1
  mosaic editor` exits 124 with the same three `[INFO]` lines and zero VUIDs, byte-identical to
  the base capture. The demo scene now authors a layout-only container bottom-left (three
  pips in a row with no bar behind them), which is what the eye-check looks at.

  **WorldLabel was judged in scope** — it is world-space and in no layout, but it is the
  fourth of the four draw paths that early-out on `alpha <= 0`, and a field on three of four
  would mean "drawable, sometimes".

## U8a — inherited alpha and stack layout

- ~~**U8a — inherited alpha and stack layout**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-17.**
  `UiStack{axis, spacing, padding}` on a parent narrows each child's frame to
  an evenly divided slot, inside `resolve_parent_frame` — the one function
  panels, images, button hit-tests and text all funnel through. A container's
  alpha multiplies down the `UiParent` chain at render time, writing nothing
  back. Two of U8's own claims were wrong and are corrected in the spec: layout
  cannot be a mutating pre-pass (resolution is lazy and `pos` is reflected, so
  the overwrite would serialise), and the hook belongs in
  `resolve_parent_frame` rather than `resolve_ui_rect` (the text pass calls the
  former directly). The decision U8 reserved for the human turned out not to
  need one: narrowing the frame means `resolve_in` already treats `pos` as an
  offset from the slot. Spec
  `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-17-ui-layout-containers-design.md`.

## U6 interaction depth — hover enter/exit + visual feedback, pointer capture/drag, z-order-aware overlap resolution. First: its prerequisite just landed, and it …

- ~~**U6 interaction depth** — hover enter/exit + visual feedback, pointer
  capture/drag, z-order-aware overlap resolution. First: its prerequisite just
  landed, and it is the gap a user meets in the first five seconds.~~
  **SHIPPED 2026-10-10** — two of the three features. Z-order hit resolution now lives in
  `engine/ui/ui_hit.hpp`'s pure `topmost_hit`, `UiButton` carries its own `order`, and
  `UiSystem` emits `hover_enter`/`hover_exit` on the button's existing `event` channel with
  the same `{entity, action}` payload the click path uses. **The motivating bug turned out to
  be a wrong answer rather than an undefined one:** the press took the FIRST button under
  the pointer while the panel pass draws the LAST of an equal-order run on top, so a click
  on two overlapping equal-order buttons went to the button the user could not see.
  ~~**Capture/drag remains open** (listed above).~~ **U6 IS COMPLETE as of 2026-10-12** —
  capture/drag shipped; see its own entry. So do both accepted costs — a button's
  `order` can disagree with its visual's, and the engine still renders no hover feedback of
  its own — and neither was resolved by this slice. ~~Carries one open
  question for a human — whether `UiButton` gets its own `order` or inherits its
  `UiPanel` ancestor's.~~ **DECIDED 2026-10-10: `UiButton` gets its own `order`**, matching
  `UiPanel`, `UiImage` and `UiText`, all of which already carry one — `UiButton` was the
  only UI component without it. The known cost of that choice, recorded so it is not
  rediscovered as a surprise: a button's `order` and its overlaid visual's `order` **can
  disagree**, and nothing warns about it. **Scoped 2026-10-10 to z-order + hover**, with
  capture/drag split out (below).

## Pointer capture and drag for `UiButton`

- ~~**Pointer capture and drag for `UiButton`**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-12, closing U6.**
  `UiSystem::pressed_` became `captured_`, which is what it already meant; hover now resolves
  against the captured entity alone, so no other button lights up mid-drag and the captured
  one is hovered only while the cursor is inside its own rect. Four new actions on the
  button's existing channel: `drag_start` (on movement past `kDragThreshold`, **not** on
  press, or every click would also emit one), `drag` per moving frame, `drag_end` on release,
  `drag_cancel` when the pointer leaves the surface. `drag_start`/`drag`/`drag_end` carry
  `x`/`y`; `click` and the hover actions keep their exact `{entity, action}` shape.
  **Both open questions this entry named are answered:** a captured press does *not* follow
  the pointer outside the surface — the editor's provider deliberately drops `down` there, so
  a drag cannot keep running while the user clicks in the Inspector — and a drag emits
  per-frame events *and* a start/end pair, since `drag_start` is an edge and `drag` a stream.
  Original entry: split out of U6 on 2026-10-10 when U6 was
  scoped, not dropped. **Why it was separated:** z-order and hover are *coupled* — "which
  button is hovered" has no answer without a tie-break, so hover built on the current
  behaviour would flicker between overlapping buttons — whereas capture is partly present
  already (`UiSystem` remembers `pressed_` and requires release inside the same button), so
  what remains is a smaller and genuinely independent change. **And it is the riskiest
  third:** its failure modes are the subtlest (the pointer leaving the window mid-drag, a
  press surviving a play-mode change), which is the exact bug class that shipped viewport
  pointer input **dead** while thirteen tests stayed green. Reviewing it beside two
  unrelated features is how that recurs. **Depends on** U6's z-order work only loosely;
  it mainly needs the pointer-state widening recorded for U9 (wheel/multi-button) if drag
  is ever to mean more than left-button. **What it will need deciding:** whether a captured
  press follows the pointer outside the window, and whether a drag emits per-frame events
  or only a start/end pair.

## A pointer that leaves the window can still hover an edge-anchored button

- ~~**A pointer that leaves the window can still hover an edge-anchored button**~~ —
  **FIXED 2026-10-10 in the U6 review pass, and this entry was WRONG twice over while it
  stood.** It scoped the problem to `app/main.cpp` and "the pointer is over another
  application", when in the editor the clamp region is a *panel* inside a window full of
  other panels — so leaving it is a constant, ordinary action, not an edge case. And it
  justified deferring by saying the honest fix needed a `PointerState` widening "the same
  one U9 needs"; that was false for the editor path, where `ViewportPointerSample::hovered`
  was **already sampled and already consulted three lines away** for `down`. It also never
  mentioned the `(0,0)` case at all, which is not "tracks to the edge, better than
  sticking" but the exact phantom hover the `if (!pointer_)` guard was written for, arriving
  through a different door: headless `mosaic run` never creates a window, so its provider
  returned `{0,0,false}` every tick and hovered any button containing the origin forever.
  **What shipped instead:** `PointerState` gained an explicit `inside` flag, honoured in
  `topmost_hit` so press and hover cannot disagree about it; the editor provider sets it
  from `hovered`; and headless is given **no** provider rather than one returning zeroes.
  The lesson worth keeping: the entry asserted the demo was unaffected and therefore eyes
  would not catch it, and treated that as a reason to defer rather than as a reason to fix.

## Engine-rendered hover visuals (a `UiHoverStyle` component)

- ~~**Engine-rendered hover visuals (a `UiHoverStyle` component)**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-10.**
  `UiHoverStyle` (authored, reflected) multiplies an element's colour and alpha while
  hovered; `UiHovered` (runtime, deliberately **unregistered**) marks the hovered entity;
  `active_hover_style` walks up the `UiParent` chain and `resolve_appearance` does the
  multiply and clamp; the three UI render passes apply it as a **render-time override** so
  nothing mutates the authored values. The demo now authors a style instead of running a
  script, and its nested "HP" label inherits the style from the bar it is parented to.
  **Still open:** the `order`-disagreement item below, which this slice unblocked but
  deliberately did not take. Originally recorded as **UNBLOCKED and specified 2026-10-10**: `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-10-ui-hover-ownership-design.md`. The blocker
  turned out to be a non-problem. **The ownership relation already existed as entity
  identity:** every button in the repository shares an entity with its visuals (the shipped
  template's entity 8 carries `UiButton` + `UiImage` + `UiPanel` + `UiText`; the demo puts
  `UiButton` on the `camera` entity that already holds the HUD `UiPanel`), with no
  counter-example anywhere. The convention was in the data and had simply never been written
  down. The stated rule is now "the element's own entity, or its nearest `UiParent`
  ancestor", resolved by walking **up** — `UiParent` points upward, so finding descendants
  would have meant scanning every entity. Original reasoning kept below, because it was wrong
  in an instructive way. **Why it was deferred:** it needs a button→visual **ownership**
  relationship that does not exist.
  `UiButton` is deliberately non-visual and `UiParent` is a *transform* hierarchy, not an
  ownership one, so "tint the thing this button looks like" has no definition today. It
  would also put interaction state into the render path, which has so far been kept free of
  it. **The honest cost of deferring:** on the events-only design the engine shows a user
  **nothing** by itself — a button acknowledges the pointer only if someone wrote a script
  for it, which is not what a person expects from a button. U6 ships a demo script so the
  behaviour is visible, but that is a demonstration, not a default. **Depends on** settling
  UI ownership, which is also what the `order`-disagreement item below needs — they are
  likely one piece of work.

## The REAL `order` bug is pairwise and relative, not per-entity and absolute

- ~~**The REAL `order` bug is pairwise and relative, not per-entity and absolute**~~ —
  **SHIPPED 2026-10-12** as `find_order_conflicts` in `engine/ui/ui_order_check.hpp`, with the
  button-rect flatten extracted to `engine/ui/ui_button_rects.hpp` so the check polices the
  exact rects `UiSystem`'s hit test uses. Two kinds: **Inverted** (both axes ordered,
  opposite rankings — the definite bug) and **Ambiguous** (one axis tied, so the outcome
  falls to iteration order and can flip when an unrelated button is destroyed). Both-axes-tied
  is deliberately silent: the author expressed no preference either way, and reporting it
  would fire on every pair of overlapping default-constructed buttons. Overlap is inclusive,
  matching `topmost_hit`, so buttons touching along an edge count. The per-entity lint
  **stays** — it catches the authoring slip before a second button exists, which is exactly
  when this check has nothing to say — but **it stands down for any entity this check names**
  (`without_entities_in_conflict`, added 2026-10-12 after a human working the verification
  checklist saw two warnings for one mistake). One problem, one line. Original framing, which was right:  found
  2026-10-11 while building the lint above, which is why that lint is honest about being a
  heuristic. A single button in a scene cannot misroute a click whatever its `order` says: the
  failure needs **two overlapping buttons**, where A's `UiButton::order` ranks above B's while
  A's `UiPanel::order` ranks below B's, so the click goes to A and the user sees B on top. The
  shipped lint catches the authoring slip that bug grows from (editing a panel's order and
  forgetting its button), which is cheap; the real check needs resolved-rect overlap testing
  between every pair of buttons, the screen aspect, and is O(n²) over buttons. **Deferred
  because it is a different and much larger piece of work** than the recorded item asked for,
  and the cheap lint plausibly prevents most instances. **Depends on** nothing new —
  `resolve_ui_rect` already exists — so this is a scoping decision, not a blocker.

## `UiImage::order` and `UiText::order` are ignored by the renderer entirely

- ~~**`UiImage::order` and `UiText::order` are ignored by the renderer entirely**~~ —
  **FIXED 2026-10-11.** Both passes now collect, `stable_sort` ascending, then emit, so higher
  `order` draws on top within its own pass and equal orders keep `for_each` order. The
  semantics are documented on both components for the first time. Original finding, which
  still explains the pass-boundary limitation below:
  confirmed
  2026-10-11 by reading `engine/render/render_system.hpp`'s three HUD passes. They are fixed
  and sequential (panels, then images over panels, then text over both) and **only the panel
  pass `stable_sort`s**. So a `UiText` is above a `UiPanel` unconditionally, whatever either
  `order` says, and both fields are reflected, documented and inert. This is **why the lint
  above compares panels only**: an earlier version compared all three and fired on the shipped
  demo, whose `camera` entity carries `UiPanel{0}`, `UiImage{0}` and `UiText{1}` — a finding
  **no button order could have silenced**, because those three do not agree with each other
  either. The 🟢 quick win "Sort the HUD image pass by `UiImage::order`" is half of the fix;
  the text pass needs the same treatment and is **not** currently tracked as a quick win.
  Until both land, any cross-pass `order` comparison is meaningless.

## Three copies of the same anchor clamper

- ~~**Three copies of the same anchor clamper**~~ — **FIXED 2026-10-12.** One
  `mosaic::engine::ui::to_anchor` now lives in `engine/ui/ui_rect.hpp`, beside the `Anchor`
  enum it clamps to, and all five call sites use it. Its upper bound is written against
  `Anchor::BottomRight` rather than a literal `8`, so extending the enum needs no edit there
  at all — which was the actual hazard, not the duplication. Original entry: `to_anchor` in
  `engine/render/render_system.hpp`, `hierarchy_anchor` in `engine/ui/ui_hierarchy.hpp` and
  `button_anchor` in `engine/ui/ui_button_rects.hpp` are the same four-line
  `(a >= 0 && a <= 8) ? cast : TopLeft` in three files. Noticed 2026-10-12 while moving the
  third one, and a **deliberate non-goal of that slice**: collapsing them is an unrelated
  refactor touching the render system, and bundling it into a diagnostic diff is how a
  reviewer loses track of which half to scrutinise. Pre-existing, correctness-safe (all three
  are identical), cheap whenever someone wants it. **Depends on** nothing.

## No end-to-end test that a conflicting pair actually misroutes a click

- ~~**No end-to-end test that a conflicting pair actually misroutes a click**~~ — **CLOSED
  2026-10-12**, the same night it was filed. `tests/unit/render/render_system_test.cpp`'s "a
  conflicting pair really does misroute the click" builds one world with an inverted pair,
  drives a real `PointerState` through press and release, asserts the `ScriptEvent` came from
  the button with the higher `UiButton::order`, then renders the same world through
  `FakeRenderDevice` and asserts the OTHER button's panel is the last quad in the batch —
  so the one that answered is provably the one drawn underneath. Probed both ways: inverting
  `topmost_hit`'s ordering and reversing the panel pass's sort each turn it red, so it holds
  both mechanisms and not just one. Original entry: recorded
  2026-10-12 as the one thing the pairwise check cannot prove about itself. It reports that
  `UiButton::order` and `UiPanel::order` rank a pair differently; that this *causes* the wrong
  button to receive the event rests on `topmost_hit`'s tests plus the panel pass's sort, each
  covered separately but never together. A test that drives a real `PointerState` into a
  conflicting pair and asserts which entity gets the `ScriptEvent` would close it. **Depends
  on** nothing — `UiSystem` already takes an injected pointer, so it is headless; it was left
  out only to keep the slice's diff about the check itself.

## The GLFW provider never reports `inside = false`, so `drag_cancel` is unreachable in `mosaic run --window`

- ~~**The GLFW provider never reports `inside = false`, so `drag_cancel` is unreachable in
  `mosaic run --window`**~~ — **FIXED 2026-10-12: the standalone runtime now matches the
  editor.** The policy moved to `app/run/window_pointer.hpp` as a pure
  `window_pointer_state(cursor_x, cursor_y, window_w, window_h, button_down)`, which is the
  same split `studio/viewport.hpp` makes for the editor's provider and for the same stated
  reason — the policy is the part worth testing and stays testable only if the GLFW calls
  live at the call site. Presence is gated on the cursor being over the window, `down` is
  gated on presence, and the edges are inclusive to match `topmost_hit`. **Two visible
  consequences, both intended:** a cursor on the desktop no longer hovers an edge-anchored
  button, and a drag off the window edge now ends as `drag_cancel` rather than a `drag_end`
  carrying a clamped coordinate. Original entry: found by review 2026-10-12. `app/main.cpp`'s pointer lambda leaves
  `inside` at its default `true` except for a null or minimised window, and clamps `x`/`y`
  into `[0,1]`. GLFW keeps reporting the button pressed while the cursor is outside a focused
  window, so dragging off the edge and releasing there gives `down` false with `inside` true →
  a **`drag_end` carrying a clamped coordinate the user never pointed at**. That is exactly
  the dishonesty the capture spec cites as its reason for omitting the position from
  `drag_cancel` — but the reasoning only holds for the editor's provider, which gates `inside`
  on `hovered`. **Deferred because the fix is a behaviour decision about the standalone
  runtime** (should hover stop at the window edge there, as it does in the editor?) rather
  than part of the slice that exposed it. Nothing is affected today: no shipped handler reads
  a drag action.

## A press surviving a play-mode change emits a stale drag action

- ~~**A press surviving a play-mode change emits a stale drag action**~~ — **FIXED
  2026-10-18**, where this entry said it belonged: in the editor, which is the only
  party that knows a transition happened. `UiSystem` gained
  `reset_press_state()` (silent, and deliberately leaving hover alone), and
  `editor.cpp` calls it edge-triggered on `is_playing()` changing. Three tests
  cover it: the abandoned gesture emits nothing, `press_consumed()` stops
  claiming the click, and hover is undisturbed. Original entry:

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **A press surviving a play-mode change emits a stale drag action** — found by review
>   2026-10-12, and the backlog predicted this failure class when it split capture/drag out of
>   U6 ("its failure modes are the subtlest: the pointer leaving the window mid-drag, a press
>   surviving a play-mode change"). While the editor is stopped, `World::tick` never runs, so
>   `UiSystem` freezes mid-press with `was_down_` true. Press the HUD button, drag past the
>   threshold, hit **Stop**, then later **Play** or **Step**: the first tick takes the release
>   edge and emits a `drag_cancel` — or `drag_end` plus `click` if the cursor happens to be back
>   over the button — for a gesture that ended seconds earlier. The `click` half is pre-existing;
>   the slice widens it from one stale action to three. **The right fix is in the editor**
>   (reset the system's press state on a play-state transition), not in `UiSystem`, which is why
>   it is deferred rather than patched where it was noticed.

## `int` fields are read-only in the Inspector (no `field_int`)

- ~~**`int` fields are read-only in the Inspector (no `field_int`)**~~ — **FIXED 2026-10-11**,
  so the order lint's advice is now actionable in-editor: the field the warning names can be
  dragged. Original entry: recorded 2026-10-11.
  `ImGuiFieldVisitor` (`studio/inspector.hpp`) implements `field_float`, `field_string`,
  `field_asset` and `field_readonly` only, so every `order`, `anchor` and `fit_mode` renders as
  a label. **This makes the new lint's advice unactionable inside the editor**: a user reads
  "UiButton order 7 but UiPanel order 0" in the Console and then has to go and edit the scene
  file or write a script, because the field the warning names cannot be typed into. Cheap on
  the existing visitor machinery — one `field_int` override plus a `DragInt` — and it is the
  natural companion to the lint rather than part of it.

## U7 image presentation — aspect-preserving fit/fill, 9-slice scaling, texture atlas sub-rects. Independent of everything else and the cheapest real group.

- ~~**U7 image presentation** — aspect-preserving fit/fill, 9-slice scaling, texture
  atlas sub-rects. Independent of everything else and the cheapest real group.~~
  **SHIPPED 2026-10-10** — but only two of the three features. `UiImage` gained
  `sub_rect_pos`/`sub_rect_size` (texture **pixels**, zero size = whole texture) and
  `fit_mode` (Stretch/Fit/Fill), with all the arithmetic in one pure function,
  `engine/ui/ui_fit.hpp`'s `resolve_image_quad`, tested with no world and no device.
  `TextureCache` now keeps `{handle, width, height}` — it already loaded the `Image`
  to create the texture and threw the dimensions away. `UiImagePush` went 32 → 48 and
  `ui_image.vert` interpolates the UVs with the same `mix()` form `ui_text.vert` has
  used for glyph sub-rects all along. **9-slice is still open** and still listed above;
  its stated dependency — this slice's sub-rect UVs — now exists.

## Retune the demo's particle, ribbon, decal and world-text colours

- ~~**Retune the demo's particle, ribbon, decal and world-text colours**~~
  — **DONE 2026-10-17**, after the human verification pass said yes. The
  curve desaturates: the puffer's cyan went from 0.70 saturation to 0.46,
  and the decal's warm tint from 0.40 to **0.16** — nearly grey, and
  invisible against the white ground, which is why it could not be found
  in the pass's own screenshots. The fountain's orange was worse than a
  desaturation: ACES RAISES the green channel (0.5 -> 0.62) while lowering
  red (1.0 -> 0.80), so it read yellow. Retuned by authoring ABOVE 1.0 and
  letting the roll-off do the work — which is the headroom this slice
  bought, used for the first time. Ribbons and world text have no entity
  in the demo scene, so they were not retuned and remain unjudged.

## No demo entity exercises bloom on ribbons or world text

- ~~**No demo entity exercises bloom on ribbons or world text**~~ — **DONE
  2026-10-21.** Two entities now: a scripted counter-orbiting "comet" whose
  only visible part is a magenta `TrailRenderer` `{2.4, 0.12, 1.9}`, and a
  static lime `WorldLabel` `{1.0, 2.85, 0.25}`. **Predicted on-screen colours**
  (the Narkowicz ACES fit, checked first against the fountain's known
  `{2.85, 0.30, 0.05}` -> `(0.95, 0.44, 0.04)`): ribbon head
  `(0.93, 0.16, 0.91)`, label interior `(0.80, 0.95, 0.37)`, both before
  bloom's halo and for the opaque core only (the ribbon tapers to nothing).
  **Nobody has seen either yet**; these are arithmetic, not observation, and
  the lime label on a light-grey lit ground is a low-contrast pairing that may
  not read. The next human pass should judge both. **What the entry got wrong:**
  it said "two entities and the existing retune arithmetic". The arithmetic
  held, but a ribbon is a `TrailRenderer` (there is no standalone ribbon
  component; `build_ribbon` is a function) and a trail only exists behind
  something that MOVES, so the comet needed a third thing, a new orbit script
  (`kRibbonOrbitScript`), and that script moved a fourth count assertion (the
  scripted-object count 3 -> 4) beside the three entity counts 12 -> 14.
  `bundle_test`'s `== 11` needed no change: it does not use
  `build_default_scene`.

## Demo props are placed against the world origin, not against the surface they sit on

- ~~**Demo props are placed against the world origin, not against the
  surface they sit on**~~ — **DONE 2026-10-21** (the class; #265 had fixed
  the decal). The test `nothing authored in the demo sits below the ground's
  top face` loops over every non-ground entity that draws something (mesh,
  decal, trail, world label, particle emitter) over the ground's footprint
  and requires its origin at or above the top face, the ground being the
  scene's only static `RigidBody`. **Ruling: the general test, not a
  `place_on_surface` helper**, because it pins the class with no new API; a
  helper can follow if a second case wants to PLACE rather than check.
  **Probed:** burying the new comet, the new label, and the decal each turns
  it red. Two things the entry did not predict: "props" needed a definition
  (a test over only `MeshRenderer` would have missed the decal, the very
  bug), and it compares ORIGINS, so the fountain and puffer emitters sit at
  exactly `y = 0.5` and pass only because the check is `>=`. A prop whose
  origin is above the face but whose extent dips below it is NOT caught;
  every mesh here is centred, so that is not a live gap.

## The editor forwards no pointer input to the scene, so nothing in it can be clicked

- ~~**The editor forwards no pointer input to the scene, so nothing in it
  can be clicked**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-07**, play-mode only. `ViewportPanel`
  records the raw ImGui facts and computes the `PointerState`; `editor.cpp`
  hands the same provider to `UiSystem` (through `setup_runtime`'s `pointer`
  parameter, which it had been leaving defaulted) and to `ClickNudgeSystem`
  (through `register_windowed_systems`, which only `main.cpp` had ever
  called). **Three things the original analysis below did not anticipate.**
  (1) The panel-relative coordinate transform *already existed* in
  `draw_viewport`, for edit-mode click-to-select — which is what made
  play-mode-only cheap, and which now has to be gated on `!is_playing()` or
  one click both drives the pointer and moves the selection. That gate is a
  behaviour change to shipped code: clicking the viewport no longer selects
  while playing. (2) `ClickNudgeSystem` fires on a press **edge**, so gating
  `down` on play mode *synthesises a click* — hold the button in edit mode,
  press Play, and `down` flips false→true with no physical press. The panel
  latches presses that began while ineligible and stays suppressed until
  release. (3) The `aspect` provider must be the viewport **panel's**, not the
  window's; the scene renders into a panel-sized offscreen target, so a
  window-derived aspect diverges the nudge ray from the clicked image.
  Original analysis follows.

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **The editor forwards no pointer input to the scene, so nothing in it
>   can be clicked** — found 2026-10-06 while visually verifying IBL in the
>   editor. `app/main.cpp:301-314` builds a `glfw_pointer` lambda (cursor
>   position normalised against the window, plus the left button) and hands
>   it to `setup_runtime`; `app/run/editor.cpp:159-160` stops its own
>   `setup_runtime` call at the audio backend, so the `pointer` parameter
>   takes its empty default. `UiSystem` and `ClickNudgeSystem` therefore
>   receive nothing, and UI buttons, click-driven scripts and any
>   pointer-reading gameplay cannot be exercised in the editor at all --
>   only by `mosaic run --window`, which has no camera or object
>   manipulation. The two halves of testing a scene are in different
>   programs.
>
>   **Why this is not the one-line fix it looks like.** The editor renders
>   the scene into an *offscreen viewport texture* that ImGui composites
>   into a panel, so window-relative cursor coordinates are simply wrong:
>   they must be relative to that panel's rect, valid only while the cursor
>   is over it, and suppressed entirely while ImGui has captured the mouse
>   (dragging a gizmo, typing in a field). `main.cpp:299` also records that
>   `UiSystem` and `ClickNudgeSystem` **must agree about the pointer within
>   a tick, or the UI-claim arbitration breaks** -- so the editor needs one
>   shared provider too, not two call sites that happen to compute
>   something similar.
>
>   There is a second design question underneath: whether clicking in the
>   viewport should drive the *game's* pointer at all while the editor is
>   in edit mode, or only in play mode -- otherwise selecting an object
>   would also press whatever UI button is under the cursor. That is a
>   behaviour decision, not an implementation detail.

## Destroying an entity never destroys its physics body

- ~~**Destroying an entity never destroys its physics body**~~ — **SHIPPED
  2026-10-02.** `PhysicsBackend` gained a pure-virtual `destroy_body`, and
  `PhysicsSystem::update()` gained a reap as step 0 inside the existing lock:
  any entry in `bodies_` whose `RigidBody` has vanished loses its body and its
  three map entries. The predicate is "`RigidBody` is gone" rather than "the
  entity is invalid", which covers `destroy_entity` committing, `World::clear()`
  (which bypasses the deferred queue and runs no systems), and removing
  `RigidBody` from a living entity -- all three with one check. The Box3D side
  was the bulk of it: `handle == index + 1` in an insert-only vector, so a
  destroyed slot is tombstoned with `b3_nullBodyId` and all four handle-taking
  methods now route through one `live_body()` guard, where three of them
  previously checked only the range. Evidence: with the backend
  call dropped but the maps still erased, the call-counting tests fail merely
  because the call is absent while the stacked-boxes test fails
  `1.99934 < 1.49934` -- the upper box still propped up by a ghost. That
  asymmetry is why a positional test was written instead of a call-counting one.
  Original entry follows.

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **Destroying an entity never destroys its physics body.** Found 2026-10-01
>   while scoping the ContactEvent lock-ordering fix. Neither `PhysicsSystem` nor
>   `PhysicsBackend` has any body-removal path: `create_body` has no counterpart,
>   and `bodies_`, `handle_to_entity_` and `synced_pose_` are only ever inserted
>   into. So destroying a simulated entity leaks its Box3D body, which keeps
>   stepping and keeps generating contacts forever, while the three maps keep
>   stale entries that grow without bound over a long session. `update()` happens
>   to tolerate it -- step 2 and step 4 both `continue` when `get_component`
>   returns null, and step 5 drops contacts whose handles are unmapped -- which is
>   exactly why this has stayed invisible. Deferred 2026-10-01: the fix adds a
>   pure virtual (`destroy_body`) to `PhysicsBackend`, which every backend must
>   then implement, plus a decision about how `PhysicsSystem` learns an entity
>   died (poll the ECS each tick for vanished bodies, or an explicit
>   entity-destroyed event it subscribes to). That is an interface change and a
>   design question, not a patch. Noted now because the ContactEvent fix lets a
>   subscriber call back into `PhysicsSystem` for the first time, which makes
>   "handler destroys a colliding entity" a reachable shape rather than a
>   hypothetical one -- it is still safe today, but it is one step closer.

## The `[script]` SIGSEGV

- ~~**The `[script]` SIGSEGV**~~ — **ROOT-CAUSED AND FIXED 2026-10-03. It was
  never a flake, and the cause was written in our own comment.**
  `ensure_interpreter` noted that after init "the calling (main) thread holds
  the GIL ... no explicit GIL handoff" — so whoever won the `std::call_once`
  race kept the GIL for the process. The threaded script test passed alone
  because the `ScriptWorker` thread won and held it; it died after any other
  engine had been created on the main thread, because the worker then called
  `PyDict_New()` with no GIL. `ctest` never saw it because
  `catch_discover_tests` gives every `TEST_CASE` its own process, so the worker
  was always first — **the gate was green and blind at the same time.**
  Fixed by making the invariant real: no thread owns the GIL implicitly, and
  every entry into CPython acquires. Four things that were not obvious going
  in, each of which cost a failing-gate iteration: `globals_` had to become a
  `std::optional<py::dict>` in both engines, because a `py::dict` *member* is
  built before the constructor body and destroyed after the destructor body,
  leaving no scope in which to guard `PyDict_New`; `restricted_builtins`'
  function-local static `ProxyClasses` had to start leaking, because
  `__cxa_atexit` destroys it with no GIL and `dec_ref` aborts -- and that abort is a
  *debug* symptom only (`PYBIND11_ASSERT_GIL_HELD_INCREF_DECREF` is defined when
  `NDEBUG` is unset), so in a Release build the same path is a silent unguarded
  `Py_XDECREF`: the leak is **more** necessary there, not less; `trace_fn_` had
  to be emptied inside the guarded destructor, since a `py::` member outlives
  the body; and every direct pybind11 user outside the engines — the docs
  generator and four test files that build their own `py::dict` — had to
  acquire too. Evidence: `[script]` in one process is 5/5 clean where it was
  RC=139 every time, the whole binary now runs in a single process (1081
  cases, 31577 assertions) which the crash made impossible, and removing the
  release makes the new tests **deadlock** rather than crash.
  The entry that called this "unexplained" follows for context.

## `raycast()`'s script binding indexes its sequence arguments without a length check

- ~~**`raycast()`'s script binding indexes its sequence arguments without a
  length check**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-03.** It now uses the same `to_vec3`
  helper `apply_impulse` has used since #207, so a short sequence or a
  non-numeric element yields the miss dict the call already returns when no
  host callback is set, rather than raising out of pybind11 and aborting the
  tick. The host is deliberately not called at all on malformed input --
  zero-filling the missing components would hand the simulation a ray the
  script never asked for -- which the tests pin with a call counter. On the
  behaviour-change worry recorded below: what goes away is an *undocumented*
  exception. `raycast`'s `py::doc` promised only "hit data as a dictionary" and
  never promised to raise; it now states the miss-on-malformed rule explicitly.
  Each of the three new sections asserts both the miss AND that the script
  keeps running, and all three fail under the mutation that restores unguarded
  indexing. Original entry follows.

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **`raycast()`'s script binding indexes its sequence arguments without a
>   length check.** `python_script_engine.cpp`'s `raycast` reads
>   `origin[0..2]` and `direction[0..2]` directly, so a script passing a
>   two-element list raises out of pybind11 rather than returning a miss,
>   killing the tick. Found 2026-09-29 while adding `apply_impulse` (which
>   guards its own reads). Deferred: pre-existing, and tightening it would
>   change behaviour a script might already depend on, so it wants its own
>   change rather than riding along silently.

## Five small `ScriptWorker` tidy-ups found in review 2026-09-30

- ~~**Five small `ScriptWorker` tidy-ups found in review 2026-09-30**~~ —
  **ADDRESSED 2026-10-04, and two of the five were WRONG.** Checked each against
  the code rather than taking the entry at its word:

  **(a) "`host_` is now write-only" — FALSE.** It is read at
  `script_worker.cpp:19`, inside the recording `apply_impulse` lambda
  (`if (!host_.apply_impulse) { return false; }`). There is a live reader; the
  member is not dead. No change made.

  **(d) "`execute()` clears `pending_impulses_` after `std::move`-ing it, a
  no-op" — FALSE.** A moved-from `std::vector` is valid but *unspecified*, so the
  `clear()` guarantees empty instead of relying on an implementation detail. It is
  defensive and correct. No change made.

  **(b) "`has_pending()` has no callers" — true. Removed.** Grep found only its
  declaration and definition; public API with zero consumers and its correctness
  verified by nothing. The gate proved nothing used it.

  **(c) "`dispatch()` clears `result_ready_` unconditionally" — true, and now
  documented with two measurements rather than a vague comment.** The clear is
  deliberate, not vestigial: without it a `collect()` after two dispatches would
  return the STALE result while the newer job was still pending, which is worse
  than losing the older one. Two things were measured and are recorded in the
  header: the drop is reachable only by a race between `dispatch()` and the worker
  dequeuing, which came out **9 passes / 11 failures over 20 runs** — so a test of
  it would be flaky rather than load-bearing; and **removing the clear entirely
  leaves the whole suite green (1081/1081)**, so nothing pins it today. The
  precondition (collect before dispatch) is now stated as the contract.

  **(e)** stands as recorded: a script-spawned Python thread calling
  `apply_impulse` after `on_update` returns would race `execute()`'s tail, and
  `SnapshotContext` has the identical exposure, so it is not a new hazard class.

  Lesson worth keeping: this entry had been carried for five days with two
  incorrect claims in it. A recorded finding is a hypothesis until someone
  re-checks it against the code.

## `PhysicsSystem::update()` emits `ContactEvent` synchronously while holding `backend_mutex_`

- ~~**`PhysicsSystem::update()` emits `ContactEvent` synchronously while holding
  `backend_mutex_`**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-01.** `update()` now maps contacts
  into a local vector inside a braced block holding the lock, and emits after
  the block closes. The extent is delimited lexically on purpose: a `unique_lock`
  plus `unlock()` would have been a smaller diff but would leave the locked
  region ending at an unmarked statement, so a later edit adding a call-out above
  it would reintroduce exactly this bug. The pose write-back already preceded
  emission, so handler-visible ordering did not change -- the entry below, and the
  #209 summary it came from, were wrong to claim it would. Evidence: both new
  re-entrancy tests fail on a 3000 ms watchdog with the emit back under the lock
  and pass with it outside; the watchdog runs the tick on a detached thread so the
  failure is a named assertion in seconds rather than a 25-minute ctest stall.
  Original entry follows.

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **`PhysicsSystem::update()` emits `ContactEvent` synchronously while
>   holding `backend_mutex_` — a lock-ordering constraint, not just a
>   re-entrancy one.** Found 2026-09-30 in review of the mutex slice.
>   `EventBus::emit` dispatches immediate subscribers on the same stack, so a
>   subscriber runs inside the lock. The narrow risk is a subscriber that calls
>   `raycast`/`has_body`/`apply_impulse` and hangs on a plain `std::mutex`;
>   today's two subscribers were checked (one logs, one re-emits onto the
>   *deferred* bus) and neither does. The wider and already-live consequence is
>   that **every lock a synchronous subscriber takes is now ordered *under*
>   `backend_mutex_`** — which today includes the global logger mutex, since the
>   contact logger calls `log::info` once per contact inside the lock. Nothing
>   inverts that ordering right now, but it is an unwritten invariant: a future
>   log sink, or any sync subscriber holding a lock that another thread holds
>   while waiting on `backend_mutex_`, deadlocks in a way that looks nothing
>   like the re-entrancy case. There is a performance half to the same point: a
>   worker `raycast()` can wait behind per-contact logging and the `Table`
>   construction in the contact-to-`ScriptEvent` bridge — work with nothing to
>   do with the backend. The spec accepts holding the lock across ECS
>   traversal explicitly; it never considered holding it across subscriber
>   dispatch. Deferred 2026-09-30: the fix is to collect contacts under the
>   lock and emit them after releasing it, which changes when subscribers
>   observe a contact relative to the pose write-back — a behaviour decision
>   worth its own slice. A recursive mutex would hide the re-entrant hang
>   without making the read correct, and would do nothing about the ordering.

## A spurious watchdog trip in the contact-reentry tests degrades into a use-after-free

- ~~**A spurious watchdog trip in the contact-reentry tests degrades into a
  use-after-free**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-03.** The World and the state the
  handlers write are now heap-owned (`std::shared_ptr<World>` plus a `Probe`
  struct), and `tick_with_watchdog` captures both by value, so a thread that
  outlives the `TEST_CASE` keeps what it touches alive and they leak on a trip --
  self-healingly for a spurious slow-runner trip (the thread finishes late and
  drops its references), but for the rest of the test *process* on a genuine
  deadlock trip, since Catch2 keeps running later cases after a failed
  `REQUIRE`. Both are bounded by process exit, and `backend_mutex_` is
  per-instance, so a leaked parked thread cannot block the next case. **The entry below understated the hazard:** heap-allocating the World
  alone would not have fixed it, because the subscriber lambdas also captured
  `handler_calls`/`hit_the_box`/`pushed`/`box` by reference -- all `TEST_CASE`
  stack locals, reachable from inside the World's own EventBus. The ordering
  that makes the fix correct: `Probe` deliberately does NOT own the World, since
  the EventBus holds a lambda owning the Probe, and a Probe owning the World
  back would close a reference cycle and leak on the *passing* path too; the
  handlers hold World and PhysicsSystem as raw non-owning pointers instead.
  Measured both ways with a 50 ms watchdog against 4,000,000 ticks, so the
  thread is certainly still running when the case unwinds: before, RC=134
  (SIGABRT) and RC=139 (SIGSEGV), printing the assertion and *then* dying on a
  signal; after, **RC=1 five times out of five** with the clean named failure.
  Original entry follows.

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **A spurious watchdog trip in the contact-reentry tests degrades into a
>   use-after-free instead of the named failure it is designed to produce.**
>   Found 2026-10-01 in review of the contact-emit slice.
>   `tests/unit/physics/physics_contact_reentry_test.cpp`'s `tick_with_watchdog`
>   runs the tick on a detached thread capturing `&world`, so when the main
>   thread times out and `REQUIRE` throws, `world` is destroyed at scope exit
>   while that thread may still be using it. In the genuine-deadlock case the
>   thread is parked on a mutex that is then destroyed -- UB, though observed to
>   unwind cleanly and print the assertion. In the case the 3000 ms margin is
>   actually guarding against (a loaded runner, no deadlock), the thread is
>   *actively ticking* a destroyed `World`, so the run reports a segfault rather
>   than "the tick did not return" -- defeating the whole reason the watchdog was
>   chosen over a ctest `TIMEOUT`. Damage is bounded: `catch_discover_tests` gives
>   each case its own process, so it cannot take other cases down. Deferred
>   2026-10-01: the fix is to heap-allocate the `World` on the watchdog path and
>   deliberately leak it when the flag never sets, which turns both failure modes
>   into a clean named assertion. Not folded into that slice because it only
>   affects a failure path that the four-orders-of-magnitude timing margin makes
>   very unlikely, and leaking a `World` on purpose wants its own justification
>   in the test's comments rather than arriving as a fix-round edit.

## No ctest `TIMEOUT` property anywhere in the suite.

- ~~**No ctest `TIMEOUT` property anywhere in the suite.**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-07**
  as `catch_discover_tests(mosaic_unit_tests PROPERTIES TIMEOUT 60)`. The value is
  measured, not chosen by taste: over a full run the slowest single case is **1.11s**
  (`PluginFileWatcher.poll detects a modified file's mtime`, which waits out filesystem
  mtime granularity), the next tier is 0.40s (the IBL furnace cases baking a 64x64 BRDF
  LUT), and the whole suite is 24s — so 60s is ~54x the slowest case and still ~10x
  clear if CI runs 5x slower than this machine, while one case reaching 60s in a 24s
  suite is unambiguously wrong rather than merely slow. Verified with
  `ctest --show-only=json-v1`: all 1158 discovered tests carry `TIMEOUT 60.0`, rather
  than trusting that the CMake call did what its docs say. It does **not** replace the
  in-test watchdogs, which give a named assertion in seconds and work when running the
  binary directly; it is the backstop for the next hang nobody anticipated. Original
  entry follows.

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **No ctest `TIMEOUT` property anywhere in the suite.** Recorded 2026-10-01 as
>   a non-goal of the ContactEvent lock-ordering slice. `tests/CMakeLists.txt`
>   calls `catch_discover_tests(mosaic_unit_tests)` with no timeout, so ctest's
>   1500s default applies to every case in the suite: any test that hangs costs 25 minutes
>   of CI before it fails, and says only "Timeout" when it does. Deferred
>   2026-10-01 rather than folded into that slice, which solves its own hang with
>   an in-test watchdog instead -- the watchdog gives a named failure in seconds
>   and works when running the test binary directly, which a ctest property does
>   not. A suite-wide timeout is still worth having as a backstop for the next
>   hang nobody anticipated; it needs its own value chosen against the slowest
>   legitimate test rather than a number picked to fit one case.

## `PhysicsSystem::raycast is callable from another thread while update runs` is flaky on CI, and the flakiness is in the test's design.

- ~~**`PhysicsSystem::raycast is callable from another thread while update runs` is
  flaky on CI, and the flakiness is in the test's design.**~~ — **FIXED 2026-10-09**,
  in the same PR that recorded it. The case ran 200 main-thread ticks, set `stop`,
  joined the reader thread, and only *then* asserted `REQUIRE(hits.load() > 0)`.
  Nothing synchronised the two, so on a **2-core** GitHub runner with the main thread
  busy stepping box3d the reader could be starved for the entire run and `hits` stayed
  0 — failing a test whose actual claims (no deadlock, no crash, the ray hits) had all
  been met. Observed failing 2026-10-09 on a **docs-only** PR that changed no code, and
  **not reproducible on a 20-core machine in 35 runs**, including under deliberate CPU
  contention — which is the whole story: the reader always gets a core here and may get
  none there. **The fix removes the dependency on the scheduler rather than widening a
  tolerance:** the main thread now waits (bounded, 5s, uncontended so it completes in
  microseconds) for the reader to land its first hit *before* the tick loop, so
  `hits > 0` is established deterministically; the concurrent phase that follows asserts
  nothing a scheduler can zero out — reaching the join without a deadlock or crash *is*
  the claim; and the ray's geometry is re-asserted afterwards **from the main thread**,
  which cannot be starved. The 5s ceiling exists so a genuine failure reports instead of
  hanging, and its message distinguishes "the reader was never scheduled" from "raycast
  from a non-main thread returns no hit".

## Nine existing `TEST_CASE` names contain commas, so none of them can be run by name.

- ~~**Nine existing `TEST_CASE` names contain commas, so none of them can be run by
  name.**~~ — **WITHDRAWN 2026-10-09: the premise was wrong, and the count was wrong.**
  There are **89**, not nine (the original count came from grepping four files), and
  **they are not broken.** `CatchAddTests.cmake` escapes commas when it generates each
  `add_test`, which the generated
  `build/debug/tests/mosaic_unit_tests-*_tests.cmake` shows directly:
  `add_test( [==[... (0,0,0,1)]==] ... [==[... (0\,0\,0\,1)]==] )`. So every one of
  them runs correctly under `ctest`, and under a manual invocation that escapes the
  comma, and under `ctest -R`. Verified both ways 2026-10-09.
  **The defect was in my own tooling, not in the test names.** Catch2 treats a comma in
  a name filter as a test-spec separator, so the *unescaped* per-name invocation used by
  mutation-probe scripts matched nothing and exited non-zero — which the scripts read as
  a failing test. That cost a full probe round on the multi-scatter slice and two
  false-red columns on the exposure slice. The fix is to escape the comma (or filter by
  tag) in probe scripts, which is now recorded, and **not** to rename 89 well-written
  test names to work around it. Acting on the original entry would have been pure churn
  against a non-problem.
  **What survives as real:** a leading `--` and an unbalanced `[` in a `TEST_CASE` name
