  genuinely break ctest discovery, and those two are worth a guard — see the lint entry
  below.

## A guard against `TEST_CASE` names that break ctest discovery.

- ~~**A guard against `TEST_CASE` names that break ctest discovery.**~~ — **SHIPPED
  2026-10-09** as `tools/ci/check_test_case_names.py`, wired into
  `.githooks/pre-commit` and CI alongside the dependency lint, with 12 unit tests of
  its own. It rejects exactly two things, both silent when violated — the suite still
  builds and the damage appears as tests that *do not exist* rather than tests that
  fail: a name beginning with `--`, which ctest parses as a flag, and a name with an
  unbalanced `[`, which breaks `CatchAddTests.cmake`'s tag parsing. **The suite is
  currently clean, so this is a guard and not a cleanup** — it exists because both
  traps have cost this project real time before, and neither announces itself.
  **It deliberately does NOT reject commas,** and that non-rule has its own unit test
  so nobody "completes" the lint by adding one: `CatchAddTests.cmake` escapes commas
  when generating `add_test`, so comma-containing names run fine under ctest.

## A dict passed to `apply_impulse` raises instead of returning `False`

- ~~**A dict passed to `apply_impulse` raises instead of returning `False`**~~ —
  **SHIPPED 2026-10-04, for `raycast` too.** `to_vec3` now takes a `py::handle` and
  checks sequence-ness itself, and both bindings take `py::object` instead of
  `py::sequence`. The signature was the whole problem: with `py::sequence` there,
  pybind11's *dispatcher* rejected a non-sequence before the binding body ran, so the
  guard the spec relied on never got a chance. A dict, an int and `None` now all
  return `False`/a miss without aborting the tick.
  **Taken wider than recorded, deliberately:** the entry scoped this to
  `apply_impulse`, but #214 had just made `raycast` degrade for malformations *within*
  a sequence, so fixing only one would have left a dict returning `False` from one
  binding and raising from the other -- a new inconsistency rather than a closed one.
  Also documented at both surfaces, including the one imprecision that stays:
  `apply_impulse` under `--script-thread` returns `True` meaning "accepted for replay",
  not "a body was pushed", since the worker can only judge entities in its own job
  snapshot. That was a deliberate 2026-09-30 choice and remains so -- it was just
  undocumented at the binding. One behaviour is pinned as a two-layer guard rather than
  one: `str` passes `PySequence_Check`, so a string falls through to the element casts
  and fails there, which the tests now cover so the layers stay honest. Mutation:
  restoring `py::sequence` in either signature fails that binding's cases (3 and 4
  sections respectively). Original entry follows.

**Original backlog text** (an unstruck copy that sat under this entry's stub until 2026-10-23; moved here unedited so nothing is lost):

> - **A dict passed to `apply_impulse` raises instead of returning `False`.**
>   The spec says a malformed argument returns `False` rather than raising, but
>   that only covers malformations *within* a sequence. A non-sequence -- and
>   the single most likely wrong argument here is a **dict**, because this same
>   scripting surface uses dicts for component values (`tr['position']`) -- is
>   rejected by pybind11's dispatcher before the binding's own guard runs, so
>   `apply_impulse(e, {'x':1,'y':0,'z':0}, ...)` raises and aborts that
>   script's `on_update` for the tick. Found 2026-09-30 in review. Deferred:
>   it mirrors `raycast`'s existing behaviour exactly, and tightening it means
>   taking `py::object` and hand-rolling the sequence check for both bindings,
>   which is its own change.

## The default-scene HUD-underlay case takes 16.5 s locally and 23.4 s on CI.

- ~~**The default-scene HUD-underlay case takes 16.5 s locally and 23.4 s on CI.**~~
  — **FIXED and ROOT-CAUSED 2026-10-21.**
  `the default scene's HUD underlay image draws beneath the checker`
  (`tests/unit/app/demo_scene_test.cpp`) was the only case in the suite that
  renders a frame, and `setup_runtime` registers the demo environment as a lazy
  asset whose loader bakes the full-size IBL products (equirect -> 512 cubemap,
  irradiance, prefiltered chain) on the FIRST FRAME with a device, plus the BRDF
  LUT factory in the same branch. Fix: the case now calls
  `runtime.render_system->set_environment_asset({})` (an existing public
  setter) before rendering; its claim, draws and assertions are unchanged, and
  there is no production change. **Measured, local, 20 cores, debug:** 14.2 s
  -> 0.17 s under `ctest -j20`; the suite's new longest case is
  `PluginFileWatcher.poll detects a modified file's mtime` at 1.11 s. **Probed:** deleting the image pass's
  `stable_sort`, flipping its comparator, and moving the underlay to
  `order = 1` each turn the case RED both before and after the fix.
  **What this entry got wrong:** it said the cause was "not yet diagnosed, so
  the effort is a guess", and guessed "`render()` on the default scene in a
  debug build" and "test or render-path work"; the cause was a known, documented
  lazy bake that only this case forces, and the fix was a one-line test change.
  Its "7 ms" for sibling cases was also off: the nearest sibling that calls the
  same `setup_runtime` takes 0.18 s. No other case in the suite pays the bake
  (the full-suite junit has nothing else above 1.2 s). The parallel CI step's
  prediction is in `docs/project/next-steps.md`.

The prediction that "the work is choosing the value, not writing the line" was exactly right: the line took seconds, the value came from timing a full run (slowest case 1.11s, suite 24s, replacing ctest's silent 1500s default). |

## Tighten the "position starts past the edge" test to exact values

| Item | Why it is a quick win |
|---|---|
| ~~**Tighten the "position starts past the edge" test to exact values**~~ | **SHIPPED 2026-10-12**, and the prediction was exactly right: the assertions took a minute, choosing the probe was the work. The old bounds (`u0 <= 1`, `u1 <= 1 + eps`, `u1 >= u0`) passed at `u0 = 0.984`, which is what an upper bound written `tw - 1` produces — so that off-by-one is the **defined** probe, and it is now red on both axes. A second section at 64x32 was added because at 64x64 a clamp measuring y against the *width* is undetectable. **Confirmed UB trap, worth remembering:** mutating the position clamp away entirely, or clamping y against the width, makes the next line `std::clamp(v, lo, hi)` with `lo > hi` — one of those aborted on a libstdc++ debug assertion rather than returning a wrong value. The real code never trips it, because `x1`'s `lo` is the already-clamped `x0`; but a probe of this function must mutate a *bound*, never remove the clamp. |

## Warn when a sub-rect lands entirely off its texture

| Item | Why it is a quick win |
|---|---|
| ~~**Warn when a sub-rect lands entirely off its texture**~~ | **SHIPPED 2026-10-12, and this row under-rated it.** "One diagnostic at the `resolve_image_quad` seam" is the one place it cannot go: that is a pure header-only function the render pass calls for every image every frame, so a `log::warn` there would fire 60 times a second with no ledger within reach — engine code cannot see the studio-side one. The shape that works is the pattern the order diagnostics established: a pure `sub_rect_off_texture` predicate in `engine/ui/ui_fit.hpp` beside the clamp it describes, plus a loop in `app/run/editor.cpp` where the asset registry actually is, de-duplicated through `WarningLedger`. A slice rather than a one-liner. The *warning-not-behaviour-change* call in this row was right and was kept. **One asymmetry worth knowing:** a NEGATIVE `sub_rect_pos` is not a finding — the lower clamp pulls it back on-texture, so it samples a shifted-but-valid region instead of collapsing. |

## Sort the HUD image pass by `UiImage::order`

| Item | Why it is a quick win |
|---|---|
| ~~**Sort the HUD image pass by `UiImage::order`**~~ | **SHIPPED 2026-10-11, image AND text together.** Two of this row's own claims were wrong. It was **not** "a copy of a shipped loop": the panel pass batches every quad into ONE `draw_ui` call after sorting, while the image and text passes draw per entity with their own texture/atlas handles — so each needed a collect → sort → emit reshape, not a copied `stable_sort`. And `order` was **not** "documented" on either component: both fields carried no comment at all, unlike `UiPanel::order`. The semantics are now written down, including that the order cannot cross a pass boundary. |

## Pointer input from the editor viewport to the scene

The panel-relative transform turned out to already exist, for edit-mode selection — which now needs gating on `!is_playing()`. Original entry: | Not the one-line pass-through it resembles. The scene renders to an offscreen texture composited by ImGui, so coordinates must be panel-relative, gated on hover, and suppressed while ImGui holds the mouse; `UiSystem` and `ClickNudgeSystem` must share one provider or the UI-claim arbitration breaks. And it needs a behaviour decision first — whether a viewport click drives the game's pointer in edit mode at all, or only in play mode. High impact: today, manipulating a scene and exercising its scripts are in two different programs. |

The gate was what the entry predicted — 1358/1358 says nothing about the image, and a validation-layer grep plus a human at the window are the real checks. Original entry: || New render target, new pass, new pipeline, and a decision about where UI draws relative to the resolve. Bounded new machinery rather than a quick win, and it is the prerequisite for bloom. **Trigger, not preference:** the moment any additive-blending pipeline is added, in-shader tone mapping stops being defensible and this becomes necessary. |

## ENet servers take a fixed port; the structural fix is bind 0 plus a `port()` accessor.

- ~~**ENet servers take a fixed port; the structural fix is bind 0 plus a `port()` accessor.**~~
  **SHIPPED 2026-10-23.** All six cases now bind 0 and read the kernel's choice back; the six
  constants and the comment describing the mitigation are gone, so there is no fixed port left in
  that file. **The decision this depended on went the other way, and that is the useful part:**
  `port()` did NOT go on `engine/net/transport.hpp`. That interface is deliberately backend-free
  and a port is meaningless for `LoopbackTransport`, so widening it to suit one backend was the
  wrong trade. The module gained `EnetServer : public Transport` instead, and
  `create_enet_server` returns that; the one production caller (`app/main.cpp:211`) assigns into a
  `shared_ptr<Transport>` and compiles unchanged. **Also wrong in this entry's own framing:** no
  extra ENet call was needed to learn the bound port -- `enet_host_create` already queries the
  socket back into `host->address` (`enet/host.c:68`), so `host->address.port` is the kernel's
  choice. Original entry: the six constants were moved out of the ephemeral range
  (34001-34006 -> 24101-24106) in the `ctest -j` slice, because a hard-coded port in that range had
  already failed a real CI run serially (`live_link_server_test.cpp:185-189`, "34906 was not
  free"). That was a mitigation, not a fix: any fixed number can still be taken.

## `ctest -j` in CI

- ~~**`ctest -j` in CI**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-21 (#273)** (spec
  `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-21-ci-parallel-ctest-design.md`). **The framing this entry used
  to carry ("gated on two flaky tests") was stale and has been replaced.** Verified 2026-10-21:
  (1) the threaded-raycast case was fixed in #230 (`e7927cf`, 2026-10-09: a bounded 5 s wait for
  `hits > 0` before the concurrent phase); (2) the threaded-`ScriptSystem` SIGSEGV cannot occur on
  the ctest path at all, because `catch_discover_tests` registers all 1455 cases as separate
  processes, so the full-suite ordering that triggers it never happens under ctest at any `-j`
  (it remains a hazard of running `mosaic_unit_tests` directly; see the Physics-pillar entry "The `[script]` SIGSEGV now has a deterministic, one-command reproduction").
  The real gates are the concurrency hazards of 1455 independent processes and the per-test
  `TIMEOUT 60` against one slow case; the spec audits both. With a restored ccache a warm job is
  ~93-143 s and the serial `ctest` step is 35-64 s of it (CI log 2026-10-21: 60.8 s, image
  `20261012.334.1`, nproc 4, gcc 13.3.0), which compiler caching can never touch. **What the win is:**
  the step cannot drop below its longest single case (23.4 s on that CI run, see the HUD-underlay
  entry below, fixed 2026-10-21), so the predicted step is ~26 s (pre-registered in the spec; 10.7 s of work is dispatched first,
  then the long case runs 23.4 s), not 60/4; the rest of a warm run is apt
  (~25-30 s) and configure (~23 s), and **neither is test parallelism**.

- ~~**`enet_transport_test.cpp` hard-codes six UDP ports too** (34001-34006).~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-24 (#284).** Older twin of the CI-section ENet entry; no fixed port is left in that file.

  covers the TCP side only. Same failure signature is available to it: a fixed
  port colliding with anything else on a shared CI runner. Not folded into that
  slice because the fix is materially bigger — ENet's `enet_host_create` accepts
  port 0 and records the kernel's choice in `host->address.port`, but Mosaic's
  transport sits behind its own neutral interface, so learning the bound port
  means adding an accessor to that interface and threading it through the
  backend. Deferred 2026-09-30: a one-file test change on the TCP side versus an

## The editor should start STOPPED, not playing.

- ~~**The editor should start STOPPED, not playing.**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-10 (#235).** `PlayState`'s default was
  `playing_ = true` (`studio/play_state.hpp:27`), so `mosaic editor` begins
  simulating the moment it opens. **Requested 2026-10-10 after the viewport
  pointer-input acceptance pass**, where the default surfaced as the first thing
  a user meets: because viewport clicks are routed to the game while playing,
  the default click behaviour is now "drive the game" and click-to-select
  requires pressing Stop first. The product is editor-facing, so the default
  should be authoring, not simulating — the user's call, and the right one.
  **It is a one-token change plus one trap.** The token:
  `playing_ = false`. The test: `tests/unit/studio/play_state_test.cpp:7`
  ("PlayState defaults to playing") inverts, name included.
  **THE TRAP — `mosaic editor --ticks N` would never terminate.**
  `FramePump` exits on `loop_.tick_count() >= *options_.ticks`
  (`app/run/frame_pump.hpp:87`), and `RuntimeLoop::advance` returns early while
  stopped (`engine/runtime/runtime_loop.hpp:29`) so `tick_count_` never
  increments. A stopped editor therefore hangs forever instead of exiting after
  N ticks. **CI is unaffected** — its smoke test runs `mosaic run`, not `mosaic
  editor` — but `--ticks` is exactly how the editor gets verified in a sandbox
  that cannot screenshot, which is how every editor change this month was
  checked. Needs a decision, not just a fix: either `--ticks` counts *frames*
  in the editor path, or `--ticks` implies play, or the editor gains a separate
  frame limit. Picking one is the actual work here.
  **Consequences that are CORRECT and must not be "fixed" on sight:** the demo
  scene sits still until Play (physics and scripts do not step — that is what
  stopped means); the Blender live-link push is gated on `is_playing()`
  (`studio/studio_app.cpp:246`) so it stays quiet until Play; the Step button
  becomes enabled at startup, which is right. Transform propagation already runs
  every frame regardless of play state, so Inspector edits still appear
  immediately — the editor will not look frozen.

U6 (interaction depth) is first and carries one open question for a human; U7 (image presentation) is independent and needs no decision; U10 is blocked on an editor keyboard-input provider. | High if interactive-game UI is a near-term goal, else Low — **and that is still the open question**, which the decomposition does not answer. It only removes the excuse that the item was too shapeless to start. |
| Networking gaps | High if shipping real multiplayer soon, else Low |
| Package manager (network/deps/registry) | Medium |
| Public marketplace | Low today, High later — gated on an actual user base |
| 3rd-person adventure template | Medium |

## The 2026-10-23 tier re-derivation, and what it found about the backlog itself

**SHIPPED 2026-10-23 (#285).** The ask was to re-rank the "Effort vs. impact" tiers after
sixteen PRs. The ranking landed — 33 rows carrying an effort class (hours / slice /
multi-slice), a gate, and a cited source, plus a **"Decided: no action" table with the
trigger that would reopen each row**, which is the part most likely to still be useful in
six months: it answers "did we already consider this?" without anyone reading a postmortem.

**The larger finding was that the backlog was misreporting its own state.** Nineteen entries
read as pending work that had already shipped: **fourteen** were live bullets sitting directly
under their own struck stub, orphaned by #280's status-based migration the night before, and
**five** had never been struck at all — including "No CI exists in this repo at all" (#198,
2026-09-21) and "The editor should start STOPPED, not playing" (`play_state.hpp:31` has read
`playing_ = false` since #235). The brief named one of them. A documentation tool that
classifies on a leading `~~` cannot see an unstruck original, which is now a backlog item.

**Predictions, against what happened.** Live bullets: predicted 147 → 135, achieved 147 → 131,
because a title-only scan missed five duplicates and two shipped leftovers; a prefix-key scan
found them. The backlog shrank to 150 KB against a predicted ~158 KB. `2026-09.md` grew +17 KB
against a predicted +3 KB — the collapsed history was consistently larger than estimated, in
the same direction both times.

**Three supervision findings, the reason this entry exists at all.**

1. **The delegate's oracles were scratch scripts, never committed.** Conservation and dead-link
   evidence existed only as pasted output. Rebuilding both independently is what caught the rest
   of this list; re-running them was not possible. Recorded as a backlog item that says plainly
   the checks are unreproducible, so whoever promotes them to CI knows they are rebuilding.
2. **A check whose scope is narrower than the property it proves.** The delegate's link checker
   honestly reported "63 anchors checked, 0 dead" — it scanned one file. A check over all of
   `docs/` finds **80 anchors and 10 dead**. The same 10 are dead on `main`, so they predate the
   pass; they are tracked, not fixed here. Promoted upstream into
   `supervise-delegated-work@1.2.0`.
3. **A derived count in prose rots immediately.** The section's own live-bullet figure was
   written exactly twice and was stale both times, the second time because the commit that
   corrected it added a bullet. It is now approximate ("roughly 130") with a line saying why, on
   the same reasoning that removed the `main` SHA row from `next-steps.md` in #283. **Do not
   turn it back into an exact number.**

**Unverified, deliberately:** roughly 100 older live bullets in the subsystem sections are
**not** ranked. They carry no evidence from the sixteen PRs, and the backlog's own standing rule
forbids re-deriving from unchanged source docs. Ranking them is its own backlog item.

## `draw_particles` and `draw_ribbons` share the world-text backend shape.

- ~~**`draw_particles` and `draw_ribbons` share the world-text backend shape.**~~ — **SHIPPED
  2026-10-23.** `VulkanRenderDevice::draw_particles` and `draw_ribbons` (`modules/render_vulkan/vulkan_render_device.cpp`)
  now stage into per-frame, per-call `std::vector<InstanceBuffer>` chunk lists (`particle_instances_`
  indexed by `particle_calls_[frame_]++`, `ribbon_vertices_` indexed by `ribbon_calls_[frame_]++`),
  reset in `begin_frame()` after the fence wait alongside `world_text_calls_[frame_] = 0`, with
  nested buffer/memory teardown loops in the destructor — matching the #275 `draw_world_text`
  pattern. **This is latent-shape removal with no observable rendering change:** `RenderSystem`
  already accumulates one `instances` vector across every `ParticleEmitter` and calls
  `draw_particles` once per frame outside the `for_each` (`engine/render/render_system.hpp:380`),
  and accumulates one `ribbon` vector across every `TrailRenderer` and calls `draw_ribbons` once
  per frame outside the `for_each` (`:397`).
  **What the backlog row got wrong (the gate):** both the 🟢 Quick wins row and `next-steps.md`
  stated the gate as "a test that fails on the old batching". **That gate was not achievable:**
  unlike world text before #275, the engine-side batching was already a single call per frame for
  both particles and ribbons, so there was no engine-side batching defect to make red, and nothing
  in the suite creates a `VkDevice`, so the Vulkan backend cannot be executed by a behavioural test.
  **What replaced it:** a source-level contract test (`"every per-frame staging buffer is chunked per call"`
  in `tests/unit/render/texture_colorspace_contract_test.cpp`) over `read_backend()`, which fails
  red against unmodified `vulkan_render_device.cpp` (`REQUIRE( body.find("chunks.emplace_back();") != std::string::npos )`
  with `method: void draw_particles(`) and passes once all three draw methods take a per-call chunk,
  the single-buffer aliases are gone, all three counters reset in `begin_frame`, and the members are
  declared as `std::vector<InstanceBuffer>` chunk lists. Suite 1461 -> 1462, all passing.
  Original Rendering bullet: Each writes one per-frame vertex/instance buffer at offset 0 on the CPU
  while the draw executes at submit, and each is correct only because `RenderSystem` calls it at
  most once per frame (found by the 2026-10-21 world-text audit; world text had the same assumption
  and broke it). Not a live bug. The fix is the per-call chunk list `draw_world_text` now uses, or
  an assert. Deferred: unreachable today and untestable without a `VkDevice`. Depends on nothing.

| Order | Item | Effort / impact | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ~~**`draw_particles` and `draw_ribbons` share the world-text backend shape.**~~ | Hours. Latent correctness: both alias one per-frame buffer and are right only because `RenderSystem` calls each once. Same class as the world-text bug fixed in #275. | ~~A test that fails on the old batching; the Vulkan side is checkable by eye only (nothing creates a `VkDevice`).~~ **Corrected 2026-10-24:** not achievable (engine-side batching was already one call per frame, and no test creates a `VkDevice`); replaced by a source-level contract test (`"every per-frame staging buffer is chunked per call"`) via `read_backend()`. | PR #275 |

## `execution.jobs` in the `debug` test preset (local `ctest -j`)

- ~~**`execution.jobs` in the `debug` test preset (local `ctest -j`).**~~ — **SHIPPED
  2026-10-24.** `CMakePresets.json`'s `debug` test preset now carries `"execution": { "jobs": 8 }`,
  so a bare `ctest --preset debug` runs parallel locally. CI is unaffected: its step passes
  `-j"$(nproc)"` explicitly, and an explicit flag overrides the preset field.

  **The decision was the human's and was delegated back with the measurements in hand.** The
  backlog had this as *decision-gated* ("a human's yes/no") because it changes every
  developer's default on machines of unknown memory. Asked, the human declined to pick blind
  and delegated the call. Measured instead (nproc=20, suite 1462, machine otherwise idle,
  3 runs per point, median):

  | `-j` | median wall | speedup |
  |---|---|---|
  | 1 | 26.44 s | 1.0x |
  | 2 | 13.42 s | 2.0x |
  | 4 | 6.99 s | 3.8x |
  | 6 | 5.05 s | 5.2x |
  | 8 | 3.92 s | 6.7x |
  | 12 | 3.28 s | 8.1x |
  | 20 | 2.93 s | 9.0x |

  **Why 8 and not `nproc`:** `execution.jobs` takes a literal integer -- there is no `nproc`
  expansion in a CTest preset -- so "nproc-wide" would mean hard-coding one machine's core
  count into a file every machine reads. That ruled out the maximum on portability grounds
  before memory was even considered. 8 then captures 6.7x of the available 9.0x (75% of the
  benefit); past it each added job buys under 0.7 s.

  **Why memory was not the binding constraint, contrary to the deferral's stated reason.**
  The deferral cited "machines with unknown memory". Measured: the heaviest single test
  process peaks at **~85 MB RSS** (typical ~19 MB), so 8 concurrent cases cost ~0.7 GB of
  private memory, safe on a 4 GB machine. The 298 MB test binary is mapped and its text is
  shared across the concurrent processes rather than duplicated. **The recorded
  `mosaic_unit_tests` memory-pressure landmine is about the LINK step** -- one linker process
  -- **and does not apply to `ctest -jN` at all.** That is the deferral's reasoning corrected,
  not just its outcome.

  **Gate (the field had to visibly move, not merely not-break):** a bare
  `ctest --preset debug` with no flag ran **3.94 s** against **28.23 s** for an explicit
  `-j1`, a 7.2x speedup, matching the `-j8` row above. A `-j8` timing taken later in the same
  session read 12.12 s and is **discarded, not averaged**: a 20-core build had started in
  another worktree, and that is an environment change rather than noise.

  **Also in this slice: the `debug` and `release` configure presets now pin
  `"generator": "Ninja"`.** They declared no generator, so `cmake --preset debug` silently
  produced Unix Makefiles while every documented command says `-G Ninja`. The two disagree
  destructively: the handoff's own verify command `cmake --preset debug -G Ninja` **fails**
  against a tree already configured the other way ("does not match the generator used
  previously"), which is how this was found -- the State table's command did not run on a
  working checkout. With the pin, a bare `cmake --preset debug` yields Ninja (verified:
  `CMAKE_GENERATOR:INTERNAL=Ninja`) and the `-G Ninja` in the documented command is
  redundant rather than load-bearing.

  **Cost of the pin, stated plainly:** an existing `build/debug` configured with Makefiles now
  fails loudly instead of silently using the wrong generator. That is the better failure, but
  it is a failure: the fix is to delete `build/debug` once. Recorded as a landmine.
## A per-test duration budget.

- ~~**A per-test duration budget.**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-24.** Enforced as observation **O3** inside `tools/ci/check_ctest_run.py`'s `check_run` (`MAX_CASE_SECONDS = 10.0`), with `python3 tools/ci/check_ctest_run.py --report "${JUNIT}"` added to `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s "Unit tests" step so the longest case and sum of durations are visible in every CI log rather than only when the budget trips.
  - **Measured distribution (2026-10-24, nproc=20, suite 1462, machine otherwise idle, 4 runs alternating `-j20` and `-j8`, worst value per case taken):** `median per-case 0.019 s | p95 0.13 s | p99 0.37 s | max 1.13 s`; cases over 1 s: 1 (`PluginFileWatcher.poll detects a modified file's mtime`, 1.13 s); cases over 2 s: 0; cases over 5 s: 0.
  - **Why 10.0 s:** 8.8x the worst observed local case (1.13 s), 9.0x the measured CI worst case (1.11 s: every one of the 1462 per-case durations was read out of PR #287's CI log, and CI is not slower per case because the slowest case waits on an mtime change rather than burning CPU), and 1/6 of the `TIMEOUT 60` hang backstop — so a case that grows 9x trips the budget instead of needing 53x to trip the timeout.
  - **Why fail rather than annotate:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` already documents an expected yellow warning annotation that must not be acted on (the duplicate-key ccache save on re-runs), so a warning-only budget would be trained-away noise. With 8.8x headroom, a trip means something real changed.
  - **The stale `TIMEOUT` rationale corrected in `tests/CMakeLists.txt`:** lines 247-253 still stated that since #241 the slowest case was `the default scene's HUD underlay image draws beneath the checker` at 16.5 s locally and 23.4 s on CI (~2.6x headroom against `TIMEOUT 60`, not 54x). That regression was fixed in #274: case #413 now runs in **0.18 s** (the comment's stated duration for it was wrong by ~90x), and the slowest case in the suite is 1.13 s, putting real headroom back at ~53x. The comment's own next sentence read *"A comment whose job is 'nothing is wrong here' must not predict the wrong margin"* — leaving it untouched would have preserved the exact defect it warned about.
  - **Original backlog entry:** `TIMEOUT 60` is a hang backstop and says nothing about a case that quietly grows 40x (the HUD-underlay case did, unseen, for weeks). A CI step that reads `ctest --output-junit` and fails or annotates any case above a stated fraction of the timeout would catch the next one. Deferred: needs a threshold chosen from CI data, and a flaky budget is worse than none. The HUD-underlay case it would have tripped on day one is fixed (2026-10-21; slowest case is now ~1.1 s), so this is unblocked.

## A checker for `next-steps.md`'s verifiable claims.

- ~~**A checker for `next-steps.md`'s verifiable claims.**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-24.**
  `tools/docs/check_next_steps_claims.py` (with unit tests in
  `tools/docs/test_check_next_steps_claims.py`) parses the `## State` table in
  `docs/project/next-steps.md` and fails CI on drift. Wired into `.github/workflows/ci.yml`
  as two steps: the checker's own unit tests beside the early lint self-tests, and the live
  `next-steps claims check` step after `Unit tests` consuming
  `--junit "${RUNNER_TEMP}/ctest-junit.xml"`.
  - **What it checks (`O1`–`O4`):**
    - `O1` — the document has a non-empty `## State` table, and every row's `Claim` is
      either in `HANDLERS` or explicitly listed in `UNVERIFIABLE_CLAIMS`.
    - `O2` (`Suite size`) — the integer test count in `Value` (`1462 tests`) equals the
      discovered count (`<testcase>` elements in `--junit`, or `Total Tests:` from
      `ctest --preset debug -N`).
    - `O3` (`Suite status`) — `Value` states `all passing` and both numbers in `N/N`
      (`1462/1462`) equal the actual testcase count, and every testcase in the suite passed.
    - `O4` (`Build`) — does not run a build; verifies that every `--preset <name>` in
      `Verify with` resolves to an existing preset in `CMakePresets.json`
      (`configurePresets` for `cmake --preset`, `buildPresets` for
      `cmake --build --preset`).
  - **Why an unrecognised claim is RED (no fallback / no skip-by-default):** a new row added
    to `## State` without either a handler in `HANDLERS` or an entry in
    `UNVERIFIABLE_CLAIMS` fails `O1` immediately, and an empty or missing `## State` table
    also fails `O1`. A skip-by-default fallback would let a new or renamed row pass
    vacuously while appearing enforced.
  - **What it deliberately does NOT check, and why:**
    - `CI test step` is declared in `UNVERIFIABLE_CLAIMS` (`"needs \`gh\` and a specific run id (network + authenticated token)"`) and reported in the summary line as skipped with that reason rather than silently ignored.
    - The timing figures (`2.7 s local` in `Suite status`, `~12-17 s` in `CI test step`) are deliberately not asserted: they are environment-dependent, and asserting them across machines or runners would turn the guard flaky (recorded as declined in the backlog's "Decided: no action" table unless a stable timing environment exists).
    - The `Checked` date column is not checked for staleness against git history (deferred as its own backlog item).
  - **Original backlog entries:**
    - *Documentation tooling:* The design makes every claim carry the command that regenerates it (`ctest --preset debug -N` beside a test count), so a reader can check one by hand. A tool that runs them all and fails on drift is a real follow-up — it would have caught the six-week-stale gate that misled a reader on 2026-10-21 — but the right time to build it is after the file's shape has settled.
    - *🟡 Medium row 1:* Slice. Medium impact: this very pass found the handoff's "twelve PRs" wrong at the time it was written (it is 16, or 18 counting #265-#284). Gate: must go red against a deliberately wrong claim. Source: PR #278.
## Pin mutable FetchContent tags to commit SHAs.

- ~~**Pin mutable FetchContent tags to commit SHAs.**~~ — **SHIPPED 2026-10-24.**
  - **The backlog's stated trade-off was false for the 9 tag-backed dependencies on CMake 4.4.3 + GitHub.** The row claimed *"`GIT_SHALLOW` needs a tag, so SHA pinning trades shallow clones away unless done per dependency."* Measured on CMake 4.4.3 against these GitHub remotes: (1) `git fetch --depth 1 origin <bare SHA>` against GitHub succeeds (0.33 s) because GitHub permits fetching any reachable SHA directly; (2) a `FetchContent_Declare` with `GIT_TAG <40-hex SHA>` and `GIT_SHALLOW TRUE` performs its `"download step (git clone)"` and produces a genuinely shallow clone (`git rev-parse --is-shallow-repository` -> `true`, `git log --oneline | wc -l` -> `1`, `.git` 2.5 MB). All 9 previously tag-pinned dependencies now carry both a 40-character lowercase commit SHA and `GIT_SHALLOW TRUE`, with the human-readable version kept as a trailing comment on the `GIT_TAG` line.
  - **One sharper finding from the clean-configure gate on `box3d` (`30c67b5e6d0a3a66f0f506c69ce9e9e0587e3b7c`):** CMake 4.4.3's `gitclone.cmake.in` implements `GIT_SHALLOW TRUE` via `git clone --no-checkout --depth 1 --no-single-branch` followed by `git checkout <GIT_TAG> --` (rather than `git fetch --depth 1 origin <SHA>`). That fetches depth-1 of every advertised branch and tag ref (`refs/heads/*`, `refs/tags/*`), which includes every commit SHA pointed to by a tag (and fails loudly at `git checkout <SHA>` if an upstream tag is ever moved or deleted, instead of silently building the new tag target). However, `box3d`'s `30c67b5e...` is an **untagged mid-history commit** behind `main` (`f36d061b...`), so `--depth 1 --no-single-branch` does not receive that object and `box3d-populate-gitclone.cmake:66` failed with `fatal: unable to read tree (30c67b5e6d0a3a66f0f506c69ce9e9e0587e3b7c)`. `box3d` therefore stays SHA-pinned without `GIT_SHALLOW` (its full history up to `HEAD` is 40 commits total), and its comment in `CMakeLists.txt` was replaced to state the exact `gitclone.cmake.in` mechanism and explicitly warn against citing commit-SHA pinning as a reason to drop `GIT_SHALLOW` on tagged dependencies.
  - **Resolved tag -> commit SHA table (`git ls-remote <repo> refs/tags/<tag> refs/tags/<tag>^{}`, taking `^{}` for the three annotated tags `Catch2`, `glfw`, `imgui`):**

    | Dependency | Old `GIT_TAG` | Tag type | Resolved 40-hex commit SHA | `is-shallow` | `commits` |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | `Catch2` | `v3.5.2` | annotated (`fc942466...`) | `05e10dfccc28c7f973727c54f850237d07d5e10f` | `true` | `1` |
    | `tomlplusplus` | `v3.4.0` | lightweight | `30172438cee64926dc41fdd9c11fb3ba5b2ba9de` | `true` | `1` |
    | `nlohmann_json` | `v3.11.3` | lightweight | `9cca280a4d0ccf0c08f47a99aa71d1b0e52f8d03` | `true` | `1` |
    | `CLI11` | `v2.4.2` | lightweight | `6c7b07a878ad834957b98d0f9ce1dbe0cb204fc9` | `true` | `1` |
    | `glfw` | `3.4` | annotated (`a74efa0d...`) | `7b6aead9fb88b3623e3b3725ebb42670cbe4c579` | `true` | `1` |
    | `box3d` | `30c67b5e...` (already SHA) | untagged commit | `30c67b5e6d0a3a66f0f506c69ce9e9e0587e3b7c` | `false` | `40` |
    | `miniaudio` | `0.11.21` | lightweight | `4a5b74bef029b3592c54b6048650ee5f972c1a48` | `true` | `1` |
    | `enet` | `v1.3.18` | lightweight | `2662c0de09e36f2a2030ccc2c528a3e4c9e8138a` | `true` | `1` |
    | `imgui` | `v1.91.5-docking` | annotated (`97fa363a...`) | `368123ab06b2b573d585e52f84cd782c5c006697` | `true` | `1` |
    | `pybind11` | `v2.13.6` | lightweight | `a2e59f0e7065404b44dfe92a28aca47ba1378dc4` | `true` | `1` |

  - **Standing CI gate:** `tools/ci/check_dependency_pins.py` + `tools/ci/test_check_dependency_pins.py` (8 unit tests covering clean 40-hex lowercase SHA with version comment, mutable tag, uppercase SHA, truncated SHA, missing `GIT_TAG`, file with no `FetchContent_Declare` blocks, and missing CMake file so a broken regex cannot pass vacuously), wired into `.github/workflows/ci.yml` beside `check_dependency_rules.py`. Against unmodified `CMakeLists.txt`, `check_dependency_pins.py` exited 1 and named all 9 tag-pinned dependencies (`9 violation(s).`); after the edit it exits 0.
  - **Clean build and test gate:** `rm -rf build/debug && cmake --preset debug && cmake --build --preset debug -j"$(nproc)" && ctest --preset debug -j"$(nproc)"` configured from scratch in 15.1 s, built all 514 targets cleanly, and passed **1462 / 1462** tests (`Total Test time (real) = 3.49 sec`).
  - **Original backlog entry:** Found while designing: 9 of 10 dependencies are pinned by tag (box3d alone by SHA). A retagged upstream would change a build silently. Unrelated to caching but it changes the trust model of any dependency cache; GIT_SHALLOW needs a tag or branch, so pinning to SHAs would trade away shallow clones unless done per dependency. **Sharpened by the shallow clones (Slice A):** if an upstream tag is ever moved, a developer with an existing shallow `build/*/_deps` can hit a confusing fetch failure, because the shallow history does not contain the new commit. CI is immune (it always clones fresh). Recovery is `rm -rf build/*/_deps` and reconfigure; that is noted beside the declarations in `CMakeLists.txt`. Documented rather than fixed in that slice. This strengthens the case for SHA pinning.

## Reduce header fan-out / precompiled headers / unity builds

**2026-10-24.** The BEFORE half of this row was measured the day before; this is the
AFTER half, the decision between PCH and unity builds, and the ruling on the risk.

**Shipped: a stdlib-only PCH on four targets.** One `MOSAIC_PCH_HEADERS` list in the
root `CMakeLists.txt`, applied `PRIVATE` to `mosaic_unit_tests`, `mosaic_engine`,
`mosaic_app` and `mosaic_studio`. Sixteen headers, chosen from measured inclusion
counts across the repo rather than taste: `<string>` (180 files), `<vector>` (143),
`<cstdint>` (111), `<memory>` (67), then `<cmath>`, `<algorithm>`, `<filesystem>`,
`<utility>`, `<optional>`, `<functional>`, `<unordered_map>`, `<array>`, `<cstddef>`,
`<string_view>`, `<chrono>`, `<stdexcept>`. `<fstream>`/`<sstream>`/`<iostream>` were
excluded: 22, 15 and 0 users is too few to justify making all 180 TUs load them.

**Result: 89.6 s -> 80.0 s on the row's own gate, a 10.7% improvement.**
`touch foundation/core/entity_id.hpp && cmake --build --preset debug -j20`, 180 TUs
both times. BEFORE 89.6 / 90.4 / 88.9 s; AFTER 80.4 / 80.0 / 79.6 s. Both spreads are
under 1% and the ranges do not overlap (worst AFTER 80.4 s beats best BEFORE 88.9 s),
so three runs each is enough and no statistics are needed.

**The measurement trap, recorded because it would have inflated the result.** The
baseline in the backlog was 83.2 s over 171 TUs, measured 2026-10-24 earlier. Comparing
the new 80.0 s against *that* gives 3.8%, and it would be wrong: the suite has gained
tests since, so the rebuild is now 180 TUs, not 171. Those two numbers describe
different amounts of work. The BEFORE was therefore **re-measured on the same tree, in
the same build directory, minutes before the AFTER** -- 89.6 s, not 83.2 s. Do not
quote 83.2 -> 80.0. The same reason is why the AFTER was not measured in the delegate's
worktree, which had a cold build directory: that would have compared a warm incremental
build against a cold one and reported a fictitious result.

**Two decisions, both recorded with their reasons.**

*Stdlib-only, no project headers.* This was settled before delegating, because the
tempting move is wrong in a way that is easy to miss: putting `entity_id.hpp` in the PCH
would be invalidated by exactly the edit the gate measures, forcing a PCH rebuild *and
then* all 180 TUs -- strictly worse than no PCH at all. A comment at the list says so,
because someone will try to add a project header there later.

*`PRIVATE`, not `PUBLIC`.* `PUBLIC` propagates the PCH to every consumer and silently
changes what their TUs see. The PCH is a build optimisation for these four targets, not
part of their interface.

**Unity builds were declined, not forgotten.** 10.7% was bought with a 6-line,
fully reversible CMake change. Unity builds would pay for the next increment in ODR and
internal-linkage semantics, and in error messages that point at files the author did not
touch. The trigger for revisiting is in the backlog.

**The risk, and the ruling on the guard.** A PCH's real hazard is include hygiene
drifting: a new file that omits `<vector>` compiles anyway, and breaks only for whoever
builds without the PCH. That was verified *not* to be true at ship time, as a negative
control rather than an assumption -- `cmake -DCMAKE_DISABLE_PRECOMPILE_HEADERS=ON` built
the whole repo with 0 errors in 97.6 s, which also independently confirms the gain (97.6 s
no-PCH vs 84.5 s full build with it). The delegate was explicitly forbidden from removing
any existing `#include`, for the same reason.

**The ruling on the standing guard was made twice, and CI overturned the first one.**
Initially it was declined on measured cost: a second full build against a job that takes
~1m42s in total, to protect a latent failure that only bites someone who disables the PCH.
That reasoning was sound and the conclusion was still wrong, because it assumed the only
way to get the guard was to *add* a build.

**What CI actually showed, and why the PCH is now off in CI.** The first CI run with the
PCH passed, but took **6m49s against the usual ~1m38s**. The cause was in the ccache stats,
not the build log:

| Run | Cacheable calls | Uncacheable | Job time |
|---|---|---|---|
| #294, pre-PCH | 452 / 452 (100%) | 0 | 1m38s |
| #295, PCH on | 213 / 456 (46.7%) | **243 / 456 (53.3%)** | 6m49s |

ccache cannot cache a compilation that consumes a precompiled header without sloppiness
settings, so 243 calls became uncacheable *by nature*. This was **not** a cold-cache
one-off, and the numbers rule that out rather than argue it: the cache restored fine
(315 MB on the restore key) and all 213 still-cacheable calls hit at 100%. So the trade as
first shipped was 10.7% off a local rebuild in exchange for ~5 extra CI minutes on every PR,
permanently -- a bad trade, and the opposite of the standing priority that CI time is a lever.

**The fix makes both problems one problem.** CI now configures with
`-DCMAKE_DISABLE_PRECOMPILE_HEADERS=ON`. The PCH's entire justification is the local
developer loop -- its own backlog row said "impact is on the developer loop, not CI" -- and
CI does no incremental header-edit rebuilds, so disabling it there costs nothing that was
ever wanted and restores 452/452 cacheable. **And the include-hygiene guard that was
declined on cost is now free**, because the no-PCH build it asked for is the build CI
already performs on every PR. The consequence to accept knowingly: a missing `#include` can
now compile locally and fail in CI. That is the right direction for the failure to point.

**The lesson, stated plainly because it nearly shipped:** the local gate (an edit-one-header
rebuild) was the gate the row asked for, it was measured correctly, and it was still not
sufficient to justify the change. A build-system change has a second cost centre that the
local measurement cannot see, and the probe that found it was not the gate -- it was
comparing one CI run's ccache statistics against the previous PR's.

**Delegation note.** Implemented by agy (Gemini 4 Argon, High) in an isolated worktree
from a task file that front-loaded both decisions above. The diff came back exactly as
specified -- one shared list, `PRIVATE`, stdlib-only, the comment present, no `#include`
removed anywhere -- and its claim of 1462/1462 held up. The measurements were taken by
the supervisor, not the delegate, because the gate is the deliverable here and a cold
build directory would have silently answered a different question.

## A headless VkDevice for tests, slice 1: surfaceless device creation

**2026-10-24.** Raised by the user the same day ("we are limited from taking screenshots
because of this missing VkDevice, we should fix this soon"). This is slice 1 of 3, and it
is the foundation only: the project can now create a `VkDevice` inside a test, which it
could not do at any price before. It renders nothing.

**The reframe came first, and it is what made the work tractable.** Two blockers had been
conflated, and only one was ours. The sandbox genuinely cannot screenshot a GUI window
(`hyprctl`/`grim` are broken on this machine) -- environmental, unfixable. Nothing in the
test binary constructed a `VkDevice` -- entirely our own coupling. Fixing the second makes
the first *irrelevant* rather than fixing it, because an offscreen render plus a buffer
readback produces pixels through the driver with no compositor and no window in the
picture. **The goal was never a screenshot; it is a readback.** Framing it as "screenshot
the editor" is exactly what had made it look environmental and therefore hopeless.

**Feasibility was probed before anything was designed or delegated**, with a ~50-line
standalone program compiled and run on this machine. It reported: validation layer
present; instance created with NO surface extensions; 1 physical device; picked NVIDIA
GeForce RTX 3080, graphics family 0, `maxPushConstantsSize=256`; `vkCreateDevice` with no
swapchain extension SUCCESS; graphics queue obtained; **validation messages = 0**. So the
environment was never the obstacle, and the delegate was handed a working reference rather
than an open question. (The 256-byte push-constant limit also happens to satisfy the
production renderer's requirement exactly, which matters for slice 3.)

**What the blocker actually was, named to the function.** `VulkanRenderDevice`'s
constructor takes a `GLFWwindow*` and calls `glfwCreateWindowSurface` immediately;
`pick_physical_device` is surface-gated three ways (`device_supports_swapchain`,
`vkGetPhysicalDeviceSurfaceSupportKHR`, and surface-format/present-mode counts); and
`create_logical_device` always requests `VK_KHR_swapchain`. A headless path does not need
new rendering code -- it needs to stop *requiring* the swapchain.

**The probe discipline finding, which is the most useful thing in this entry.** The
delegate's test asserted `validation_message_count() == 0` and all 12 assertions passed.
Run again with the layer disabled (`VK_LOADER_LAYERS_DISABLE='*'`), **it still passed all
12**: with no layer there is no messenger, the callback never fires, and the count is
trivially zero. The case was reporting "validation clean" on a run where validation had
never executed -- a probe that could not fail. Fixed by asserting
`dev.validation_enabled()` *before* the count, in both scopes, with the measurement written
beside it so nobody deletes it as redundant.

**Both directions were then proved by mutation, and the first mutation was discarded for
being the wrong kind of red.** Removing `vkDestroyDevice` from `cleanup()` did turn the
test red -- with a SIGSEGV. That proves the test notices a crash, not that the validation
counter is load-bearing, so it was discarded and replaced with a mutation that produces a
real validation message without crashing: a deliberate zero-size `vkCreateBuffer` after
device creation. That failed on exactly the intended line, `validation_message_count() == 0`
expanding to `1 == 0`. So the counter fires on a genuine validation error, and the
`validation_enabled()` assertion fires when validation is absent. The case is now
load-bearing in both directions.

**The registration decision, which was forced rather than chosen.** The GPU case lives in a
new `mosaic_gpu_tests` target, built only under `-DMOSAIC_ENABLE_GPU_TESTS=ON`, default OFF.
The obvious alternative -- a Catch2 `SKIP` when no GPU is present -- would set `skipped=1`,
and `tools/ci/check_ctest_run.py`'s O2 check fails the build on any skip. That guard is
right and must not be loosened to accommodate this, because its whole purpose is that a
skip cannot masquerade as a pass. The default suite is therefore still **exactly 1462
tests, all passing** (verified in the same warm build directory, not taken from the
delegate's report), so `next-steps.md`'s State table and the #290 checker stay true.

**What this does NOT establish, stated plainly.** It is opt-in, so it protects nothing
automatically -- it is a manual verification tool until there is a GPU runner. And it
exercises a *parallel* surfaceless implementation, not the production constructor, because
`vulkan_render_device.cpp` was deliberately not touched: a slice whose gate cannot see
pixels has no business editing the renderer. Sharing the selection code between the two
paths is a follow-up for after slice 2 can observe a result.

**Delegation note.** Implemented by agy (Gemini 4 Argon, High) in an isolated worktree from
a task file carrying the probe output and the settled decisions. The diff came back as
specified and its claims of 1462 and of a clean run held up. What it missed was the
vacuous-assertion problem, which no amount of green could have surfaced -- it took running
the case in a configuration the delegate had no reason to try.

## A headless VkDevice for tests, slice 2: offscreen readback and the first asserted pixel

**2026-10-24.** Slice 2 of 3. **This is the one that makes the gate able to see a pixel.**
Before it, nothing in 1462 tests had ever checked one.

**What it does.** `modules/render_vulkan/vulkan_headless_readback.{hpp,cpp}` takes an
existing `VulkanHeadlessDevice`, creates a 16x16 `VK_FORMAT_R8G8B8A8_UNORM` offscreen
image, clears it to RGBA (0x12,0x34,0x56,0x78), transitions
`UNDEFINED -> TRANSFER_DST_OPTIMAL -> TRANSFER_SRC_OPTIMAL`, copies the image into a
host-visible buffer, waits on a fence, maps it, and returns the pixels.
`tests/gpu/offscreen_readback_test.cpp` asserts **all 256** of them, twice in one process.
2587 assertions across the two GPU cases.

**A clear, not a drawn triangle, and that was a decision rather than a shortcut.** A clear
exercises the entire readback path -- image creation, memory type selection, layout
barriers, command submission, fence wait, image-to-buffer copy, host mapping and
non-coherent invalidation -- with none of the graphics-pipeline or SPIR-V surface. Had the
first asserted pixel been gated behind shader tooling it would not have landed in this
slice, and the readback machinery is the part everything later depends on. Drawing geometry
is slice 3.

**Design choices aimed specifically at making the assertion hard to satisfy by accident.**
The colour is (0x12,0x34,0x56,0x78) rather than black or white, so zeroed or uninitialised
memory cannot pass. All 256 pixels are checked rather than one, because a single sampled
pixel can match for the wrong reason while a whole buffer cannot. 16x16 is large enough
that a row-pitch error shows up. The test asserts the literal channel values
(`pixels[i].r == 0x12`) as well as the struct equality, so the expectation cannot drift with
the helper's input.

**Proved load-bearing by mutation, and the mutation was the decisive one.** The risk with a
readback helper is that it echoes its own input instead of reading the GPU, in which case
the test asserts nothing. Perturbing the clear's red channel by +1 in the implementation
turned the case red on exactly the right line -- `REQUIRE(pixels[i].r == 0x12)` expanding to
`19 == 18`, with the INFO message reporting `r=19 g=52 b=86 a=120`. So the assertion reads
real GPU output, pixel-exact, and the readback is genuine. Reading the implementation had
already ruled out an echo, but reading is not the same as proving.

**Slice 1's vacuity lesson carried forward and re-verified.** Both GPU cases assert
`validation_enabled()` before any message count, with the reason in a comment. Confirmed by
running the suite under `VK_LOADER_LAYERS_DISABLE='*'`: **both cases fail**, which is the
correct behaviour and the thing that was broken when slice 1 arrived from the delegate.

**Default suite untouched: still exactly 1462 tests, all passing**, verified in the same
warm build directory rather than taken from the delegate's report. The GPU cases remain in
the opt-in `mosaic_gpu_tests` target for the reason recorded in slice 1's entry: a Catch2
`SKIP` would trip `check_ctest_run.py`'s O2, and that guard is correct.

**What is still NOT established, and it is the thing the user actually asked for.** Slice 2
proves the project *can* assert a pixel. It does not yet assert anything about the
renderer's own output, because it clears an image rather than running
`VulkanRenderDevice`'s draw paths -- which still construct through `glfwCreateWindowSurface`
and remain untouched by design. The eye-only classes named in `next-steps.md`'s landmines
are therefore still eye-only: the `screen_effect.vert` Y-flip (which ships upside-down with
nothing able to see it), the per-frame buffer aliasing class behind #275 and #287, and the
HUD underlay. **Slice 3 is where that changes, and it needs a graphics pipeline and real
shaders** -- which is exactly what slice 2 avoided, so it is a genuine slice and not an
extension of this one.

**Delegation note.** agy (Gemini 4 Argon, High), isolated worktree, task file carrying the
settled decisions and slice 1's vacuity measurement verbatim so the same defect could not
recur. It did not: the delegate asserted `validation_enabled()` in the right place without
being corrected. Its report also claimed a synchronisation-validation run
(`VK_LAYER_VALIDATE_SYNC=1`) passing, which was not asked for and is a genuinely useful
extra given that sync validation is normally off and the project has shipped barrier bugs
behind that blind spot.

## Environment quirks and retired landmines (moved out of the handoff 2026-10-24)

Moved here to keep `next-steps.md` under its 8 KB ceiling. **These are still true.** They were
moved because they are narrow-audience -- each bites only while doing one specific kind of work --
not because they stopped mattering, and `next-steps.md` carries a pointer to this section.

**Note on the archive tool, because the obvious move does not work.**
`tools/docs/archive_next_steps.py` could NOT do this, and reported "no dated blocks found; nothing
to do", which is correct. It moves dated `**Last updated:**` blocks, and the handoff no longer has
any: it was restructured into a pointer document, and CLAUDE.md now forbids dated narrative there.
The handoff's growth is in the LANDMINE LIST, and trimming that is an editorial judgement about
which warnings a cold reader still needs -- not a mechanical roll. Do not reach for that script the
next time the handoff is too big.

**What was actually over-long, for the record:** the two landmines added earlier the same day. The
`VkDevice`/GPU-test one had reached 1111 bytes and the PCH one 636, together 23% of the whole file.
Both were compressed to their actionable warning with a link to the reasoning here, which is the
split the three-document policy already asks for. The lesson is that a landmine should state the
hazard and the action, not argue its case.

### Sandbox environment quirks

- **The sandbox cannot screenshot the GUI.** Verify a windowed run functionally (exit 124 under `timeout`, silent stderr); do not chase `hyprctl`/`grim`.
- **Cross-process loopback sockets here are unpredictably slow** (seconds to 20+ s for a first packet). A sandbox property, not an engine bug.
- **`/tmp` here is a 32 GB tmpfs, and filling it makes two `mcp_tools` tests fail in a way that reads exactly like a regression.** `build_project packages a project into a standalone bundle` and `build_project honors a custom out_dir argument` both write into `std::filesystem::temp_directory_path()`. When `/tmp` hit 80% on 2026-10-24, `create_project` still succeeded and `build_project` returned `ok:false`, so the failure surfaced as `REQUIRE( result.at("ok").get<bool>() == true )` with `false == true` and nothing about disk anywhere in the output. Reproducible, not flaky, and it passed on `main` in the real checkout at the same moment — which is what made it look like a change-induced regression. Cause: four opt-in GPU build dirs (~8.2 GB) left in `/tmp` by delegate task files that named `/tmp/gpubuildN` instead of the session scratchpad. Check `df -h /tmp` before believing a `build_project` failure, and build out-of-tree work in the scratchpad, not `/tmp`.
- **`blender --background` cannot import the stdlib here** (`math`, `subprocess` fail even with a clean env). A broken Blender install, not a project or script bug; do not re-diagnose it as one.

### Retired landmines -- NOT landmines; this note exists to stop them being re-added

Two threaded-test flakes recorded in older notes are **not** landmines: the `PhysicsSystem::raycast` cross-thread case was fixed in #230, and the threaded-`ScriptSystem` SIGSEGV cannot occur under `ctest` (one process per case). If either goes red, it is a real finding.
## A headless VkDevice for tests, slice 3 step 1: separating surface construction

**2026-10-24.** Slice 3 needs the readback pointed at the renderer's REAL draw paths,
which means `VulkanRenderDevice` has to be constructible without a window. This is step 1
of 2, and it is deliberately a **pure refactor that adds no capability**: it separates the
surface/swapchain-coupled construction so step 2 can add an offscreen variant. Splitting it
this way means that if something breaks, it is known which half broke it.

**The shortcut was probed and ruled out first.** `VK_EXT_headless_surface` would have let
the existing renderer work unchanged -- surface, swapchain, present and all -- with no
refactor whatsoever. It is not offered on this machine: the loader reports 25 instance
extensions under `DRIVER_ID_NVIDIA_PROPRIETARY` 610.57.04 and that is not among them. So the
refactor is necessary rather than merely the first idea. The probe also turned up that Mesa
**lavapipe** does implement it, which matters for a different reason and is captured in the
backlog: it would let these tests run on GPU-less CI runners, so slice 1's recorded trigger
of "a CI runner with a GPU" was too narrow.

**Scope, measured rather than assumed.** The constructor runs ~35 steps and only four areas
are surface-coupled: `glfwCreateWindowSurface`; `pick_physical_device`'s present-support
query, `device_supports_swapchain` and surface-format/present-mode checks;
`create_logical_device`'s `VK_KHR_swapchain` request; and the
`create_swapchain`/`create_image_views`/`create_framebuffers`/`extent_` group. Every render
pass, pipeline, descriptor and shadow/HDR/bloom resource needs only a colour format and an
extent. Nothing else was touched. `create_vulkan_render_device(GLFWwindow*)` keeps its exact
signature and wraps the argument, so no caller changed.

**THE GATE, and the finding that made it possible.** A standing assumption needed
correcting. `next-steps.md` said renderer behaviour is "checkable only by eye", and that is
true of PIXELS -- but it had been read as though nothing about the renderer were checkable.
The editor runs fine in this sandbox, and it runs under the validation layer with
**synchronisation validation enabled**. On unmodified `main` that produces exactly:

```
exit=124
[INFO] vulkan: synchronization validation ENABLED (MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION); expect it to be slower
[INFO] demo: baking environment IBL (a few seconds, once)
[INFO] demo: environment IBL ready
```

Three lines, zero VUIDs, zero ERROR, zero WARN, exit 124 (no natural exit, so the timeout is
the pass). That baseline was taken BEFORE the refactor and handed to the delegate as a
must-match. After the refactor the log is **byte-identical** -- verified with `diff`, in the
real checkout, not the worktree. For a change to 2700 lines of renderer code whose output no
test can see, that is the strongest evidence available, and it is considerably better than
"the suite is green". **This gate existed all along and was not being used.**

**The defect found by review, which green would never have shown.** The delegate's report
flagged, honestly, that it had kept the literal text
`glfwSetWindowUserPointer(window_, this);` intact so that a source-text contract test in
`bloom_backend_contract_test.cpp` would keep matching. The way it did so was to introduce a
**local variable named `window_`** -- trailing underscore, which in this codebase means a
member -- purely to satisfy a grep. Two things were wrong with that:

1. A local named like a member is actively misleading, especially now that the member of
   that name no longer exists.
2. More seriously, the test was using that line as its "end of the constructor" marker, and
   the line had just moved inside an `if (WindowTarget)` block. On a surfaceless path it
   will not exist at all, so in step 2 the marker would silently stop meaning what the test
   thinks it means -- while still passing.

Fixed on both counts: the local is gone (the call uses `wt->window` directly), and the test
is re-anchored on `VulkanRenderDevice(const VulkanRenderDevice&) = delete;`, which is unique
in the file, is genuinely the first thing after the constructor body, and does not depend on
which target was built. The reason is written into the test so the anchor is not "tidied"
back. **Re-anchoring preserved the test's teeth, and that was proved rather than assumed:**
removing `write_bloom_descriptors()` from the constructor still fails exactly 1 of the 44
`[bloom]` cases, with its intended message.

**The honest cost of splitting into two steps.** With only one variant in the
`std::variant`, every `holds_alternative<WindowTarget>` branch is currently always true, so
the not-taken branches are unexercised. That is the price of making step 1 provably
behaviour-preserving, and step 2 is what exercises them.

**Delegation note.** agy (Gemini 4 Argon, High). It did the refactor correctly, ran the
editor gate as instructed, and reported the contract-test contortion rather than hiding it
-- which is what made the defect findable. The report was accurate; the judgement that
shaping production code to satisfy a brittle grep was acceptable is what needed overruling.

## A headless VkDevice for tests, slice 3 step 2a: the real renderer, offscreen

Step 1 (#299) separated surface construction behind `RenderTarget`. Step 2a adds
the `OffscreenTarget` alternative, so the real ~6000-line `VulkanRenderDevice` —
the same class the editor uses, with its real pipelines and render passes — can
be built with no window, no surface and no swapchain, render, and be read back.

**Step 1 was more inert than "adds no capability" conveyed.** Reading the merged
code before specifying step 2 turned up that the offscreen path was not merely
unexercised, it was *not constructible*, for four independent reasons. Three I
predicted and the delegate confirmed; the fourth it found:

1. `create_swapchain()` was skipped, so `swapchain_format_` stayed
   `VK_FORMAT_UNDEFINED`, and `create_render_pass()` / `create_offscreen_render_pass()`
   use it as their colour format.
2. `create_render_pass()` set `finalLayout = PRESENT_SRC_KHR`, which is provided
   by `VK_KHR_swapchain` — not an enabled device extension offscreen.
3. `framebuffers_` was left empty while `ensure_swapchain_pass()` indexes
   `framebuffers_[image_index_]`.
4. **(found by the delegate)** `extent_` stayed `{0, 0}`, so `create_depth_resources()`
   and `create_hdr_target(extent_.width, extent_.height)` would create 0x0 images.

It also corrected one of my three: the hdr and bloom passes do *not* use
`swapchain_format_`, they hardcode `R16G16B16A16_SFLOAT`. Only the swapchain and
viewport passes were affected.

**The decision that made the slice worth doing:** the offscreen path *creates
swapchain-equivalent resources* rather than skipping. Same member names
(`images_`, `views_`, `framebuffers_`, `extent_`, `swapchain_format_`), so every
downstream pipeline, render pass and `draw_*` method runs unchanged. The
alternative — offscreen special-cases threaded through the draw paths — would
have produced a test exercising a *second* renderer, worthless as a gate on the
first. No `draw_*` method was touched. The only target-dependent change outside
construction, `begin_frame` and `end_frame` is `create_render_pass()`'s
finalLayout.

**Format: `B8G8R8A8_SRGB`, measured not guessed.** `choose_surface_format()`
prefers exactly that and also falls back to it, so it is what the window path
actually gets. A UNORM offscreen image would differ from the editor by a gamma
curve and the test would assert the wrong numbers confidently.

**Two images, not one,** matching `kFramesInFlight`: `render_finished_` is sized
from `images_.size()`, and ImGui's Vulkan backend requires MinImageCount >= 2.

### The defect in the test, and why "run it twice" was not enough

Slices 1 and 2 each ran their subject twice in one process, after a one-instance
fixture hid a shared-vertex-buffer alias from 1456 tests. So step 2a's spec
demanded two frames, reasoning that with `kFramesInFlight == 2` a second frame
uses the *other* image.

That spec was insufficient, and the delegate implemented it exactly as written:
**both frames cleared to the same colour.** A `read_pixels()` returning the stale
image, or an `end_frame()` rendering both frames into `images_[0]`, produces
byte-identical output and passes.

This was not argued, it was measured. With a one-line mutation making
`read_pixels()` always copy `images_[0]`, the same-colour test passed **all 35362
assertions**. With distinct colours per frame, the same mutation fails on the
intended line: `frame=1 pixel=0 r=137` where 225 was expected. The structure was
right and the data made it decorative.

The clear colours are now distinct per frame, and the expected bytes are computed
in the test from the float input through the sRGB transfer function and asserted
against a named constant (+/-1 per channel for rounding), so no value is
back-filled from observed output.

A second probe proved the validation assertion load-bearing too: adding
`COLOR_ATTACHMENT_WRITE` to a buffer barrier's `dstAccessMask` under
`PIPELINE_STAGE_HOST` fails `REQUIRE(validation_messages.empty())` with all 4096
pixel assertions still passing — a validation-only red, not a crash.

### Two defects fixed in review

- **`in_flight_` indexed by image, not by slot.** `read_pixels()` waited on
  `in_flight_[last_rendered_index_]`. `in_flight_` is `VkFence[kFramesInFlight]`,
  frame-slot indexed, and every other use indexes it with `frame_`. It was
  correct only because offscreen sets `image_index_ = frame_`, making the two
  numerically equal — a coincidence, not a reason, and an out-of-bounds read the
  moment the offscreen image count stops matching `kFramesInFlight`. Now tracks
  `last_rendered_frame_` explicitly.
- **A dead `if/else`** around `create_framebuffers()` with identical branches.

### Gates

Default suite 1462/1462 (3.63 s). GPU build: 3 `[gpu]` tests, 35362 assertions,
zero validation messages under `MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1`. The editor gate:
exit 124 and `diff` against the merged-`main` baseline **empty**, so windowed
behaviour is byte-identical. `create_vulkan_render_device(GLFWwindow*)` unchanged.

### What is still not covered

Step 2a asserts a *cleared* frame. No `draw_*` call is asserted, so the Y-flip in
`screen_effect.vert`, the per-frame buffer aliasing and the HUD underlay remain
eye-only. That is step 2b, deliberately split so each half has a gate that can
fail for one reason.

## A headless VkDevice for tests, slice 3 step 2b: the first asserted draw path

Step 2a made the real `VulkanRenderDevice` renderable with no window, but asserted
only a *cleared* frame — it could not see a single shader bug. Step 2b asserts
**where a real draw path puts its pixels**, which makes the negative-height
(Y-flip) viewport compensation machine-checkable for the first time in the project.

`tests/gpu/offscreen_ui_orientation_test.cpp` draws HUD quads through the real
`draw_ui` path on a 128x128 offscreen device and asserts the corners.

### A promise retracted before it was shipped

Three times across this slice the stated payoff was "the `screen_effect.vert`
Y-flip becomes machine-catchable". **That was wrong, and checking the types before
writing step 2b is what caught it.** `ScreenEffectParams` is exactly
`{ Vec3 tint; float tint_strength; float vignette; }` — a tint uniform across the
whole frame, and a vignette radially symmetric about the centre. Neither can
distinguish a vertically flipped `fragUV` from a correct one. **That shader's
missing compensation is not pixel-observable with today's parameters**, so no
readback test can catch it, and one claiming to would have been theatre.

The bug is real and still latent. The trigger for catching it is the first
*asymmetric* screen effect (a gradient, a scanline, a directional blur, an
off-centre vignette); at that point this test file is the template and the gate is
a few lines. Recorded rather than quietly dropped.

What IS catchable, and is now guarded, is `ui.vert`:
`gl_Position = vec4(nx * 2.0 - 1.0, 1.0 - ny * 2.0, 0.0, 1.0)`. That `1.0 - ny * 2.0`
is the compensation, it is the thing `screen_effect.vert` omits, and until now
nothing in 1462 tests touched it.

### The mutation, which is the whole evidence

A passing orientation test proves nothing by itself, so `ui.vert` was mutated:
`1.0 - ny * 2.0` -> `ny * 2.0 - 1.0`, removing the compensation. Run independently
twice (delegate, then re-run in review from a separate build dir), identical result:

```
frame 0 corners: top-left(8,8)=(99, 137, 188, 255) bottom-left(8,119)=(225, 188, 99, 255)
                 top-right(119,8)=(99, 137, 188, 255) bottom-right(119,119)=(99, 137, 188, 255)
```

`(99, 137, 188)` is the clear colour and `(225, 188, 99)` is the quad: the quad
moved from the top-left corner to the **bottom-left**, and the test failed on its
intended assertion. Restored, recompiled to SPIR-V, green again, `git status` clean.

### Design points

- **Asymmetric placement is the test.** The quad covers the top-left quadrant only
  (`rect = {0, 0, 0.25, 0.25}`). A centred or fullscreen quad cannot detect a
  Y-flip; it is the same reason a symmetric vignette cannot.
- **Both directions asserted:** the quad is where it should be AND the other three
  corners are still the clear colour. "It drew something somewhere" is not the claim.
- **Interior sample points** (8,8) / (8,119) / (119,8) / (119,119), not edges, so no
  off-by-one rasterisation argument is possible.
- Expected bytes computed through the sRGB transfer function from the float input,
  +/-1 per channel. One sample came back 241 against a computed 240 — within
  tolerance, and worth noting the tolerance is load-bearing, not decoration.
- Part two makes **two separate `draw_ui` calls** in one frame, two corners, both
  asserted. `draw_ui` uses push constants per quad with no per-frame staging buffer,
  so this is a composition check and is described as one — it is **not** an
  aliasing check, and the per-frame buffer aliasing class (`draw_particles`,
  `draw_ribbons`, `draw_world_text`) is still unasserted.

### Gates

Default suite 1462/1462. 4 `[gpu]` tests, 35402 assertions, zero validation
messages under `MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1`. Editor gate exit 124, `diff` against
the merged-`main` baseline empty. No shader and no `.cpp` in `render_vulkan` changed.

### A red run that was not a regression

The first full-suite run in this worktree failed two tests,
`mcp_tools: build_project packages a project into a standalone bundle` and
`... honors a custom out_dir argument`, with `false == true`. Classified rather
than re-run: the cause was `/tmp` (a tmpfs) at 80% from 8.2 GB of leftover GPU
build directories that earlier task files in this slice had told delegates to
create under `/tmp` instead of the session scratchpad. Freeing them took `/tmp` to
54% and both tests passed, full suite 1462/1462. Captured as an environment quirk
above, because the symptom names nothing about disk.

## Two gates, defined precisely (moved out of the handoff 2026-10-24)

The handoff points here rather than carrying these in full. Both are load-bearing
and neither is discoverable by reading the code.

### The renderer regression gate

```
timeout 12 env MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION=1 ./build/debug/app/mosaic editor
```

**PASS is exit 124.** The editor has no natural exit, so the timeout *is* the
pass; exit 0 means it died early and is a FAILURE. A passing run prints **exactly
three `[INFO]` lines** and nothing else:

```
[INFO] vulkan: synchronization validation ENABLED (MOSAIC_VK_SYNC_VALIDATION); expect it to be slower
[INFO] demo: baking environment IBL (a few seconds, once)
[INFO] demo: environment IBL ready
```

Zero VUIDs, zero `[ERROR]`, zero `[WARN]`, with *synchronisation* validation on
(it is off unless that variable is set). The way to use it is to capture that log
on unmodified `main` **before** a renderer change and `diff` against it after —
a byte-identical diff is far stronger evidence than "the suite is green" for a
change the suite cannot see. It catches a broken object graph, a bad barrier and a
missing synchronisation scope. It does **not** see pixels.

It was found unused: the handoff had said renderer behaviour was "checkable only
by eye", which is true of pixels and had been read as though nothing about the
renderer were checkable. It verified the #299 target-abstraction refactor as
byte-identical, and has gated every renderer change since.

### Why the GPU tests are opt-in, and the rule that must not be relaxed

The `[gpu]` tests need a real `VkDevice`, so they are a separate target behind
`-DMOSAIC_ENABLE_GPU_TESTS=ON` (default OFF) and are **not** in CI, which has no
GPU:

```
cmake -S . -B <dir> -G Ninja -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Debug -DMOSAIC_ENABLE_GPU_TESTS=ON
ctest --test-dir <dir> -R gpu
```

The obvious alternative — register them in the default suite and have them
`SKIP` when no device is present — **is not available**, because
`tools/ci/check_ctest_run.py`'s O2 fails on *any* skip. That rule is deliberate
and valuable: it is what stops a test from quietly becoming a no-op in CI. **Do
not loosen O2 to let the GPU tests skip.** A skipping GPU test in CI would be
indistinguishable from a GPU test that had silently stopped asserting anything,
which is the exact failure mode O2 exists to prevent.

The standing fix is an environment that can run them, not a weaker gate: see the
Mesa lavapipe entry in the backlog (a software ICD implementing
`VK_EXT_headless_surface`, which this driver does not offer).

## Handoff trim and branch cleanup (2026-10-24)

**`next-steps.md`: 7691 -> 6857 bytes**, 94% -> 84% of the 8 KB ceiling (the
request was "under 7 KB"). **No landmine was dropped.** All 20 were kept; four
were compressed and their detail moved to the record:

- the stdlib PCH entry (the 243/456-uncacheable measurement already lived in the
  fan-out entry, so the handoff now carries only the flag, the prohibition and a link)
- the renderer regression gate and the no-`VkDevice` entry, both of which now
  point at "Two gates, defined precisely" above
- the Y-flip entry, whose latency reasoning lives in the backlog

**The conservation check is what made this safe, and it found two things.** Before
cutting, every fact being removed was grepped for in the record and the backlog.
Two existed *only* in the handoff and would have been destroyed:

1. the gate's exact pass condition — **exactly three `[INFO]` lines**, not merely
   "a short log"
2. **"do not loosen `check_ctest_run.py`'s O2"** to let the GPU tests skip

Both were written to the record *first*, then cut from the handoff. A trim done by
reading for redundancy rather than by grepping for conservation would have silently
lost the O2 rule, which is the thing stopping a test from becoming a no-op in CI.

An anchor audit alongside it found a pre-existing broken-looking link
(`#reduce-header-fan-out--precompiled-headers--unity-builds`, from #298). It is
**correct**: GitHub replaces each space with its own hyphen rather than collapsing
runs, so a `/` surrounded by spaces yields a double hyphen. The first checker
written for this collapsed whitespace and produced a false positive — worth
knowing before "fixing" that link.

**Branches: 36 local -> 1, 28 remote -> 1.** 34 local deleted with `git branch -d`
(which refuses anything unmerged, so it was the safety rail rather than a check to
be skipped). Two needed `-D` and were inspected first rather than force-deleted on
the strength of being old:

- `ci/build-caching-experiment` (28 commits, 2026-10-20) — failure-injection
  scaffolding: blocking the cache service via `/etc/hosts`, corrupting restored
  cache files, forcing a failure before Save. Its spec and plan are on `main` and
  ccache itself shipped, so nothing was exclusive to it. SHA `b29f43e`.
- `docs/handoff-refresh-wip` (1 commit, 2026-09-06) — a WIP touching
  `docs/updates/`, which is closed. SHA `2c9d93f`.

Of the 28 remote branches, 27 were fully contained in `main`. The 28th,
`pointer-input-rebased`, carried one real feature commit with tests and had **no
PR ever opened** — it was checked rather than assumed: the feature shipped
2026-10-07 (`main` has `ViewportPointerSample`/`set_pointer_sample`, 87 pointer
references in `viewport_test.cpp`, and the backlog marks it done), so the branch
held a duplicate under a different SHA. All 28 SHAs were recorded before deletion.

## Assert the per-frame buffer-aliasing class through the offscreen target

**Shipped 2026-10-24 (PR #303), for `draw_particles`.** The bug class — "a draw path
called once per entity reusing one per-frame buffer aliases itself" — had bitten twice
for real (#275 world text, #287 `draw_particles`/`draw_ribbons`), been fixed both times,
and been guarded by nothing. Reverting either fix was silent. It is not silent now.

### The backlog's stated dependency was wrong, and checking it first is what saved the slice

The live bullet said this work "depends on building meshes/textures and staging uploads
on the offscreen device, which step 2b did not need and did not do." **`draw_particles`
needs neither a mesh nor a texture**: it uploads instance data, sets push constants, and
issues `vkCmdDraw(cmd, 6, instances.size(), 0, 0)`.

The real prerequisite was never written down anywhere: every world-space draw opens with
`if (!in_hdr_pass_) { return; }`, so the test must drive
`begin_hdr_scene_pass` → draws → `end_hdr_scene_pass` → `draw_hdr_resolve` → `end_frame`.
`create_hdr_target` was already on the common constructor path (`:324`), so the offscreen
device had one all along. **Had the stated dependency been briefed as fact, the slice
would have been spent building an upload path that nothing needed.** The general lesson is
the one the backlog's own header states and that is easy to skip: an entry records what
was true when someone last looked.

### The oracle was chosen before measuring, and deliberately not colour-exact

Two `draw_particles` calls in one frame at different screen positions; assert a particle
at **both** P_A (32,32) and P_B (96,96), and that a control point (32,96) stays
background. Positional rather than colour-exact **on purpose**: the HDR resolve tonemaps,
so an assertion predicting exact RGB would be brittle for reasons that have nothing to do
